The prime decomposition fibre sequence for moduli spaces of reducible 3-manifolds
Rachael Boyd, Corey Bregman, Jan Steinebrunner

Abstract
. We study the moduli space B ​ Diff + ​ ( M ) B\!\Diff^{+}(M) , for M M a reducible, oriented 3-manifold with irreducible prime factors P 1 , … , P n P_{1},\ldots,P_{n} . A programme of César de Sá–Rourke, Hendriks–Laudenbach, and Hendriks–McCullough studies the homotopy type of Diff + ⁡ ( M ) \Diff^{+}(M) in terms of the Diff + ⁡ ( P i ) \Diff^{+}(P_{i}) . Inspired by a delooping proposed by Hatcher, we construct a map from B ​ Diff + ​ ( M ) B\!\Diff^{+}(M) to B ​ Diff + ⁡ ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P n ) B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}) , called the splitting map , that yields a prime decomposition fibre sequence . The fibre ℋ g ​ ( P 1 , … , P n ) \mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}) is a space of 1 1 -handle attachments which we describe geometrically as a homotopy colimit of certain configuration spaces on the P i P_{i} . Firstly, this allows us to show that for n > 0 n>0 the fibre is equivalent to a finite, connected cell complex. Secondly, this makes the fibre sequence an effective tool for computations, which we illustrate by computing the rational cohomology ring of B ​ Diff + ⁡ ( ( S 1 × S 2 ) ♯ ​ 2 ) B\!\Diff^{+}\!\left((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2}\right) .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification

57T20, 58D29 (primary),
57M50, 55R40, 57S05, 58D05 (secondary)

1.
Introduction

Throughout this paper,
M
is a compact, connected, oriented 3-manifold with prime decomposition

M=P_{1}\sharp\cdots\sharp P_{n}\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}
,
where the
P_{i}
are irreducible and not diffeomorphic to
S^{3}
.
We will assume throughout that
M
has no spherical boundary components,
i.e.
that no
P_{i}
is a disc, see
2.9
.
Recall that
\Diff^{+}(M)
is the topological group of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of
M
, equipped with the
C^{\infty}
topology.
We will focus on the moduli space
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
, which classifies smooth oriented
M
-bundles.

A programme announced by César de Sá and Rourke
[
9
]
, then completed by Hendriks and Laudenbach
[
29
]
, and Hendriks and McCullough
[
30
]
, aimed to study diffeomorphisms of
M
in terms of its irreducible prime factors
P_{i}
.
More precisely, they construct a homotopy fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Diff^{+}_{\partial}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Diff^{+}_{\partial}(P_{i}\setminus\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}})}

and moreover show that it splits as a product when
g=0

[
29
, Theorem 2]
.
Here
C(M)
is a space of decompositions of
M
into its prime factors and
\Diff_{\partial}^{+}(M)\leq\Diff^{+}(M)
denotes the subgroup of diffeomorphisms that fix the boundary point-wise.
The fibration in this sequence is only defined up to homotopy and is not a group homomorphism. In particular this fibre sequence only describes
\Diff^{+}_{\partial}(M)
as a space and not as a group.

Hatcher proposed a “delooping” of this fibre sequence, which describes the classifying space
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
and thus also captures group theoretic information about
\Diff^{+}(M)
.
This idea appeared in an unfinished draft available on the author’s website
[
24
]
.
In this paper we use tools developed in our previous work
[
6
]
to realise this vision by constructing the
prime decomposition fibre sequence
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\!\Diff^{+}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}}

The
splitting map

\mathbb{W}
admits a geometric description in terms of cutting
M
along a collection of spheres and capping them with discs.
Its fibre is a moduli space of
1
-handle attachments to
P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}
.
This is analogous to the space
C(M)
in the work of
[
9
,
29
,
30
]
, but an advantage of our construction is that we provide an effective way of computing
\mathcal{H}_{g}(\dots)
by expressing it as a finite homotopy colimit of spaces that admit explicit geometric descriptions in terms of framed configuration spaces of the
P_{i}
.
We are able to leverage this construction in two ways:

(1)

For
n>0
, we show that
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n})
is equivalent to a finite, connected cell complex.

(2)

For
n=0
, we construct a spectral sequence converging to the rational cohomology ring of
B\!\Diff^{+}((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g})
and we completely compute this cohomology ring when
g=2
.

This is the first such cohomology computation for a
3
-manifold that goes significantly beyond the case of irreducible
3
-manifolds.

Separating systems and the splitting map

The finiteness of the fibre in (
1
) complements our previous work
[
6
]
, where we showed that when
M
has non-empty boundary
B\!\Diff_{\partial}(M)
has the homotopy type of a finite CW-complex, proving a conjecture of Kontsevich for oriented
3
-manifolds with boundary.

A key tool introduced in
[
6
]
was a space
\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
of
separating systems
for
M
.
Elements of
\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
are collections of spheres
\Sigma\subset M

such that each component of
M\setminus\Sigma
is a punctured irreducible prime factor of
M
or a punctured 3-sphere.
This space forms a topological poset under inclusion and we showed that the geometric realisation of the nerve of this poset is contractible when
M\ncong S^{1}\times S^{2}
.
This gave us a new model for
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
:

B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\simeq|N_{\bullet}(\sep^{\nparallel}(M))|/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(M).

The starting point for this paper is
Section

3
where we rewrite this model to express
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
as a homotopy colimit of terms of the form
B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)
,
where
\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)
is the group of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms that preserve
\Sigma
set-wise.
This homotopy colimit is indexed by a category
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
of
dual graphs

G_{\Sigma}
to separating systems
\Sigma
, where the vertices of
G_{\Sigma}
correspond to components of
M\setminus\Sigma
and the edges to components of
\Sigma
.

.

For every
M\ncong S^{1}\times S^{2}
there is a functor
\mathcal{S}\colon\mathrm{Gr}(M)\to\mathrm{Top}
such that

B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\simeq\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{S}(\Gamma).

If
\Gamma=G_{\Sigma}
is the dual graph of a sphere system
\Sigma\subset M
, then
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
is homotopy equivalent to the classifying space of the group of those orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms
\varphi\in\Diff^{+}(M)
such that for all spheres
S\subseteq\Sigma
we have
\varphi(S)=S
and
\varphi_{|S}
is orientation preserving.

Giansiracusa has given a similar description for the moduli spaces of
3
-dimensional handlebodies, writing
B\!\Diff^{+}((S^{1}\times D^{2})^{\natural g})
(for
g\geq 2
) as a homotopy colimit over a finite category of graphs
[
23
]
.
(Note that the handlebody is irreducible and hence admits only the empty separating system, so our theorem is vacuous in this case.
Another point of difference is that the moduli spaces Giansiracusa considers are all aspherical.)
The perspective taken in
[
23
]
is that the moduli spaces of handlebodies form a modular operad, which is the “modular envelope” of the framed little
2
-disc operad.
An analogous interpretation of
3.24
in terms of modular (
\infty
-)operads will appear in upcoming work of Barkan and the third author, as sketched in
[
49
, Proposition 4.16]
.

Figure 1
.
The cut-and-cap map
\kappa_{\Sigma}
.

Given a fixed separating system
\Sigma\subset M
, we construct a cut-and-cap map

\kappa_{\Sigma}\colon B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n})

that is roughly given by cutting
M
along
\Sigma
, then capping in the punctures in
M\setminus\Sigma
with
3
-discs, and finally discarding all connected components that are
3
-spheres, see
Fig.

1
.
In
Section

4
, we assemble these maps into a map out of the homotopy colimit model for
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
.
This yields the
splitting map

\mathbb{W}\colon B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n})

such that for each separating system
\Sigma\subset M
the restriction of
\mathbb{W}
to
B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)
gives the cut-and-cap map
\kappa_{\Sigma}
described above.
The geometric description of
\kappa_{\Sigma}
has the following consequence.

.

The splitting map induces a surjection

\pi_{1}\mathbb{W}\colon\pi_{0}(\Diff^{+}(M))\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}))

from the mapping class group of
M
onto the mapping class group of
P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}
.

For every compact submanifold
A\subset\partial M
let
\Diff_{A}^{+}(M)\leq\Diff^{+}(M)
be the subgroup of those diffeomorphisms that fix
A
pointwise.
We also construct a variant of the splitting map

\mathbb{W}_{A}\colon B\!\Diff_{A}^{+}(M)\longrightarrow B\!\Diff_{A}^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}),

compatible with
\mathbb{W}
, such that the homotopy fibres of
\mathbb{W}_{A}
are equivalent to those of
\mathbb{W}
.

Describing the fibre

Conceptually, the fibre of
\mathbb{W}
is the space of possible
0
- and
1
-handle attachments one can make to
P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}
to build a manifold diffeomorphic to
M
.
In order to make the map
\mathbb{W}
an effective tool for computations, we need to make this description more concrete.

Let
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
denote the category whose objects are pairs
(\Gamma,\sigma)
of a finite, connected, graph
\Gamma
with first Betti number
g
and an
n
-tuple
(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n})
of distinct marked vertices such that every non-marked vertex is at least trivalent.
Morphisms in this category are generated by graph isomorphisms and edge-contractions, see
2.29
.

Such a tuple
(\Gamma,\sigma)
is the first half of a blueprint for building a manifold diffeomorphic to
M
from the disjoint union
P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}
.
(The irreducible component
P_{i}
will correspond to the marked vertex
\sigma_{i}
.)
We begin by adding a disjoint
S^{3}
for each unmarked vertex, and then we attach a
1
-handle for each edge in the graph.
The second half of the data, needed to perform these handle-attachments topologically, is a choice of disjoint embeddings of discs into
P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}
,
or equivalently a choice of framed configurations.
We define

\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Conf_{H_{\sigma_{i}}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{i})\times\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\Conf_{H_{v}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(S^{3}),

where
\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{A}(N)
denotes the space of positively framed configurations of a finite set
A
in
N
, and for
v\in V_{\Gamma}
,
H_{v}
is the set of half-edges at
v
, and
V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}=V_{\Gamma}\setminus\{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\}
is the set of unmarked vertices in
\Gamma
.
This configuration space comes with an action of the Lie group
\smash{\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)\times\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4)}
by re-choosing framings along each edge and rotating the
S^{3}
components.
The homotopy quotient of
\Conf_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
by this Lie group roughly parametrises the moduli space of all possible 0- and 1-handle attachments in the pattern determined by
(\Gamma,\sigma)
.

To relate this heuristic picture to the homotopy colimit in
3.24
, we introduce a space
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
of separating systems augmented by an embedding
\iota\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)\hookrightarrow P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{n}
, where
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is a manifold called the
maximal prime piece
, see
4.2
. Abstractly,
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is diffeomorphic to
P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{n}
minus a union of open discs, and crucially, it is constructed so that the pair
(P(\Sigma\subset M),\iota)
vary continuously with
\Sigma
and functorially with respect to inclusions of sphere systems.
We then rewrite the fibre
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
as a homotopy colimit over
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
of the
\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
and establish the
prime decomposition fibre sequence
:

.

For every compact, connected, oriented 3-manifold
M
that is not diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times S^{2}
, there is a homotopy fibre sequence

\hocolim_{(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}}\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{\mathbb{W}}B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n})

where for each
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
there is a homotopy equivalence

\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\simeq\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\sslash\bigg(\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)\times\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4)\bigg).

The fibre sequence is particularly simple when
g=n=1
, in which case the homotopy colimit reduces to the homotopy colimit over the group

\Aut(\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to9.22pt{\vbox to7.8pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-3.89899pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.97 0 C 1.97 1.09 1.09 1.97 0 1.97 C -1.09 1.97 -1.97 1.09 -1.97 0 C -1.97 -1.09 -1.09 -1.97 0 -1.97 C 1.09 -1.97 1.97 -1.09 1.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.12 0 M 10.24 0 C 10.24 2.83 7.95 5.12 5.12 5.12 C 2.29 5.12 0 2.83 0 0 C 0 -2.83 2.29 -5.12 5.12 -5.12 C 7.95 -5.12 10.24 -2.83 10.24 0 Z M 5.12 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}})=\mathbb{Z}/2
.
This yields a description of the moduli space of an irreducible
3
-manifold
P
with one
1
-handle attached to it.

Example 1.1
.

(See
5.10
for more details.)
For every irreducible
3
-manifold
P
that is not
S^{3}
or
D^{3}
, there is a homotopy fibre sequence

\frac{\Conf_{2}(P)\times\SO(3)}{\mathbb{Z}/2}\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2}))\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P)

where
\mathbb{Z}/2
acts by swapping the configuration points and inverting the element of
\SO(3)
.
We can for example use this to determine the classifying space of the identity component
\Diff_{0}(P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2}))
of the diffeomorphism group.
Suppose, for simplicity, that
P
is aspherical and not diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times D^{2}
.
Then
B\!\Diff_{0}(P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2}))
is homotopy equivalent to a product of
S^{3}
and a wedge sum of infinitely many copies of
S^{2}
, indexed by elements of
\pi_{1}(P).

Therefore

\pi_{1}\Diff(P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2}))=\pi_{2}B\!\Diff(P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2}))=\oplus_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{Z}

has infinite rank, but we show that, as a module over the integral group ring of the mapping class group
\pi_{0}\Diff(P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2}))
, it is generated by one element.

Kalliongis and McCullough
[
32
]
showed that
\pi_{1}\Diff(M)
has infinite rank when
g+n\geq 3
or when
g=n=1
and
\pi_{1}(P)
is infinite.
The above example precisely determines this infinite rank group in the
g=n=1
case and shows that it is still finitely generated as a module over the integral group ring of the mapping class group.

In
[
26
,
28
]
Hatcher describes
\Diff(M)
for
M=S^{1}\times S^{2}
and
P_{1}\sharp P_{2}
(as before the
P_{i}
are irreducible) by using that in these cases any two essential
2
-spheres are isotopic.
The above example deals with the natural next case: in
P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})
it is not true that any two essential spheres are isotopic, but any two
non-separating
spheres are isotopic.

If the prime decomposition

M=P_{1}\sharp\cdots\sharp P_{n}\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}

satisfies
n>0
and
g+n\geq 2
,
then the Lie group action in
5.2
is free.
We use this to show that
\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
is homotopy equivalent to a compact manifold with corners of dimension at most
15(g-1)+12n
. Using this upper bound in the homotopy colimit yields an upper bound for the dimension of the fibre.

.

For
n>0
and
n+g>1
, the fibre
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})

of the splitting map
\mathbb{W}
is homotopy equivalent to a finite cell complex of dimension at most
17(g-1)+13n
.

Combining
5.6
with work of Gabai
[
21
]
yields the following example.

Example 1.2
.

(See
5.7
.)
Let
n>0
and
g\geq 0
such that
g+n\geq 2
.
For
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
hyperbolic
3
-manifolds,
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)

admits a finite cover that is equivalent to a finite cell complex of dimension at most
17(g-1)+13n
.
However,
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
itself need not be finite-dimensional.

Rational cohomology computations

When studying the moduli space
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
, an invariant of particular interest is its rational cohomology ring.
Each smooth oriented
M
-bundle
\pi\colon E\to B
is classified by some map
f_{\pi}\colon B\to B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
, and hence each cohomology class
\alpha\in H^{k}(B\!\Diff^{+}(M);\mathbb{Q})
, defines a
characteristic class

\alpha(\pi):=f_{\pi}^{*}\alpha
of smooth
M
-bundles.

In the case of surfaces
M=\Sigma_{g}
the Madsen–Weiss theorem
[
38
]
computes this cohomology ring in the “stable range”, and shows that it is a polynomial algebra on Miller–Morita–Mumford classes, as conjectured by Mumford.
In even dimensions
d=2n\geq 6
, work of Galatius–Randal-Williams
[
22
]
generalises the Madsen–Weiss theorem and describes the rational cohomology ring of
B\!\Diff^{+}((S^{n}\times S^{n})^{\sharp g})
in a stable range. Similar results have been obtained in high odd dimensions
d=2n+1\geq 7

[
3
,
20
]
.

While these results describe each cohomology ring up to a certain degree (depending on the genus), they do not describe the complete cohomology ring for any given genus.
For
g\leq 4
, work of Looijenga and Tommasi

[
36
,
51
]

uses algebro-geometric tools to compute the cohomology ring of the moduli stack
\mathcal{M}_{g}
of smooth curves of genus
g
, which is equivalent to
B\!\Diff^{+}(\Sigma_{g})
.

For
3
-manifolds, one can make concrete computations of the rational cohomology ring of
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
when
M
is irreducible.
For example, if
M
is hyperbolic, then
\Diff^{+}(M)
is equivalent to the (finite) group of isometries and hence the rational cohomology of the moduli space is trivial.
In the case of the connected sum of two lens spaces Lelkes in
[
35
]
used the aforementioned theorem of Hatcher
[
26
]
to compute the rational cohomology ring of
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
.

For more general
3
-manifolds, and in particular the family of connected sums
U_{g}=(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}
, which are analogous to the manifolds considered in higher-dimensional variants of the Madsen–Weiss theorem,
little is known about the rational cohomology of
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
.
The prime decomposition fibre sequence from
5.2
presents an opportunity to make such computations in dimension
3
tractable.
Concretely, there is a Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence (see
6.18
) converging to the cohomology of the homotopy colimit:

E_{2}^{p,q}=\operatornamewithlimits{lim^{\it p}}_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm op}}H^{q}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n});\mathbb{Q})\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})).

Here the
E_{2}
-page consists of the derived limits of the cohomology groups of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(\dots)
as functors on
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm op}
.
In
Section

6
we use the description of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
from
5.2
to compute its rational cohomology ring in terms of the rational cohomology of configuration spaces of the
P_{i}
, see
6.11
.
This description is
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
and
\prod_{i}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
-equivariant (the latter action factors through
\prod_{i}\pi_{0}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
), but we do not fully describe its functoriality in
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm op}
.

In the case of
U_{g}
, where the base of the prime decomposition fibre sequence is trivial, we give a complete description of
H^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma};\mathbb{Q})
as a functor of
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,0}
.
This allows us to completely determine the rational cohomology ring of the moduli space for
g=2
.

.

The rational cohomology ring of
B\!\Diff^{+}\!\left((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2}\right)
has presentation

H^{*}\left(B\!\Diff^{+}\!\left((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2}\right);\mathbb{Q}\right)\cong\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\varepsilon]/\langle\varepsilon^{2},\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}\varepsilon\rangle

where
|\gamma_{1}|=|\gamma_{2}|=4
and
|\varepsilon|=8
.

To illustrate the geometric meaning of these cohomology classes, in
Section

7.4
we construct smooth circle actions
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\colon\SO(2)\to\Diff^{+}((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2})
that distinguish
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
in the sense that
(B\xi_{i})^{*}\gamma_{j}=e^{2}
for
i\neq j
and
(B\xi_{i})^{*}\gamma_{i}=0
, where
e
is the Euler class in
H^{*}(B\!\SO(2);\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{Q}[e]
.
This in particular yields
7
-manifolds
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
fibred over
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
with fibre
U_{2}
such that the characteristic classes
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
evaluate as

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(E_{2}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2}(E_{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2})=e^{2}

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(E_{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2}(E_{2}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2})=0.

For higher
g
, one can still use the spectral sequence to try to compute the cohomology ring of
H^{*}(B\!\Diff(U_{g});\mathbb{Q})
, but already determining the
E_{2}
-page is a non-trivial task:
the
0
th row
E_{2}^{*,0}
is the rational group cohomology of
\Out(F_{g})
.

Outline

In
Section

2
, we record some preliminary and background results about group actions, 3-manifolds, separating systems, homotopy colimits, and categories of combinatorial graphs.
In
Section

3
, we consider dual graphs to separating systems and prove
3.24
, establishing a homotopy colimit formula for
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
.
In
Section

4
, we define the “maximal prime piece” of a separating system and use it to construct the splitting map
\mathbb{W}
.
Here we also give a geometric description of
\mathbb{W}
and introduce variants that fix part of the boundary.

Section

5
describes the fibre in more geometric terms:
we first state
5.2
, consider several applications, and use it to deduce finiteness of the fibre for
n>0
.
Then, we introduce thickened sphere systems in order to prove
5.2
and set up a spectral sequence that describes the cohomology of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
.
In
Section

6
, we compute the rational cohomology of
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})
and
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})\sslash\SO(4)
, which we then use to calculate the cohomology of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
via the aforementioned spectral sequence, and also determine the effect of graph morphisms on several of the resulting classes.
Finally, in
Section

7
, we apply these tools to compute the rational cohomology of
B\!\Diff^{+}((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2})
and evaluate the resulting characteristic classes on two bundles obtained from circle actions.

2.
Background on separating systems, homotopy colimits, and graphs

The purpose of this section is to collect some necessary background results and notation to be used throughout the paper. We will assume some familiarity with foundational results due to Cerf
[
8
]
and Palais
[
41
]
concerning embedding spaces of submanifolds. For more details, we refer the reader to Section 2 of
[
6
]
and Section 2 of
[
7
]
. After reviewing some results about homotopy fibre sequences and group actions in
2.1
, we recall the notion of a separating system in
2.2
, which we introduced in
[
6
]
. Our main technical result from
[
6
]
established that the nerve of the poset
\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
of separating systems has contractible geometric realisation, which is recorded here as
2.14
. In
Section

2.3
, we recall Smale and Hatcher’s theorems concerning the homotopy type of
\Diff^{+}(D^{3})
and deduce some consequences for general 3-manifolds.
Section

2.4
reviews the homotopy colimit construction and proves a sufficient condition for a homotopy colimit to be finite. Lastly, in
Section

2.5
we introduce the graph categories that we will work with and prove some of their basic properties.

2.1.
Fibre sequences and
G
-locally retractile spaces

We begin by recalling some of the tools we use for working with topological group actions and for constructing fibrations.

Definition 2.1
.

A space
X
equipped with an action of a topological group
G
is called
G
-locally retractile
if for every
x\in X
there exists a neighbourhood
U
of
x
and a map
\xi\colon U\rightarrow G
such that
\xi(u).x=u
for all
u\in U
. We call
\xi
a
G
-local retraction at
x
.

The crucial feature of
G
-locally retractile spaces is that every
G
-equivariant map into them is automatically a fibre bundle.
(This elementary, but extremely useful observation is due to Cerf, see e.g.
[
7
, Lemma 2.5]
for a proof.)
As a special case of this we have the following.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
X
be a locally path connected
G
-locally retractile space and let
p\colon Y\to X
be a
G
-equivariant map with discrete fibres. Then
p
is a covering map and
Y
is
G
-locally retractile.

Proof.

By
[
7
, Lemma 2.5]
,
p
is a locally trivial fibre bundle, hence a covering map since the fibres of
p
are discrete. Fix
y\in Y
and let
x=p(y)
. We can find path-connected open sets
U\ni y
,
V\ni x
such that
p|_{U}\colon U\cong V
and there exists a
G
-local retraction
\xi\colon V\rightarrow G
satisfying
\xi(x)=1
. Define
\tau=\xi\circ p|_{U}\colon U\rightarrow G
and let
f=(p|_{U})^{-1}
. We claim that
\tau
is a
G
-local retraction at
y.

Given
u\in U
, choose a path
\gamma_{t}\colon[0,1]\to V
such that
\gamma_{0}=x
and
\gamma_{1}=p(u)
. Using that
\xi(x)=1
, we obtain two paths in
U
starting at
y
defined by
\gamma_{t}^{\prime}=\xi(\gamma_{t}).y
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{t}=f(\xi(\gamma_{t}).x)
. Since
p
is
G
-equivariant, both
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
cover
\gamma
, hence must be equal by uniqueness of path-lifting. It follows that

u=f(\gamma_{1})=\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\xi(\gamma_{1}).y=\xi(p(u)).y=\tau(u).y,

hence
\tau
is a
G
-local retraction at
y\in Y
, as required.
∎

Definition 2.3
.

A
G
-action on a space
X

principal
if the quotient map
X\to X/G
is a principal
G
bundle.
We call a topological subgroup
H\leq G

admissible
if
H
acts principally on
G
,
i.e.
if
G\to G/H
is a fibre bundle.

Observation 2.4
.

We have the following general constructions for principal actions and admissible subgroups.

(1)

G
acts on
X
in a locally retractile way, then the inclusion of the stabilizer
\Stab_{x}(G)\leq G
is an admissible subgroup inclusion for all
x\in X
.

(2)

If
G
acts principally on
X
, then it also acts principally on
X\times Y
for any
G
-space
Y
.

(3)

By
[
6
, Lemma 2.9]
, if
H\leq G
is admissible and
G
acts principally on
X
, then so does
H
.

Example 2.5
.

We give a few examples of principal actions and admissible subgroups.
Let
M
be a compact manifold and
N\subset M
a compact submanifold.
See
[
6
, §2.2]
for references.

(1)

The action of
\Diff(N)
on
\emb(N,\smash{\mathring{M}})
is principal.
Its quotient is the space
\Sub(N,\smash{\mathring{M}})
of submanifolds of
\smash{\mathring{M}}
that are abstractly diffeomorphic to
N
. The action of
\Diff(M)
on
\Sub(N,\smash{\mathring{M}})
is locally retractile.

(2)

The subgroup
\Diff(M,N)\leq\Diff(M)
, which is the stabiliser of the action of
\Diff(M)
on
\Sub(N,\smash{\mathring{M}})
, is admissible.

(3)

The action of
\Diff(N)
on
\emb(N,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
is principal.

Throughout the paper we will use the homotopy orbit construction
X\sslash G
, which we define as
(X\times EG)/G
for
EG
a weakly contractible principal
G
-space.
We refer the reader to
[
6
, §2.3]
for a recollection of the basic properties of this construction, but we especially highlight the following fact, which can be found in
[
2
, Corollary 2.14]
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Consider group actions
G_{i}\curvearrowright X_{i}
and equivariant maps between them inducing maps

X_{1}\sslash G_{1}\longrightarrow X_{2}\sslash G_{2}\longrightarrow X_{3}\sslash G_{3}.

This is a homotopy fibre sequence, if the
X_{i}
and the
G_{i}
each form a homotopy fibre sequence and
G_{2}\to G_{3}
is surjective on path components.

2.2.
Review of 3-manifolds and separating systems

We will always assume that our manifolds are smooth, compact, and oriented. Recall that a 3-manifold
N
is called
irreducible
every embedded
S^{2}\subset N
bounds
D^{3}
, and
reducible
otherwise. Irreducible 3-manifolds are
prime
in the sense that any decomposition
N=N_{1}\sharp N_{2}
implies that either
N_{1}
or
N_{2}
is homeomorphic to
S^{3}
. The only prime but not irreducible orientable 3-manifold is
S^{1}\times S^{2}
. It is a classical result
[
33
,
40
]
that orientable 3-manifolds admit connected sum decompositions into essentially unique irreducible factors and copies of
S^{1}\times S^{2}
:

Theorem 2.7
.

Any compact, oriented 3-manifold
N
admits a minimal connected sum decomposition

N=P_{1}\sharp\cdots\sharp P_{n}\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}\sharp(D^{3})^{\sharp m}

satisfying:

(1)

The
P_{i}
are oriented, irreducible and not diffeomorphic to
D^{3}
nor
S^{3}
.

(2)

The factors are uniquely determined by
N
, up to reordering and orientation-preserving diffeomorphism.

Remark 2.8
.

The number
g
of
S^{1}\times S^{2}
factors is the maximal number of disjointly embedded 2-spheres in
N
whose union does not separate. The number
m
of
D^{3}
factors is the same as the number of
S^{2}
-components of
\partial N
. In particular, when
N
has no spherical boundary components then
m=0
. We regard the case
N=S^{3}
as having no factors:
n=g=m=0
.

If
\Sigma\subset\smash{\mathring{N}}
is an embedded, codimension 1 submanifold, then
\smash{\mathring{N}}\setminus\Sigma
is homeomorphic to the interior of a compact manifold with boundary which we denote
N\!\mid\!\Sigma
. When
\Sigma
is a disjoint union of 2-sided submanifolds,
e.g.
when
\Sigma=\sqcup_{k}S^{2},
then the boundary of
N\!\mid\!\Sigma
contains two copies of
\Sigma
, one for the left and right pushoff, which we denote by
2\Sigma\subset\partial(N\!\mid\!\Sigma).
There is a canonical map
N\!\mid\!\Sigma\rightarrow N
which is a homeomorphism away from
\Sigma
and a double cover along
\Sigma
. If
N
has
S^{2}
boundary components, the
spherical closure of
N
, denoted
\widehat{N}
, is the manifold obtained by capping all
S^{2}
boundary components with copies of
D^{3}
.

Remark 2.9
.

All of our main theorems concern
3
-manifolds (smooth, compact, connected, oriented)
without spherical boundary
.
If
N
is a
3
-manifold with
m
spherical boundary components, then we can write
N=\widehat{N}\setminus\amalg_{m}\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}}
.
As in
[
6
, Lemma 6.3]
there is a homotopy fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\frac{\Conf_{m}(\widehat{N})}{\Sym_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(N)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(\widehat{N})}.}

The fibre, which is the unordered configuration space of
m
points in
\widehat{N}
, is fairly well-understood – for example, it always admits a compactification as a compact
3m
-manifold with corners
[
46
]
, hence is equivalent to a finite CW complex.
Therefore, this fibre sequence allows us to reduce most qualitative or computational questions about
B\!\Diff(N)
to the case without spherical boundary.

In
[
6
]
, we introduced the notion of a separating system, which we now recall.

Definition 2.10
.

An (unparametrised) embedded 2-sphere
S\subset N
is
reducing
if it does not bound a ball in the spherical closure
\widehat{N}
.
(Equivalently, if each component of
\smash{\widehat{N\!\mid\!S}}
has fewer prime factors than
\widehat{N}
.)
A submanifold
\Sigma\cong\sqcup_{k}S^{2}\subset N
is called a
separating system
if every component of
\Sigma
is reducing, and if
\smash{\widehat{N\!\mid\!\Sigma}}
is irreducible.

The set of separating systems forms a topological poset
\sep(N)
with respect to inclusion, topologised as a subspace

\sep(N)\subset\coprod_{k=0}^{\infty}\umb(\sqcup_{k}S^{2},N).

With this topology, a connected component of
\sep(N)
corresponds to an isotopy class of separating system in
N
. Since
\sep(N)
is a disjoint union of
\Diff(N)
-locally retractile spaces, namely
\amalg_{k=0}^{\infty}\umb(\sqcup_{k}S^{2},N)
, it is itself
\Diff(N)
-locally retractile.
In particular, for any fixed
\Sigma\in\sep(N)
, the orbit map
\Diff(N)\rightarrow\sep(N)
defined by
\varphi\mapsto\varphi(\Sigma)
is a principal
\Diff(M,\Sigma)
-bundle.

Definition 2.11
.

Two disjointly embedded 2-spheres
S_{0},S_{1}\subset N
are called
parallel
if they cobound a submanifold of
N
diffeomorphic to
S^{2}\times[0,1]
. Let
\sep^{\nparallel}(N)\subset\sep(N)
denote the subposet of all separating systems
\Sigma
where no two components of
\Sigma
are parallel.

It follows from results of Laudenbach
[
34
]
and Perelman’s solution to the Poincaré conjecture
[
42
,
44
,
43
]
that two disjoint 2-spheres are parallel if and only if they are (unparametrised) isotopic (see
[
6
, Lemma 3.14]
). Thus, an element of
\sep^{\nparallel}(N)
may equivalently be defined as a separating system whose components lie in pairwise distinct isotopy classes.

Proposition 2.12
.

The poset
\sep^{\nparallel}(N)
enjoys the following properties:

(1)

As a union of
\Diff(N)
-orbits of
\sep(N)
, it is
\Diff(N)
-locally retractile.

(2)

By
[
6
, Lemma 6.5]
, it has finite height.

(3)

By
[
6
, Lemma 6.6]
, it is a union of finitely many
\Diff(N)
orbits.

Remark 2.13
.

Since finite index subgroups are admissible
2.4
(3) implies that Properties (1) and (3) of
2.12
also hold after passing to finite-index subgroups, such as
\Diff^{+}(N)
.

Let
\sep^{\nparallel}_{k}(N)=\{\Sigma_{0}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Sigma_{k}\mid\Sigma_{i}\in\sep^{\nparallel}(N)\}
, be the space of
k
-chains of
\sep^{\nparallel}(N)
.
This is a simplicial space, namely exactly the nerve of the topological poset
\sep(M)
.
We let
\|\sep^{\nparallel}_{\bullet}(N)\|
denote its fat geometric realisation.
(The fat and thin realisation of
\sep^{\nparallel}_{\bullet}(N)
are homotopy equivalent, see e.g. the proof of
[
6
, Theorem 6.2]
.)
In
[
6
]
, we prove the following.

Theorem 2.14

(Theorem 3.20,
[
6
]
)
.

If
N\not\cong S^{1}\times S^{2}
, then
\|\sep^{\nparallel}_{\bullet}(N)\|
is contractible.

Since the
\Diff(N)
-action on
\sep^{\nparallel}(N)
preserves inclusions, it acts component-wise on every level
\smash{\sep^{\nparallel}_{k}(N)}
, and hence on the fat geometric realisation
\smash{\|\sep^{\nparallel}_{\bullet}(N)\|}
. By taking homotopy orbits,
2.14
implies the following following fundamental equivalence:

(1)

B\!\Diff(N)\simeq\|\sep^{\nparallel}_{\bullet}(N)\|/\!\!/\Diff(N).

2.3.
Extending diffeomorphisms over 3-discs

At the heart of our construction of
\mathbb{W}
in
4.16
are the following two foundational results:

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
D^{3}
be a closed 3-ball with boundary
\partial D^{3}=S^{2}
.

(1)

(Smale,
[
47
]
)
The inclusion
\SO(3)\hookrightarrow\Diff^{+}(S^{2})
is a homotopy equivalence.

(2)

(Hatcher,
[
27
]
)
The inclusion
\SO(3)\hookrightarrow\Diff^{+}(D^{3})
is a homotopy equivalence.

The first part implies that every family of diffeomorphisms of
S^{2}
extends over
D^{3}
, while the second part (which is equivalent to the Smale conjecture) says that this extension is unique up to homotopy, even when varying in families. Equivalently, the second part states that
\Diff_{\partial}(D^{3})
is contractible.

Definition 2.16
.

Given compact 3-manifolds
N,Q
, let
\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q)
denote the subspace of those embeddings
i\colon N\hookrightarrow Q
for which there exists a diffeomorphism
\widehat{N}\cong Q
restricting to
i
on
N
.

The notation “
\widehat{=}
” is meant to indicate “diffeomorphism after taking spherical closures.” We apply the above results of Smale and Hatcher to obtain canonical fillings of
2
-sphere boundary components.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
N
and
Q
be compact
3
-manifolds, and assume that
\widehat{N}
is abstractly diffeomorphic to
Q
.
Then for each embedding
\iota\colon N\hookrightarrow Q
such that the complement of
\iota(N)
is a disjoint union of discs, the map

\Diff^{+}(Q)\longrightarrow\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q),\qquad\varphi\mapsto(\varphi\circ\iota).

is a homotopy equivalence.
Moreover, the homotopy orbit space

\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(Q)

is contractible.

Proof.

By Smale’s theorem, every diffeomorphism of
\partial D^{3}
extends over
D^{3}
, so in particular
\iota
extends to a diffeomorphism
\widehat{\iota}\colon\widehat{N}\cong Q
and
\iota\in\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q)
. Given
f\in\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q)
, there exists a diffeomorphism
\widehat{f}\colon\widehat{N}\cong Q
restricting to
f
on
N
and therefore
\varphi=\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{\iota~}^{-1}
is a diffeomorphism of
Q
such that
\varphi\circ\iota=f
. Therefore, the orbit map
\Diff^{+}(Q)\rightarrow\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q)
is onto and by
2.5
(1), there is a fibre sequence

(2)

\Diff_{N}(Q)\to\Diff^{+}(Q)\to\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q).

On the other hand,
\Diff_{N}(Q)\simeq\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Diff_{\partial}(D^{3})
which is contractible by
[
27
]
.
Thus
\Diff(Q)\rightarrow\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q)
is a homotopy equivalence.
Lastly, the fibre sequence in
Eq.

2
is equivariant with respect to the exact sequence of groups
\{1\}\rightarrow\Diff^{+}(Q)\rightarrow\Diff^{+}(Q)
, hence
2.6
tells us that
\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(Q)\simeq B\!\Diff^{+}_{N}(Q)
, which is contractible.
∎

Remark 2.18
.

Although we will not need it, one can show that if
\widehat{N}\cong Q
, then the complement of any embedding of
N
into
Q
is diffeomorphic to a union of discs.

2.4.
Background on homotopy colimits

Our main theorem
4.16
describes the fibre of the splitting map as a homotopy colimit over a certain finite category of graphs.
In this subsection we recall some of the relevant definitions and constructions, and establish a criterion for showing when such homotopy colimits are homotopy finite.

Definition 2.19

(Homotopy colimit indexed by
\mathcal{C}
)
.

For a reference, see
[
17
, Section 4]
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category. Given a diagram of topological spaces

X\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Top}

we construct a simplicial space
E_{\bullet}
by

E_{n}:=\coprod_{C_{0}\to\dots\to C_{n}}X(C_{0})

where the coproduct runs over all sequences of
n
composable morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
and we denote the morphisms by
f_{i}:C_{i-1}\to C_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. The first face map is given by

\coprod_{C_{0}\to\dots\to C_{n}}X(C_{0})\,\,\overset{X(f_{1})}{\longrightarrow}\,\,\coprod_{C_{1}\to\dots\to C_{n}}X(C_{1})

and the remaining face maps are given by mapping the copy of
X(C_{0})
in
E_{n}
indexed by
C_{0}\to\dots\to C_{n}
to the copy of
X(C_{0})
in
E_{n-1}
indexed by the chain
C_{0}\to\cdots\hat{C}_{j}\cdots\to C_{n}
with
C_{j}
removed. Degeneracy maps are given by adding an identity morphism
C_{j}\to C_{j}
to the indexing chain.
The homotopy colimit of
X
is now defined as the geometric realisation

\hocolim_{C\in\mathcal{C}}X(C):=|E_{\bullet}|.

Recall that a category
\mathcal{I}
is an EI-category if every endomorphism in
\mathcal{I}
is invertible,
i.e.
it is an automorphism.
(Equivalently,
\mathcal{I}
admits a conservative functor to a poset, i.e. a functor
F\colon\mathcal{I}\to P
such that a morphism in
\mathcal{I}
is an isomorphism if and only if its image in
P
is an isomorphism.)
For such EI-categories we can compute homotopy colimits in two steps: first we take a homotopy quotient for finite group actions and then a homotopy colimit over finite posets.

Definition 2.20
.

Given an EI-category
\mathcal{I}
and
n\geq 0
we let
P_{n}(\mathcal{I})
denote the groupoid of conservative functors
[n]\to\mathcal{I}
:
its objects are
n
-tuples of composable morphisms
(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
(where none of the
f_{i}
are isomorphisms) and its morphisms are natural isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}

We let
\pi_{0}P(\mathcal{I}):=\coprod_{n\geq 0}\pi_{0}P_{n}(\mathcal{I})
denote the poset of isomorphism classes of such tuples for all
n
, ordered by refinement.

Theorem 2.21

(
[
50
]
)
.

The homotopy colimit of an EI-diagram
X\colon\mathcal{I}\to\mathrm{Top}
can be calculated in two steps as

\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}X(i)\simeq\hocolim_{[f_{1},\dots,f_{n}]\in\pi_{0}P(\mathcal{I})}\left(X(i_{0})\sslash\Aut(i_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{f_{n}}i_{n})\right)

Example 2.22
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}
is an EI-category with two objects
a,b\in\mathcal{I}
such that there is a morphism
f\colon a\to b
.
Suppose further that every other morphism
g\colon a\to b
is of the form
\beta\circ f\circ\alpha
for
\alpha\in\Aut(a)
and
\beta\in\Aut(b)
.
Then Słominska’s formula says that for every diagram
X\colon\mathcal{I}\to\mathrm{Top}
there is a homotopy pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(a)\sslash\Aut(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(a)\sslash\Aut(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(b)\sslash\Aut(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}X(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

where the top map is induced by the group homomorphism
\Aut(f)\to\Aut(a)
and the left map by the group homomorphism
\Aut(f)\to\Aut(b)
as well as the map
X(a)\to X(b)
.

Definition 2.23
.

We say that and EI-category
\mathcal{I}
is
finite
if it has finitely many objects and finitely many morphisms.
In this case, we define the depth
D\geq 0
of
\mathcal{I}
to be the length of the longest chain of composable non-isomorphisms in
\mathcal{I}
.

Note that the depth is bounded by the total number of morphisms in
\mathcal{I}
, and in particular it is always finite.
Indeed, suppose we had a chain of composable morphisms
(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
that is longer than the number of morphisms in
\mathcal{I}
.
Then we must have
f_{k}=f_{l}
for
k<l
, which implies that
f_{l}\circ\dots\circ f_{k+1}
and
f_{l-1}\circ\dots\circ f_{k}
both are endomorphism and thus invertible, which in turn implies that
f_{k}=f_{l}
is an isomorphism.

Remark 2.24
.

A finite EI-category
\mathcal{I}
is not necessarily finite as an
\infty
-category: for example, every finite group defines a finite EI-category with one object, but this is only finite as an
\infty
-category if the group is trivial.
In particular, it is not generally true that the homotopy colimit of a diagram
\mathcal{I}\to\mathrm{Top}
valued in finite CW complexes must again be equivalent to a finite cell complex.
Take for instance the diagram to be constant at the point, in which case the homotopy colimit is the classifying space of
\mathcal{I}
.

Nevertheless, we have the following finiteness result for homotopy colimits over certain EI-categories, if we require that the diagram is such that the homotopy colimit over each automorphism group is homotopy finite.
Recall that a morphism
f\colon i\to j
is an epimorphism if for all
g_{1},g_{2}\colon j\to k
we have that
g_{1}\circ f=g_{2}\circ f
implies
g_{1}=g_{2}
– this will be true for all morphisms in our graph categories, see
2.28
.

Proposition 2.25
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a finite EI-category such that every morphism in
\mathcal{I}
is an epimorphism.
Let
X\colon\mathcal{I}\to\mathrm{Top}
be a functor for which each
X(i)/\!\!/\Aut_{\mathcal{I}}(i)
is homotopy finite, then
\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}X(i)
is homotopy finite.

Moreover, if
D
is the depth of
\mathcal{I}
and
N
is such that each
X(i)/\!\!/\Aut_{\mathcal{I}}(i)
is equivalent to a finite CW-complex of dimension at most
N
, then
\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}X(i)
is homotopy equivalent to a finite CW-complex of dimension at most
N+D
.

Proof.

First consider the case where
\mathcal{I}
is a finite poset of depth
D
.
In this case, we construct a semi-simplicial space
E_{\bullet}^{\neq}
that is a subspace of the simplicial space
E_{\bullet}
described in
2.19
by only taking the disjoint union over those tuples of morphisms where none of them is the identity.
The simplicial space
E_{\bullet}
is then obtained by freely adjoining degeneracies to
E_{\bullet}^{\neq}
and as such we have a homeomorphism
|E_{\bullet}|\cong\|E_{\bullet}^{\neq}\|
.
On the other hand, the assumption that
\mathcal{I}
is a finite poset of depth
D
and that each
X(i)
is homotopy finite implies that
E_{n}^{\neq}
is equivalent to a finite CW-complex of dimension at most
N
for all
n
and is empty for
n>D
.
Thus
\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}X(i)\cong\|E_{\bullet}^{\neq}\|
is equivalent to a finite CW complex of dimension at most
N+D
.

For the general case we use Słominska’s formula as recalled in
2.21
.
We begin by arguing that each of the homotopy quotients
X(i_{0})\sslash\Aut(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is still homotopy finite.
There is a group homomorphism

\theta\colon\Aut(i_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{f_{n}}i_{n})\longrightarrow\Aut(i_{0})

defined by evaluating the automorphism on the first object.
This homomorphism is injective: if
\alpha=(\mathrm{id}_{i_{0}},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
is in the kernel, then because
f_{1}
is an epimorphism the equation
\alpha_{1}\circ f_{1}=f_{1}\circ\mathrm{id}_{i_{0}}
implies that
\alpha_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{i_{1}}
and inductively we see that
\alpha
is trivial.
Comparing homotopy orbits with respect to the source and target of
\theta
thus yields a finite covering map

X(i_{0})\sslash\Aut(i_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{f_{n}}i_{n})\longrightarrow X(i_{0})\sslash\Aut(i_{0})

and as we assumed that the base is homotopy finite it follows that the total space is as well, and in fact we can choose the finite model to also be
N
-dimensional.

For each
[f_{1},\dots,f_{n}]\in\pi_{0}P(\mathcal{I})
we have that
n\leq D
is bounded by the depth, and since we assumed that
\mathcal{I}
has only finitely many morphisms, it follows that
\pi_{0}P(\mathcal{I})
is finite.
In fact,
\pi_{0}P(\mathcal{I})
has the same depth as
\mathcal{I}
.
We have thus written
\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}F(i)
as the homotopy colimit over a finite poset (of depth
D
) of a diagram all of whose values are homotopy equivalent to a finite CW complex of dimension at most
N
– this is exactly the case we dealt with at the start of the proof.
∎

2.5.
Graph categories

We now introduce the combinatorial categories of graphs that index the homotopy colimits we will consider later.
Inspired by
[
45
]
, we define graphs in terms of vertices and half-edges.
In this definition, an edges are defined as the unions of two half edges that are paired via the half-edge involution
i
.
Although it is more convenient for us to define graphs in terms of half-edges, we will also rely on standard terminology from graph theory, such as paths, cycles, trees, etcetera.

Definition 2.26
.

A
graph

\Gamma
is a quadruple
(V,H,r,i)
where
V
is a finite set of vertices,
H
is a finite set of half-edges,
i\colon H\to H
is a fixed point free involution, called the
half-edge involution
, and
r\colon H\to V
is a map, called the
root map
.
We define the set of edges of
\Gamma
as

E_{\Gamma}=\{\{h,i(h)\}\;|\;h\in H\}.

A
labelled graph
is a graph
\Gamma
with a map
l\colon V_{\Gamma}\to L
for some set of labels
L
.
For a vertex
v\in V
, we let
H_{v}:=r^{-1}(v)
denote the set of half-edges incident at
v
. The number of elements of
H_{v}
is the
valence
of
v
.

Our notion of graph morphism is inspired by
[
10
, §2.2]
.

Definition 2.27
.

A morphism of graphs
f\colon\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
is a map

f\colon V_{1}\sqcup H_{1}\to V_{2}\sqcup H_{2}
such that

(1)

f
sends vertices to vertices:
f(V_{1})\subseteq V_{2}
,

(2)

for all
h\in H_{1}
, either
f(h)\in H_{2}
and
f(i(h))=i(f(h))
or
f(h)\in V_{2}
and
f(h)=f(r(h))
.

(3)

for each half-edge
h\in H_{2}
the preimage
f^{-1}(h)
is a single half-edge,

(4)

for every vertex
w\in V_{2}
the preimage
f^{-1}(w)
forms a tree.

Let
\mathrm{Gr}
denote the category of finite graphs.

A morphism of graphs
f\colon\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
is geometrically visualised by collapsing a collection of subtrees of
\Gamma_{1}
separately to points followed by an isomorphism to
\Gamma_{2}
.

Remark 2.28
.

A morphism of graphs
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
is always given by a surjective map
X_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{2}
and thus every morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}
is an epimorphism.

We also introduce a smaller category of graphs of fixed rank, equipped with a partial marking of the vertices.

Definition 2.29
.

An object of
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is a pair
(\Gamma,\sigma)
, where
\Gamma
is a connected rank
g
graph and
\sigma\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\hookrightarrow V_{\Gamma}
is an (injective) marking of a subset of the vertices
V_{\Gamma}
such that every non-marked vertex is at least trivalent. A morphism
f\colon(\Gamma_{1},\sigma_{1})\rightarrow(\Gamma_{2},\sigma_{2})
is a
\mathrm{Gr}
-morphism
f\colon\Gamma_{1}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}
such that
\sigma_{2}(i)=f(\sigma_{1}(i))
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Geometrically, for a morphism
f\colon(\Gamma_{1},\sigma_{1})\to(\Gamma_{2},\sigma_{2})
, the pre-image of
\sigma_{2}(i)
is a tree containing
\sigma_{1}(i)
as its only marked vertex. A graph
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is
maximal
if it is never the target of non-invertible morphism. Similarly, a graph
(\Delta,\tau)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is
minimal
if it is never the source of a non-invertible morphism. The following lemma enumerates some properties of
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
that we will need in the sequel.

Lemma 2.30

(Properties of
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
)
.

The category
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
enjoys the following properties:

(1)

The number of edges is
3(g-1)+2n
in any maximal graph and
(g-1)+n
in any minimal graph. The longest chain of composable non-invertible morphisms in
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
has length at most
2(g-1)+n
.

(2)

\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
has only finitely many isomorphism classes of objects.

(3)

\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is a connected category, that is, any two objects can be joined by a zig-zag of morphisms.

Proof.

If
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is maximal, then each labelled vertex must be a leaf and each non-leaf vertex must have valence exactly 3. Otherwise, we could increase the number of edges by at least 1. Since only labelled vertices are allowed to be leaves, the number of leaves is exactly
n
. The number of edges in any maximal graph
\Gamma
can then be determined by the Euler characteristic and the relation
3v+n=2e
, where
v
is the number of non-leaf (and hence non-labelled) vertices. Since
\Gamma
has rank
g
, we obtain

1-g=\chi(\Gamma)=(v+n)-e=\frac{2}{3}e-\frac{1}{3}n+n-e=\frac{2}{3}n-\frac{1}{3}e

Hence, the maximal number of edges is
3(g-1)+2n
, independent of
\Gamma
.

By contrast, a minimal graph
(\Delta,\tau)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
cannot contain any non-loop edges where at least one endpoint is unlabelled. Therefore there are exactly
n
vertices (all of which are labelled) and any maximal tree in
\Delta
must have
n-1
edges. Since
\Delta
has rank
g
, the remaining edges consist of
g
loops. Thus, the minimal number of edges is
(g-1)+n
, and is also independent of
\Delta
.

Since the number of edges (and hence vertices) of any
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is bounded and there are only finitely many possible labellings of the vertices of such a graph by
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we conclude that there are only finitely many isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
. As each non-invertible morphism collapses at least one edge, the longest possible chain of composable non-invertible morphisms starts from a maximal graph and ends in a minimal graph, and each morphism only collapses one edge. The number of morphisms in any longest chain is therefore the difference in the number of edges between a maximal and a minimal graph:
2(g-1)+n
. This proves (i) and (ii).

For (iii), let
(\Gamma_{0},\sigma_{0})\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
be the unique labelled graph consisting of a wedge of
g
circles and
n
intervals, where the wedge point is unlabelled, and the
n
leaves are labelled by
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We claim there is a zig-zag of morphisms from an arbitrary graph
(\Gamma,\sigma)
to
(\Gamma_{0},\sigma_{0})
. Given
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
, we first insert edges where exactly one endpoint is unlabelled to obtain a graph
(\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
such that each labelled vertex is a leaf. In particular, every internal vertex of
\Gamma^{\prime}
is unlabelled and no vertex labelled by
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
separates. Let
\Delta^{\prime}\subset\Gamma^{\prime}
be the (connected) subgraph obtained by deleting all leaves and their incident edges, and let
T^{\prime}\subset\Delta^{\prime}
be a maximal tree. Since
T^{\prime}
only contains edges connecting unlabeled vertices, we can collapse
T^{\prime}
to obtain
(\Gamma_{0},\sigma_{0})
as a quotient. Thus we have constructed a zig-zag

(\Gamma,\sigma)\leftarrow(\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\rightarrow(\Gamma_{0},\sigma_{0})

from the arbitrary graph
(\Gamma,\sigma)
to the fixed graph
(\Gamma_{0},\sigma_{0})
, as desired.
∎

Definition 2.31
.

An edge in
(\Gamma,\sigma)
is
redundant
if it splits the graph into two components, one of which does not contain labelled vertices.
Let
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm nr}\subset\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
denote the full subcategory on the graphs with no redundant edges.

Lemma 2.32
.

The inclusion
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm nr}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
admits a right adjoint.
In particular it is homotopy final, so any homotopy colimit over
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is equivalent to the homotopy colimit over the smaller category.

Proof.

Let
\rm J\colon\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm nr}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
be the inclusion.
Given
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
, let
T^{\rm red}(\Gamma)\subset\Gamma
be the set of all redundant edges. By definition,
T^{\rm red}(\Gamma)
is a collapsible forest. Moreover, if
f\colon(\Gamma,\sigma)\rightarrow(\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
is a morphism, then
f(T^{\rm red}(\Gamma))=T^{\rm red}(\Gamma^{\prime})
since if
\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus e^{\prime}=C_{1}^{\prime}\sqcup C_{2}^{\prime}
where
C_{2}^{\prime}
only contains unlabelled vertices, then
\Gamma\setminus f^{-1}(e)=f^{-1}(C_{1}^{\prime})\sqcup f^{-1}(C_{2}^{\prime})
and
f^{-1}(C_{2}^{\prime})
also only contains unlabelled vertices.
Thus the map
\Gamma\mapsto\Gamma/T^{\rm red}(\Gamma)
collapsing each component of
T^{\rm red}(\Gamma)
separately to a point defines a functor
\rm R\colon\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm nr}
along with a morphism
\nu_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}\colon(\Gamma,\sigma)\rightarrow\rm JR(\Gamma,\sigma)
for every
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
.
This defines a right adjoint to
\rm J
with unit
\nu
and counit the identity.
In particular, for every
(\Gamma,\sigma)
the category
(\Gamma,\sigma)/\rm J
has an initial object, namely
\rm R(\Gamma,\sigma)
, and hence is contractible, which proves that the inclusion is homotopy final.
∎

3.
The colimit formula for moduli spaces of
3
-manifolds

Let
M
be a compact, connected, orientable, non-prime
3
-manifold without
S^{2}
-boundary. The goal of this section is to write
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
as a homotopy colimit over a (finite) category of dual graphs
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
.

3.1.
Dual graphs to separating systems

In this section we introduce the dual graph of a separating system.
These dual graphs will be labelled by oriented diffeomorphism classes of irreducible
3
-manifolds, that is, they are objects in the following category.

Definition 3.1
.

We let
\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
denote the category of
3
-manifold graphs.
Its objects are graphs
\Gamma
with a labelling
l
that assigns to each vertex an oriented diffeomorphism class of irreducible
3
-manifolds such that if a vertex
v
is labelled by
l(v)=[S^{3}]
, then
v
has valence at least
3
.
A morphism
(\Gamma,l)\to(\Gamma^{\prime},l^{\prime})
in
\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
is a morphism of graphs
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
such that for every vertex
v\in V_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
its label is

l^{\prime}(v)=\sharp_{w\in V_{\Gamma}\cap f^{-1}(v)}l(w),

i.e.
the connected sum of the labels of the vertices that get collapsed to
v
(recall that
f^{-1}(v)
is a tree).

Remark 3.2
.

Determining a morphism
f\colon\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
in
\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
is equivalent to choosing a forest
F\subset\Gamma_{1}
such that each subtree of
F
has at most one vertex without an
S^{3}
label, and a label-preserving graph isomorphism from the graph
\Gamma_{1}/F\to\Gamma_{2}
where
\Gamma_{1}/F
is obtained by collapsing each tree in
F\subset\Gamma_{1}
and labelling the resultant vertex with the unique non-
S^{3}
label, or with
S^{3}
otherwise.
The condition that each tree of
F
has at most one non-
S^{3}
-label is necessary to ensure that
\Gamma_{2}
is still labelled by irreducible manifolds.

Remark 3.3
.

Let
-N
denote the manifold
N
endowed with the reverse orientation. Then if
N
admits an orientation reversing diffeomorphism it follows that its oriented diffeomorphism class
[N]
satisfies
[N]=[-N]
. If
N
does not admit an orientation reversing diffeomorphism (
N
is said to be
chiral
) then
[N]
and
[-N]
are distinct labels.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
be a
3
-manifold graph.
Pick a manifold
M_{v}
in the diffeomorphism class
l(v)
for each vertex
v
, and consider the
3
-manifold obtained by taking the disjoint union
\coprod_{v\in V}M_{v}
and then attaching a
1
-handle for each edge in the graph.
The resulting manifold
M_{\Gamma}
has prime decomposition:

M_{\Gamma}\cong(\sharp_{v\in V}M_{v})\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp b_{1}}

where
b_{1}
is the first Betti number of
\Gamma
.
If
\Gamma\to\Lambda
is a morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
then
M_{\Gamma}
and
N_{\Gamma}
must have the same prime decomposition and hence they are (oriented) diffeomorphic.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\mathrm{Gr}(M)\subset\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
be the full subcategory of those
3
-manifold graphs
(\Gamma,l)
such that
M_{\Gamma}
is (oriented) diffeomorphic to
M
.
We refer to
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
as an
M
-graph
.

We therefore have a disjoint decomposition

\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}=\coprod_{[M]}\mathrm{Gr}(M)

where the coproduct runs over the oriented diffeomorphism classes of
3
-manifolds.

Definition 3.5
.

If
\Sigma\subset M
is a separating system with no parallel spheres (
\Sigma\in\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
), then its
dual graph

G_{\Sigma\subset M}
is the
3
-manifold graph defined as follows:

•

V_{\Sigma}
is the set of connected components of the compact manifold
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
. Each vertex
K\in\pi_{0}(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)
is labelled by the oriented diffeomorphism class
[\widehat{K}]
,
i.e.
the oriented irreducible manifold corresponding to the spherical closure of
K
, up to orientation-preserving diffeomorphism. (Recall there are no spheres in
\partial M
so taking the spherical closure is equivalent to filling in spheres of
2\Sigma
.)

•

H_{\Sigma}
is the set of spheres in
\Sigma
with a choice of coorientation. This means there are two half edges for each sphere
S\subset\Sigma
, and we pair these using the half edge involution
i
.

•

Each half edge
h\in H_{\Sigma}
has as root
r(h)
the component of
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
with boundary the sphere associated to
h
, such that the coorientation is inward-pointing with respect to the connected component.
An example is shown in
Fig.

2
.

Remark 3.6
.

When the ambient manifold
M
containing
\Sigma
is clear from the context, we will sometimes just write
G_{\Sigma}
to denote the dual graph.

Figure 2
.
A separating system
\Sigma\in\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
and the corresponding dual graph.

Lemma 3.7
.

A
3
-manifold graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
appears as the dual graph of a separating system in
M
if and only if it is an
M
-graph.

Proof.

The manifold
M_{\Gamma}
was constructed by
1
-handle attachments.
If we let
\Sigma\subset M_{\Gamma}
be the collection of belt spheres for each
1
-handle, then the dual graph of
\Sigma
is canonically isomorphic to
\Gamma
.
By assumption we have
\varphi\colon M_{\Gamma}\cong M
, so
\Gamma
is isomorphic to the dual graph
G_{\varphi(\Sigma)\subset M}
.
∎

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\mathrm{SepMfd}
denote the following category.
Objects are tuples
(M,\Sigma)
of a connected, compact, oriented
3
-manifold
M
without spherical boundary, not diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times S^{2}
, and a separating system
\Sigma\subset M
in
\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
.
Morphisms
(M,\Sigma)\to(N,\Upsilon)
are orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms
\varphi\colon M\cong N
satisfying
\varphi(\Sigma)\supseteq\Upsilon
.

Definition 3.9
.

Every morphism
\varphi\colon(M,\Sigma)\to(N,\Upsilon)
in
\mathrm{SepMfd}
yields a graph map
G(\varphi)\colon G_{\Sigma\subset M}\to G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
as follows.
(An example is shown in
Fig.

3
.)
A vertex in
G_{\Sigma\subset M}
is a connected component
U\subset M\!\mid\!\Sigma
and
G(\varphi)
sends it to the unique connected component
U^{\prime}\subset N\!\mid\!\Upsilon
such that
\varphi(\smash{\mathring{U}})\subset\smash{\mathring{U^{\prime}}}
as submanifolds of
M
.
A half-edge in
G_{\Sigma\subset M}
is a connected component
S\subset\Sigma
together with a coorientation.
There are two cases:
if
\varphi(S)\subset\Upsilon
, then the graph map
G(\varphi)
sends
S
to the half edge
\varphi(S)
(with its induced coorientation) in
G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
,
otherwise it sends it to the unique vertex
U^{\prime}\subset N\!\mid\!\Upsilon
such that
\varphi(S)\subset U^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

The association
(M,\Sigma)\mapsto G_{\Sigma\subset M}
yields a well-defined functor

G\colon\mathrm{SepMfd}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}.

Proof.

First we show that
G(\varphi)
is compatible with the root map and half-edge involution.
Let
S\subset\Sigma
be some component with a coorientation pointing into
\smash{\mathring{U}}
for some component
U\subset M\!\mid\!\Sigma
,
i.e.

S
is a half-edge incident at the vertex
U
in
G_{\Sigma\subset M}
.
If
\varphi(S)\not\subset\Upsilon
then both
S
and
U
are contained in the same connected component of
N\setminus\Upsilon
and hence the half edge
S
and the vertex
U
are sent to the same vertex of
G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
.
If, on the other hand,
\varphi(S)\subset\Upsilon
, then
S
is mapped to the half-edge
\varphi(S)
, whose coorientation points into some component
U^{\prime}\subset N\!\mid\!\Sigma
and we will have
\varphi(U)\subseteq\smash{\mathring{U^{\prime}}}
.

For
G(\varphi)
to be a well-defined map in
\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
we have to show that the preimage of each vertex is a tree and that it is compatible with the labelling by diffeomorphism classes.
If
v\in G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
is a vertex corresponding to a connected component
U^{\prime}\subset N\!\mid\!\Upsilon
, then
G(f)^{-1}(v)
contains exactly those vertices corresponding to components
U_{i}\subset M\!\mid\!\Sigma
with
\varphi(\smash{\mathring{U_{i}}})\subseteq\smash{\mathring{U^{\prime}}}
.
Therefore,
U^{\prime}
can be obtained by gluing the
U_{i}
along boundary spheres and we must have
[\widehat{U^{\prime}}]=(\sharp_{i}[\widehat{U_{i}}])\sharp[S^{1}\times S^{2}]^{\sharp b_{1}}
for
b_{1}
the first Betti number of the graph
G(f)^{-1}(v)
.
Since we know that
\smash{\widehat{U^{\prime}}}
is irreducible, we must have
b_{1}=0
and
[\widehat{U_{i}}]=[S^{3}]
for all but at most one
i
.
Therefore,
G(f)
only collapses forests and moreover the labelling satisfies
l^{\prime}(v)=\sharp_{w\in G(f)^{-1}(v)}l(w)
, as required.

Finally, inspecting the definition shows that
G
is functorial,
i.e.
that
G(f_{2}\circ f_{1})=G(f_{2})\circ G(f_{1})
.
∎

Figure 3
.
The morphism
G_{\Sigma}\to G_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
induced by
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\Sigma\in\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
.

Lemma 3.11
.

The functor
G\colon\mathrm{SepMfd}\to\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
is full:
for all
(M,\Sigma)
and
(N,\Upsilon)
in
\mathrm{SepMfd}
and every graph morphism
f\colon G_{\Sigma\subset M}\to G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
there is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon M\cong N
such that
\varphi(\Sigma)\supseteq\Upsilon
and such that
G(\varphi)=f
.
Moreover,
G
is essentially surjective.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma_{0}\subseteq\Sigma
be the union of those spheres for which the corresponding edge of
G_{\Sigma\subset M}
is not collapsed by
f
.
The dual graph
G_{\Sigma_{0}\subset M}
is hence obtained from
G_{\Sigma\subset M}
by collapsing exactly those edges that are collapsed by
f
, and
f
induces an isomorphism of labelled graphs
f_{0}\colon G_{\Sigma_{0}\subset M}\cong G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
.
It will suffice to show that there is a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon M\cong N
with
\varphi(\Sigma_{0})=\Upsilon
and
G(\varphi)\colon(M,\Sigma_{0})\to(N,\Upsilon))=f_{0}
.
(Indeed, such a
\varphi
also satisfies
\varphi(\Sigma)\supseteq\varphi(\Sigma_{0})=\Upsilon
and
G(\varphi\colon(M,\Sigma)\to(N,\Upsilon))=f
.)

The graph isomorphism
f_{0}\colon G_{\Sigma_{0}\subset M}\cong G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
induces a bijection between the sets of edges
\pi_{0}(\Sigma_{0})\cong\pi_{0}(\Upsilon)
, and we can find a diffeomorphism
\psi\colon\Sigma_{0}\cong\Upsilon
such that
\pi_{0}(\psi)
is exactly that bijection.
Choose collars of
\Sigma_{0}\subset\Sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon}
in
M
and
\Upsilon\subset\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}
in
N
.
(That is, we have
\Sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon}\cong\Sigma_{0}\times[-1,1]
and
\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}\cong\Upsilon\times[-1,1]
.)
Then we can extend
\psi
to an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\psi^{\varepsilon}\colon\Sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon}\cong\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}
.
Up to composing with reflections where necessary, we may assume that the map
\psi^{\varepsilon}
induces on the set of spheres with coorientation is exactly the bijection that
f_{0}
induces on the set of half-edges.

We need to show that
\psi^{\varepsilon}
extends to a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon M\cong N
. (If it does,
\varphi
will automatically induce the map
f_{0}
on dual graphs because
f_{0}
is determined by what it does on half-edges.)
For each connected component
U\subset M\!\mid\!\Sigma_{0}
corresponding to some vertex
v_{U}
in
G_{\Sigma_{0}\subset M}
, let
V\subset N\!\mid\!\Upsilon
be the component corresponding to the vertex
f_{0}(v_{U})
in
G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
.
Because
f_{0}
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
, it preserves the labelling by oriented diffeomorphism classes (of spherical closures) and in particular
[\widehat{U}]
must agree with
[\widehat{V}]
,
i.e.
there exists an orientation preserving diffeomorphism
\eta\colon\widehat{U}\cong\widehat{V}
.

The preimage of
\Sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon}
under the map
U\to M
(which on the interior is the inclusion) defines a collection of annuli
A\subset U
that collar the spherical boundaries of
U
.
There is one such annulus for each half-edge incident at
v_{U}
.
Similarly, the preimage of
\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}
under the map
V\to N
defines a collection of annuli
A^{\prime}\subset V
collaring the spherical boundaries.
Because
f_{0}
is a graph map it preserves the incidence of half-edges at vertices and thus
\psi^{\varepsilon}
yields a diffeomorphism
A\cong A^{\prime}
.
In the spherical closure, we can complete the annuli to discs and
\psi^{\varepsilon}
to a diffeomorphisms between these collections of discs.
Now isotope
\eta\colon\widehat{U}\cong\widehat{V}
in such a way that it restricts to this prescribed diffeomorphism on discs.
(This follows because
\emb^{+}(\sqcup_{k}D^{3},M)
is connected for all
k
.)
In particular, after the isotopy
\eta
will restrict to a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{U}\colon U\cong V
that agrees with
\psi^{\varepsilon}
on overlaps.
Performing this construction for all
U\in\pi_{0}(M\!\mid\!\Sigma_{0})
constructs the desired diffeomorphism
\varphi
.

Essential surjectivity follows from
3.7
.
∎

By restricting the functor
G
to the automorphisms of an object
(M,\Sigma)\in\mathrm{SepMfd}
we obtain:

Corollary 3.12
.

The functor
G\colon\mathrm{SepMfd}\to\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}
induces a surjective group homomorphism

(3)

\gamma_{\Sigma}\colon\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\to\Aut_{\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}}(G_{\Sigma}).

3.2.
Separating systems indexed by graphs

In light of
3.11
, one can regard
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
as encoding, at least combinatorially, all of the ways to build
M
by attaching 1-handles to the irreducible factors of
M
. In order to get a model for
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
, we will need to define a functor from
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
to a collection of spaces equipped with a
\Diff^{+}(M)
-action.

The natural target for
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
will be a space parametrising all separating systems whose dual graph is identified with
\Gamma
. To see how
\Diff^{+}(M)
acts, observe that for any separating system
\Sigma
and
\varphi\in\Diff^{+}(M)
,
\varphi\colon(M,\Sigma)\to(M,\varphi(\Sigma)=\Upsilon
) is a morphism in
\mathrm{SepMfd}
, so under the functor
G
of
3.9
we obtain an induced isomorphism of dual graphs
\varphi_{G}\colon G_{\Sigma}\cong G_{\Upsilon}
.
In particular, if
G_{\Sigma}\cong\Gamma
then
G_{\Upsilon}\cong\Gamma
.

Definition 3.13
.

For
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
we define a
\Gamma
-separating system
to be a separating system
\Sigma\in\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
whose dual graph is (identified with)
\Gamma
.
To be precise, we define the set of
\Gamma
-separating systems as

\sep^{\Gamma}(M):=\{(\Sigma,\alpha)\;|\;\Sigma\in\sep^{\nparallel}(M)\text{ and }\alpha\colon G_{\Sigma}\cong\Gamma\}

where
\Sigma
is a separating system and
\alpha
an isomorphism of labelled graphs.
The group
\Diff^{+}(M)
acts on
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
by

\varphi.(\Sigma,\alpha)=(\varphi(\Sigma),\alpha\circ\varphi_{G}^{-1})

where
\varphi_{G}\colon G_{\Sigma}\cong G_{\varphi(\Sigma)}
is the isomorphism that
\varphi
induces on dual graphs.
(This action is transitive by
3.11
.)
We topologise
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
in the unique way such that the orbit map,

\Diff^{+}(M)\twoheadrightarrow\sep^{\Gamma}(M),

defined by acting on some (and hence any) point, is a quotient map.

Definition 3.14
.

For a morphism
f\colon\Gamma\to\Lambda
in
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
we define

f_{*}\colon\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\to\sep^{\Lambda}(M),\qquad(\Sigma,\alpha)\mapsto(\Sigma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

where
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
contains all those spheres corresponding to edges in
G_{\Sigma}\overset{\alpha}{\cong}\Gamma
that are not collapsed by
f

and
\alpha^{\prime}\colon G_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\cong\Lambda
is the unique isomorphism such that the following square commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{\Sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

where the left hand map is the image of the morphism
\mathrm{id}\colon(M,\Sigma)\to(M,\Sigma^{\prime})\in\mathrm{SepMfd}
under the functor
G
from
3.9
.
This defines a functor
\sep^{(-)}(M)\colon\mathrm{Gr}(M)\to\Diff^{+}(M)\text{-}\mathrm{Top}
from
M
-graphs to topological spaces with a continuous
\Diff^{+}(M)
-action.

Remark 3.15
.

From a (higher) category theory perspective, we can think of
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
as the fibre of the restricted functor

G_{|\sep(M)}\colon\sep(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}(M)\subset\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm mfd}.

This functor is (equivalent to) a left fibration and hence sending an object
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
to its fibre
G_{|\sep(M)}^{-1}(\Gamma)
defines a functor from
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
to the category of spaces.
(To make this precise, we would have to turn our topological categories into
\infty
-categories.)
Moreover, “straightening” a left fibration in this way always yields a diagram whose colimit is exactly the weak homotopy type of the total space
[
37
, Corollary 3.3.4.6]
.
This analogy hence lines up with
3.23
where we will see that the homotopy colimit of
\sep^{(-)}(M)
is equivalent to
\|\sep(M)\|
.

The next lemma highlights some topological properties of
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
that we will need.

Lemma 3.16
.

For any
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
:

(1)

The projection map
p_{\Gamma}\colon\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\to\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
sending
(\Sigma,\alpha)\mapsto\Sigma
is a covering whose fibre at a separating system
\Sigma
is the set of graph isomorphisms
\Gamma\cong G_{\Sigma}
.
(In particular the fibre is empty when
G_{\Sigma}
is not isomorphic to
\Gamma
.)

(2)

\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
is
\Diff^{+}(M)
-locally retractile.

Proof.

We know that
\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
is
\Diff^{+}(M)
-locally retractile by
2.12
(1) and
2.13
. Since
p_{\Gamma}
is
\Diff^{+}(M)
-equivariant, it will suffice to verify the fibres of
p_{\Gamma}
are discrete by Lemma
2.2
. By definition, the fibre of
p_{\Gamma}
is the set of graph isomorphisms
\Gamma\cong G_{\Sigma}
in
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
, which is either empty or finite and discrete.
∎

Definition 3.17
.

For a separating system
\Sigma\subset M
we define the subgroup
\smash{\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
to be the subgroup consisting of
\smash{\varphi\in\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
such that for each sphere
S\subset\Sigma
, we have
\varphi(S)=S
and
\varphi
preserves the coorientation of
S
.

This subgroup is the kernel of the homomorphism
\gamma_{\Sigma}\colon\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\to\Aut_{\mathrm{Gr}(M)}(G_{\Sigma})
defined in
3.9
.
Since
\gamma_{\Sigma}
is surjective,
\smash{\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
fits into the short exact sequence

(4)

1\rightarrow\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)\rightarrow\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\twoheadrightarrow\Aut_{\mathrm{Gr}(M)}(G_{\Sigma})\rightarrow 1.

In terms of the group action,
\smash{\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
is exactly the
\Diff^{+}(M)
-stabiliser of
(\Sigma,\alpha)\in\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
for any isomorphism
\alpha\colon G_{\Sigma}\cong\Gamma
.
In particular, since
\Aut_{\mathrm{Gr}(M)}(G_{\Sigma})
is finite,
\smash{\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
is a finite index subgroup of
\smash{\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
.

Remark 3.18
.

The subgroup

\smash{\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
can be identified as those orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms
\varphi\colon M\to M
that are isotopic (in
\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)
) to a diffeomorphism that is the identity in a neighbourhood of
\Sigma
.
However,
\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)
is not equivalent to the subgroup of diffeomorphisms which fix a neighbourhood of
\Sigma
because we still allow the spheres to rotate.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
\Sigma\subset M
be a separating system with dual graph
\Gamma=G_{\Sigma}
.

(1)

There is a homeomorphism

\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\cong\Diff^{+}(M)/\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma).

(2)

The quotient map
\Diff^{+}(M)\to\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
is a principal
\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)
-bundle, and in particular,
\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)
is an admissible subgroup of
\Diff^{+}(M)
.

Proof.

Since the action of
\Diff^{+}(M)
on
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
is transitive, we can identify
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
with the coset space of the stabiliser of any separating system
\Sigma
whose dual graph is isomorphic to
\Gamma
. This stabiliser is
\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)
, showing (1). Now by Lemma
3.16
(2)
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
is
\Diff^{+}(M)
-locally retractile, which, together with (1), implies (2).
∎

Since
\sep^{(-)}(M)
(
3.14
) defines a functor to topological spaces equipped with a
\Diff^{+}(M)
-action, we obtain a new functor from
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
to
\mathrm{Top}
by taking a homotopy quotient with respect to the
\Diff^{+}(M)
-action.

Definition 3.20
.

We define a functor
\mathcal{S}\colon\mathrm{Gr}(M)\to\mathrm{Top}
by

\mathcal{S}(\Gamma):=\sep^{\Gamma}(M)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(M)=\big(\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\times\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\big)/\Diff^{+}(M).

A point in
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
can be described as a triple
(W,\Sigma,\alpha)
where
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
is an oriented
3
-manifold,
\Sigma\subset W
is a separating system, and
\alpha\colon G_{\Sigma}\cong\Gamma
an isomorphism of labelled graphs.
This implies that
W
is abstractly diffeomorphic to
M
, but there is no preferred such diffeomorphism.

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
, then for every
\Sigma
such that
G_{\Sigma}\cong\Gamma
, there is an equivalence

\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)\simeq B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma).

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
3.19
(1) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\big(\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\times\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\big)/\Diff^{+}(M)

\displaystyle\cong\left(\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\times\big(\Diff^{+}(M)/\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)\big)\right)/{\Diff^{+}(M)}

\displaystyle\cong\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})/\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma),

which is a model for
B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M)
by Lemma
3.19
(2) and
2.4
(3).
∎

3.3.
The homotopy colimit

Recall the definition of homotopy colimit from
Section

2.4
. We will now show that the contractibility of
|\sep^{\nparallel}_{\bullet}(M)|
implies that the homotopy colimit of
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
indexed over
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
is contractible.
After taking homotopy orbits with respect to the
\Diff^{+}(M)
action on
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
, we will then obtain a description of
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
as a homotopy colimit of
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
. Let us first recall a technical result that we will need to deduce contractibility.

Lemma 3.22

(Base-change lemma,
[
48
, Theorem 3.25]
)
.

Let
f\colon X_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
be a map of simplicial spaces such that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{0})^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}^{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{0})^{n+1}}

is a homotopy pullback square and such that
f_{0}\colon X_{0}\to Y_{0}
is surjective on connected components.
(Here the vertical map sends an
n
-simplex to its
n+1
vertices.)
Then the induced map on fat geometric realisations is an equivalence:

\|f\|\colon\|X_{\bullet}\|\xrightarrow{\simeq}\|Y_{\bullet}\|.

Proposition 3.23
.

The homotopy colimit

\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\sep^{\Gamma}(M)

is contractible.

Proof.

From Definition
2.19
the homotopy colimit is given by

|E_{\bullet}|
, where
E_{\bullet}
is the simplicial space with

E_{n}:=\coprod_{\Gamma_{0}\to\dots\to\Gamma_{n}}\sep^{\Gamma_{0}}(M)

such that the coproduct runs over all sequences of
n
composable morphisms in
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
.
Because the inclusion of the degenerate simplices is a cofibration (in fact, it is a disjoint summand) we can equivalently work with the fat geometric realisation.
Thus a point
p\in E_{n}
has the form

p=(\Gamma_{0}\overset{f_{1}}{\to}\cdots\overset{f_{n}}{\to}\Gamma_{n},(\Sigma_{0},\alpha_{0}))

where the first entry is the indexing chain, and
(\Sigma_{0},\alpha_{0})\in\sep^{\Gamma_{0}}(M)
.
We can now define a map from
E_{\bullet}
to
\sep^{\nparallel}_{\bullet}(M)
,
which is given levelwise by

\displaystyle\phi_{n}\colon E_{n}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\sep^{\nparallel}_{n}(M)

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{0}\overset{f_{1}}{\to}\cdots\overset{f_{n}}{\to}\Gamma_{n},(\Sigma_{0},\alpha_{0}))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\Sigma_{n}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Sigma_{1}\subseteq\Sigma_{0})

where
\Sigma_{i}\subseteq\Sigma_{0}
