is the subsystem containing exactly those spheres of
\Sigma_{0}
that correspond to edges in
\Gamma_{0}
that are not collapsed by the composite
(f_{i}\circ\dots\circ f_{1})\colon\Gamma_{0}\to\Gamma_{i}
.
Equivalently, we can use the functoriality of
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
to define
\Sigma_{i}
to be the image of
\Sigma_{0}
under the map
\sep^{\Gamma_{0}}(M)\to\sep^{\Gamma_{i}}(M)
induced by
(f_{i}\circ\dots\circ f_{1})
.

We claim that
\phi_{\bullet}
satisfies the conditions of the base-change lemma (Lemma
3.22
) and hence induces an equivalence:

\|\phi_{\bullet}\|\colon\|E_{\bullet}\|\xrightarrow{\simeq}\|\sep^{\nparallel}_{\bullet}(M)\|

This will conclude the proof as we know from Theorem
2.14
that the right hand side is contractible.

It remains to prove that
\phi_{\bullet}
satisfies the conditions of the base-change lemma,
i.e.
we want to show that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sep^{\nparallel}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E_{0})^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sep^{\nparallel}_{0})^{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{0})^{n+1}}

is a homotopy pullback square and
\phi_{0}\colon E_{0}\to\sep^{\nparallel}_{0}
is surjective on connected components.
To show this, we first argue that the map
\phi_{0}
is a surjective covering map (Claim 1) from which it follows that the square is a homotopy pullback if it is a pullback of spaces. We then show that the vertical maps are subspace inclusions (Claim 2), and
hence being a pullback of spaces is equivalent to being a pullback of sets.

Claim 1
:
\phi_{0}\colon E_{0}\to\sep^{\nparallel}_{0}
is a surjective covering map.

Proof of Claim 1:

For each
\Gamma
,
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\to\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
is a covering map by Lemma
3.16
.
Its image is the subspace of all those separating systems whose dual graph is isomorphic to
\Gamma
.
By taking the disjoint union over all
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
we obtain a surjective covering map

E_{0}=\coprod_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\twoheadrightarrow\sep^{\nparallel}(M).\qed

Claim 2:
The vertical maps
E_{n}\to(E_{0})^{n+1}
and
\sep^{\nparallel}_{n}\to(\sep^{\nparallel}_{0})^{n+1}
are both subspace inclusions.

Proof of Claim 2:

For
\sep^{\nparallel}
this follows from the definition, as we defined
\sep^{\nparallel}_{n}(M)
as a subspace of
\sep_{n}(M)
, which we defined as a subspace of
\sep_{0}(M)^{n+1}
.

In the case of
E_{n}
the map in question is:

E_{n}=\coprod_{\Gamma_{0}\to\dots\to\Gamma_{n}}\sep^{\Gamma_{0}}(M)\longrightarrow\coprod_{\Gamma_{0},\dots,\Gamma_{n}}\sep^{\Gamma_{0}}(M)\times\dots\times\sep^{\Gamma_{n}}(M)\subset(E_{0})^{n+1}.

This is indeed a subspace inclusion, as the information of the map
\Gamma_{i}\to\Gamma_{i+1}
can be recovered from the inclusion of separating systems
\Sigma_{i+1}\subseteq\Sigma_{i}
and the graph isomorphisms
\alpha_{i}\colon G_{\Sigma_{i}}\cong\Gamma_{i}
.∎

Now to show that the square is a homotopy pullback, by Claims 1 and 2 we need only show it is a pullback of sets, which follows from rewriting points in
E_{n}
as tuples
\{(\Sigma_{i},\alpha_{i})\}_{i=0}^{n}
.
The remaining condition of the base-change lemma is that
\phi_{0}
is surjective on connected components, which we have already established as part of Claim
1
.
∎

Theorem 3.24
.

For every
M\ncong S^{1}\times S^{2}
there is a functor
\mathcal{S}\colon\mathrm{Gr}(M)\to\mathrm{Top}
such that

B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\simeq\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{S}(\Gamma).

If
\Gamma=G_{\Sigma}
is the dual graph of a sphere system
\Sigma\subset M
, then
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
is equivalent to the classifying space of the group of those orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms
\varphi\in\Diff^{+}(M)
such that for all spheres
S\subseteq\Sigma
we have
\varphi(S)=S
and
\varphi_{|S}
is orientation-preserving.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the functor from
3.20
.
By
3.21
this satisfies that
\mathcal{S}(G_{\Sigma})
is the classifying space of
\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)
, which is exactly the group described in the statement of the theorem.

To compute the homotopy colimit of
\mathcal{S}
, we can commute homotopy colimits with homotopy quotients.
(The homotopy colimit is defined as geometric realisation of disjoint unions and homotopy quotients commute with both of those constructions
[
6
, Lemma 2.12(1) and (5)]
.)
Hence, we compute

\displaystyle\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\left(\sep^{\Gamma}(M)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(M)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\left(\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\right)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(M)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle B\!\Diff^{+}(M)

using that
\hocolim\sep^{\Gamma}(M)\simeq*
by
3.23
.
∎

4.
The splitting map

For any oriented 3-manifold
M
with prime decomposition
M=P_{1}\sharp\cdots\sharp P_{n}\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}
,
we set

P_{M}=\amalg_{i=1}^{n}P_{i},

where
P_{M}
inherits its orientation from
M
. In this section we define a functor
\mathcal{F}^{-}(P_{M})\colon\mathrm{Gr}(M)\to\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\text{-}\mathrm{Top}
, which refines the functor
\mathcal{S}(-)
in the sense that
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\simeq\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
.
This will allow us to construct a “splitting map”
\mathbb{W}
that fits into a homotopy fibre sequence:

\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{\mathbb{W}}B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M}).

In general, the functor
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})
takes disconnected values.
By restricting to one connected component we are able to rewrite the homotopy colimit as the homotopy colimit of another functor
\mathcal{H}
on the category
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
, as stated in the introduction.
We denote the fibre of
\mathbb{W}
by
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
and think of it as a “space of handle-attachments to
P_{M}
”– this perspective will be further substantiated by the descriptions in
Section

5
.
Finally, we also show that
\mathbb{W}
commutes with restriction to the boundary and use this to obtain variants
\mathbb{W}_{A}
for moduli spaces where some submanifold
A\subset\partial M
is fixed.

4.1.
Overview of the strategy

Given a separating system
\Sigma\subset M
, let
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{{\rm prime}}
denote the union of the components of
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
not diffeomorphic to
S^{3}\setminus\sqcup_{k}\smash{\mathring{D}}^{3}
for some
k\geq 0
. Our naïve approach towards constructing
\mathbb{W}
is to define a “prime marking” to be an embedding
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime}\hookrightarrow P_{M}
. This leads to consider a space of all such “prime markings” up to reparametrisations:

\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Gamma):=\emb^{\widehat{=}}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime},P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma).

(Recall the superscript
\widehat{=}
from
2.16
indicates “diffeomorphism after taking spherical closures”.) This admits an action of
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
by post-composition and we can use Lemma
2.17
to argue that the homotopy quotient recovers
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
.
In particular, we obtain a homotopy fibre sequence

\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Gamma)\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Gamma)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\simeq\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M}).

If we could now take homotopy colimits of the left and middle term over
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
, this would result in the desired prime decomposition fibre sequence:

``\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Gamma)"\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\simeq\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M}).

Unfortunately, this naïve approach suffers from a lack of functoriality with respect to morphisms in
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
, which prevents us from taking the homotopy colimit.
Concretely, given a graph
\Gamma_{0}
and a separating system
\Sigma_{0}
with
\Gamma_{0}\cong G_{\Sigma_{0}}
, an edge collapse
\Gamma_{0}\to\Gamma_{1}
in
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
corresponds to forgetting a sphere in
\Sigma_{0}
, yielding
\Sigma_{0}\supset\Sigma_{1}
. Consequently there is an (in general, strict) inclusion
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma_{0})^{\rm prime}\subset(M\!\mid\!\Sigma_{1})^{\rm prime}
. Starting with an embedding
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma_{0})^{\rm prime}\hookrightarrow P_{M}
, one thus has no functorial way of extending it to
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma_{1})^{\rm prime}
.
This motivates us to define prime markings in terms of what we call the
maximal prime piece

P(\Sigma\subset M)
.
Crucially, this modification has the advantage that passing to a sub-sphere system
\Sigma_{0}\supset\Sigma_{1}
yields a smaller maximal prime piece, hence functoriality will follow from restriction of embeddings.

4.2.
The maximal prime piece

Let
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
be defined in an analogous way
to
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
above. The components of
M\setminus\Sigma
that are diffeomorphic to punctured 3-spheres will be referred to as
spherical components
of
M\setminus\Sigma
, while components of
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
will be called
prime components
.

Definition 4.1
.

A path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M
is called
\Sigma
-primary
if
\gamma(0)\in(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
, and
\gamma^{-1}(C)
is connected or empty for each component
C\subset M\setminus\Sigma
, and moreover
\gamma
only intersects one component of
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
.

For a
\Sigma
-primary path
\gamma
, the pre-image
\gamma^{-1}((M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime})
is either
[0,1]
or
[0,\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
. In other words,
\gamma
starts in a prime component and either remains in that component for the entire path, or leaves and never re-enters that component nor any other prime component.

Definition 4.2
.

For a separating system
\Sigma\subset M
, define the
maximal prime piece

P(\Sigma\subset M)
as

P(\Sigma\subset M)=\left\{(p,[\gamma])\,\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}p\in M,~\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M,~\gamma(0)\in(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime},~\gamma(1)=p,\\
[\gamma]\text{ contains a $\Sigma$-primary representative}\end{array}\right\}

where
[\gamma]
is the equivalence class of
\gamma
up to homotopies that fix
\gamma(1)
and preserve the condition that
\gamma(0)\in(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
.

Figure 4
.
The maximal prime piece
P(\Sigma\subset M)
of a separating system
\Sigma\subset M
, and the map
P(\Sigma\subset M)\to M
.
The shaded region is the ordinary prime piece
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
on which the map restricts to a homeomorphism.
Note that the point
p\in M
has three preimages, depending on which primary path we equip it with.

The definition of
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is reminiscent of the construction of the universal cover of
M
as a set of based paths up to homotopy.
We will show that
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is a 3-manifold with boundary and that the projection map
\pi\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)\rightarrow M
defined by
(p,[\gamma])\mapsto p
is a local homeomorphism map away from the spherical boundary components.

For this, it it will be useful to consider the variant

Q_{\Sigma}:=\left\{(p,[\gamma])\;\big|\;p\in M,~\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M,~\gamma(0)\in(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime},~\gamma(1)=p\right\}

where we do not require
[\gamma]
to contain a
\Sigma
-primary representative.
The projection map
\xi\colon Q_{\Sigma}\to M
sending
(p,[\gamma])\to p
is a covering map and hence
Q_{\Sigma}
is a smooth oriented
3
-manifold, though it will typically not be compact nor connected.
By definition, the maximal prime piece
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is the subspace of those
(p,[\gamma])
where the equivalence class
[\gamma]
contains a
\Sigma
-primary representative.

To understand the connected components of
P(\Sigma\subset M)
,
note that there is a natural embedding
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}\hookrightarrow P(\Sigma\subset M)
defined by sending
p
to
(p,[\mathrm{cst}_{p}])
where
\mathrm{cst}_{p}
is the constant path at
p
.
This induces a bijection

\pi_{0}((M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime})\cong\pi_{0}(P(\Sigma\subset M)).

To see that it is bijective, we construct an inverse by sending the path component of
(p,[\gamma])
in
P(\Sigma\subset M)
to
[\gamma(0)]
, which is well-defined since
\gamma(0)
must always remain in the same prime component under a homotopy.
This is indeed an inverse as
(p,[\gamma])
and
(\gamma(0),[\mathrm{cst}_{\gamma(0)}])
are connected by a path defined by
\gamma
, if
\gamma
is
\Sigma
-primary.
By the same reasoning, the inclusion into
Q_{\Sigma}
also induces a bijection on path-components.

Proposition 4.3
.

For any sphere system
\Sigma\subset M
, the maximal prime piece is abstractly homeomorphic to a 3-manifold
P_{M}^{\prime}
obtained from
P_{M}
by removing the interiors of finitely many discs.

Proof.

As noted above,
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is a subspace of
Q_{\Sigma}
and
\xi\colon Q_{\Sigma}\to M
is a covering map.
Let
X\subset P(\Sigma\subset M)
be the homeomorphic image of
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
obtained by equipping points with the constant path.
Let
\widetilde{\Sigma}:=\xi^{-1}(\Sigma)
be the collection of spheres obtained as the lifts of the separating system in
M
.

The connected components of
P(\Sigma\subset M)
and of
Q_{\Sigma}
are in bijection with the components of
X\cong(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
, which in turn correspond to the prime factors
P_{M}=\amalg_{i=1}^{n}P_{i}
.
The inclusion
X\to Q_{\Sigma}
is an isomorphism on the fundamental group for each connected component.
1
1

1

In fact,
X\to Q_{\Sigma}\to M
is exactly the first Moore–Postnikov factorization of
X\to M
.

Indeed, if we decompose
Q_{\Sigma}=\amalg_{i=1}^{n}Q_{\Sigma,i}
, then each
Q_{\Sigma,i}\to M
is the covering space corresponding exactly to the inclusion
\pi_{1}(P_{i})\hookrightarrow\pi_{1}(M)
.
Form this it follows that each component of
\widetilde{\Sigma}\cap Q_{\Sigma,i}
separates
Q_{\Sigma,i}
:
otherwise there would be a surjection
\pi_{1}(Q_{\Sigma,i})\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
that kills
\pi_{1}(X\cap Q_{\Sigma,i})
, but this group is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(Q_{\Sigma,i})
.

The restriction on
P(\Sigma\subset M)
that
[\gamma]
contain a
\Sigma
-primary representative equivalently means that it consists of all points in
Q_{\Sigma}
that can be connected to
X
by a path that passes through at most one elevation of each component of
M\setminus\Sigma
, none of which are in
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
except
X
itself.
Since
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
only has finitely many components,
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is a union of the closure of
X
and a finite number of copies of the simply connected components of
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
, where any two such components meet along a separating 2-sphere in
\smash{\widetilde{\Sigma}}
.
In particular,
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is a compact submanifold of
Q_{\Sigma}
and can be obtained from
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
by successively attaching punctured 3-balls along boundary 2-spheres, so it is homeomorphic to
\amalg_{i=1}^{n}(P_{i}\setminus\amalg_{j_{i}}\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}})
.
∎

As alluded to above, the crucial property of the maximal prime piece is that refining the sphere system makes the maximal prime piece is larger. This is based on the following observation.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\Upsilon\subset\Sigma\subset M
be two separating systems in
M
.
Then every
\Upsilon
-primary path
\gamma
can be homotoped to a
\Sigma
-primary path while fixing the endpoint
\gamma(1)
.

Proof.

The inclusion
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}\subseteq(M\setminus\Upsilon)^{\rm prime}
is a bijection on
\pi_{0}
.
Therefore, we can homotope the starting point
\gamma(0)
within
(M\setminus\Upsilon)^{\rm prime}
such that it lies in
(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}
and
\gamma
is still
\Upsilon
-primary.

Working inductively, we may assume that
\Sigma=\Upsilon\sqcup S
for a single
2
-sphere
S\subset M\setminus\Upsilon
.
Let
C\subset N\setminus\Upsilon
be the component containing
S
, so as a set
C=C_{0}\sqcup S\sqcup C_{1}
for two components
C_{0},C_{1}\subset N\setminus\varphi(\Sigma)
.
There are two cases: either
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
both are a punctured spheres, or one of them is a punctured sphere and the other is a punctured
P_{i}
.
Because at least one of the
C_{i}
is simply connected and
S
is connected, it is possible to homotope
\gamma_{|\gamma^{-1}(C)}
(within
C
and relative to its endpoints) to
\gamma^{\prime}
with
(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(C_{i})
connected or empty.
Extending this by the constant homotopy outside of
\gamma^{-1}(C)
we obtain
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
such that
\gamma^{\prime}
is
\Sigma
-primary.
∎

From the above lemma we can deduce a
contravariant
functoriality of the maximal prime piece.

Proposition 4.5
.

Every morphism
\varphi\colon(M,\Sigma)\to(N,\Upsilon)
in
\mathrm{SepMfd}
,
i.e.
every diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon M\cong N
with
\varphi(\Sigma)\supseteq\Upsilon
,
induces an embedding of maximal prime pieces
P(\varphi)\colon P(\Upsilon\subset N)\hookrightarrow P(\Sigma\subset M)
.
This embedding is uniquely characterised by the property that it makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(\Sigma\subset M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(N\setminus\Upsilon)^{\rm prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(\Upsilon\subset N)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{|(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\varphi)}

commute.
This promotes maximal prime piece construction into a continuous functor

P\colon\mathrm{SepMfd}^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mfd}_{3}

into the category of
3
-manifolds and smooth embeddings.

Proof.

By
4.3
the maximal prime piece is a compact submanifold with boundary
P(\Sigma\subset M)\subset Q_{\Sigma}
.
There is a unique diffeomorphism
\psi
making the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{\Sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(N\setminus\Upsilon)^{\rm prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{\Upsilon}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{|(M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commute and it is given by
\psi(p,[\gamma])=(\varphi(p),[\varphi\circ\gamma])
.

From
4.4
it follows that
\psi(P(\Sigma\subset M))\supseteq P(\Upsilon\subset N)
as submanifolds of
Q_{\Upsilon}
.
Therefore we may set
P(\varphi):=(\psi^{-1})_{|P(\Upsilon\subset N)}
.
This is unique because every point in
P(\Upsilon\subset N)
can be connected by a path to a point in
\varphi((M\setminus\Sigma)^{\rm prime})
, and every path in
M
that does lift to
P(\Sigma\subset M)
lifts uniquely as that map is a local homeomorphism.

It follows from the uniqueness that for two composable morphisms
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
in
\mathrm{SepMfd}
we must have
P(\varphi_{1})\circ P(\varphi_{2})=P(\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1})
, and hence
P(-)
is functorial.
Moreover,
P(\varphi)
depends continuously (in the
C^{\infty}
-topology) on
\varphi
, as it must cover
\varphi^{-1}
.
∎

4.3.
The space of prime markings

Recall the definition of the embedding space
\emb^{\widehat{=}}(N,Q)
in
2.16
. As a consequence of
4.3
, the spherical closure of
P(\Sigma\subset M)
is diffeomorphic to
P_{M}
and hence the embedding space
\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})
is non-empty.

Definition 4.6
.

A
prime marking
of
\Sigma\subset M
is an embedding
\iota\in\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})
.

As a direct application of
2.17
, we obtain the following corollary, stating that the space of prime markings is (in a suitable homotopical sense) a torsor over the diffeomorphism group of
P_{M}
.

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
\Sigma\subset M
be a sphere system with maximal prime piece
P(\Sigma\subset M)
and fix an embedding
\iota\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)\hookrightarrow P_{M}
such that
P_{M}\setminus\im(\iota)=\sqcup_{k}\smash{\mathring{D}}^{3}
.
Then the map

\displaystyle\Diff^{+}(P_{M})

\displaystyle\to\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi\circ\iota.

is a homotopy equivalence.
Moreover, the homotopy orbit space

\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})

is contractible.

Applying
2.17
to
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm{prime}}
, we similarly see that the map defined by acting with
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
on any point of
\emb^{\widehat{=}}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime},P_{M})
is an equivalence.
From this it follows that we also have an equivalence between mapping spaces.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\Sigma\subset M
be a sphere system, then the restriction map

\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})\to\emb^{\widehat{=}}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime},P_{M})

is a
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
-equivariant homotopy equivalence.

We are now ready to define the space
\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})
of
\Gamma
-separating systems with a prime marking.
Recall that for any separating system
\Sigma
, a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon M\rightarrow M
sends
\Sigma
to another separating system
\Upsilon=\varphi(\Sigma)
and induces an isomorphism
\varphi_{G}\colon G_{\Sigma}\rightarrow G_{\Upsilon}
.
Moreover,
\varphi
induces a diffeomorphism

\varphi_{P}\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)\longrightarrow P(\Upsilon\subset M),~(p,[\gamma])\mapsto(\varphi(p),[\varphi(\gamma)]),

which is the inverse of the diffeomorphism
P(\varphi)
induces by the functoriality of the maximal prime piece (
4.5
).
For
\Upsilon=\Sigma
this map gives a group homomorphism
\Diff(M,\Sigma)\to\Diff(P(\Sigma\subset M))
.

Definition 4.9
.

For a graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
we define

\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M}):=\{(\Sigma,\alpha,\iota)\;|\;(\Sigma,\alpha)\in\sep^{\Gamma}(M),~\iota\in\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})\}.

As
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
can be identified with the quotient of
\Diff^{+}(M)
by the free action of
\smash{\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
, we topologise
\smash{\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})}
via the bijection

\displaystyle\frac{\Diff^{+}(M)\times\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma_{0}\subset M),P_{M})}{\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma_{0})}(M,\Sigma_{0})}

\displaystyle\cong\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})

\displaystyle[\varphi,\iota\colon P(\Sigma_{0}\subset M)\hookrightarrow P_{M}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\varphi(\Sigma_{0}),\alpha_{0}\circ\varphi_{G}^{-1},\iota\circ\varphi_{P}^{-1})

for any choice of
(\Sigma_{0},\alpha_{0})\in\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
.
There is an action of
\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
on
\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})
via the formula

(\varphi,\psi)\colon(\Sigma,\alpha,\iota)\mapsto(\varphi(\Sigma),\alpha\circ\varphi_{G}^{-1},\psi\circ\iota\circ\varphi_{P}^{-1}),

for
(\varphi,\psi)\in\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
.

Functoriality of
\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})
now follows from
4.5
.

Definition 4.10
.

For a map of graphs
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
we obtain an induced map

\displaystyle f_{*}\colon\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\sep^{\Gamma^{\prime}}(M;P_{M})

\displaystyle(\Sigma,\alpha,\iota)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\Sigma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\iota\circ j),

where
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are defined as for
\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
in
3.14
and
j\colon P(\Sigma^{\prime}\subset M)\hookrightarrow P(\Sigma\subset M)
is the embedding induced by the functoriality of the maximal prime piece (
4.5
).

Remark 4.11
.

The action of
\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
on
\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})
by pre- and post-composition is compatible with the functoriality.

4.4.
The splitting map

Since
\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})
admits commuting actions by
\Diff^{+}(M)
and
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
, we will obtain a space with maps to both
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
and
B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
after taking homotopy quotients. This will be the bridge that allows us to define the splitting map
\mathbb{W}\colon B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\rightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
. We first show that the marking by the maximal prime piece in
\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})
can be cancelled by quotienting out the
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
-action.

Lemma 4.12
.

There is a functorial equivalence

\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\simeq\sep^{\Gamma}(M)

Proof.

The projection
\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})\to\sep^{\Gamma}(M)
that forgets the prime marking is functorial and invariant under the action of
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
, as this only acts on the marking.
Therefore we obtain a functorial map:

\rho\colon\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})\times_{\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}E\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\to\sep^{\Gamma}(M).

Writing out the left term in full, this map becomes

\rho\colon\Diff^{+}(M)\times_{\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)}\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})\times_{\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}E\Diff(P_{M})\to\sep^{\Gamma}(M).

By
3.19
,
\Diff^{+}(M)\rightarrow\sep^{\Gamma}(M)=\Diff^{+}(M)\times_{\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)}\{*\}
is a principal
\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)
-bundle. Therefore
\rho
is a locally trivial fibre bundle with fibre

\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})\times_{\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}E\Diff^{+}(P_{M}),

which is contractible by
4.7
. Hence
\rho
is an equivalence.
∎

We now construct a variant
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})
of
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
that incorporates a prime marking.

Definition 4.13
.

Define the functor
\mathcal{F}^{(-)}(P_{M})\colon\mathrm{Gr}(M)\to\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\text{-}\mathrm{Top}
by

\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M}):=\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(M).

This definition allows us to connect
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})
with the simpler, but non-functorial, definition
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Gamma)
that we considered at the start of the section.

Lemma 4.14
.

For any graph
\Gamma
there are
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
-equivariant equivalences

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})

\displaystyle\simeq\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)

\displaystyle\simeq\emb^{\widehat{=}}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime},P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)

where the action arises via the homomorphism

\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)\to\Diff^{+}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime})
.

Proof.

By definition
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})
is

\left(\Diff^{+}(M)\times_{\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M)}\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})\right)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(M).

Spelling out the homotopy orbit construction and commuting out the quotient by
\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M)
we get

\left(E\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}(M)\times\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma\subset M),P_{M})\right)\big/(\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M))

The two copies of
\Diff^{+}(M)
cancel and we get

\emb^{\widehat{=}}(P(\Sigma_{0}\subset M),P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M),

proving the first equivalence.
The second equivalence now follows from Corollary
4.8
.
∎

Since
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})
retains its
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
-action, we can further quotient by
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
to obtain
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
.

Lemma 4.15
.

There is a functorial equivalence
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\simeq\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
.

Proof.

Recall from
3.20
that
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)=\sep^{\Gamma}(M)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(M)
. Now applying
(-)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(M)
to Lemma
4.12
gives the desired result.
∎

Theorem
4.16
follows almost immediately from
4.15
.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
M
be a 3-manifold that is not diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times S^{2}
, and let
P_{M}
be the disjoint union of the irreducible factors of
M
.
Then there is a homotopy fibre sequence

\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{\mathbb{W}}B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M}).

Proof.

For any
G
-space
X
there is a fibre sequence:

X\longrightarrow X/\!\!/G\longrightarrow*/\!\!/G=BG.

Setting
X=\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})
and
G=\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
, all that remains is to identify
X/\!\!/G
with
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
.
For this we compute:

\displaystyle\left(\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})\right)/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})

\displaystyle\simeq\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\left(\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\simeq B\!\Diff^{+}(M).

Here the first step commutes the homotopy colimit with the homotopy quotient as in the proof of
3.24
(using
[
6
, Lemma 2.12(1) and (5)]
).
The second step is
4.15

and the third step is
3.24
.
∎

4.5.
A simplified colimit

We now rewrite the homotopy colimit that describes the fibre of
\mathbb{W}
in slightly different terms.
The key idea is that instead of
\mathrm{Gr}(M)
we want to use a different, more combinatorial category
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
of rank
g
graphs with
n
marked vertices.
This has two advantages:

•

The category
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
only depends on the number of
(S^{1}\times S^{2})
-prime factors and the number of irreducible prime factors – it does not depend on which prime factors appear nor their multiplicity.

•

The new functor
\mathcal{H}^{(-)}_{(-)}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\colon\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}\to\mathrm{Top}
over which we take the homotopy colimit takes values in connected spaces, whereas
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})
typically has several connected components, all of which are homeomorphic.

Notation 4.17
.

Throughout this section, fix integers
g,n\geq 0
and compact oriented irreducible
3
-manifolds
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
not diffeomorphic to
D^{3}
or
S^{3}
.
Write
M=P_{1}\sharp\dots\sharp P_{n}\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}
and
P_{M}:=P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}
.

Recall from
2.29
that an element
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is a connected rank
g
graph
\Gamma
with a marking
\sigma\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\hookrightarrow V_{\Gamma}
such that every non-marked vertex is at least trivalent.
We let
V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}\subset V_{\Gamma}
denote the set of unmarked vertices.
Given a choice of
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
, there is a functor

\lambda\colon\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}(M)

which sends
(\Gamma,\sigma)
to the graph
\Gamma
with the
3
-manifold labelling that assigns
[P_{i}]
to the vertex
\sigma(i)
and
[S^{3}]
to all unmarked vertices.

Definition 4.18
.

For
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
we let

\sep^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(M;P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\subseteq\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n})

denote the subspace of those
(\Sigma\subset M,\alpha\colon G_{\Sigma\subset M}\cong\Gamma,\iota\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)\hookrightarrow P_{M})
where
\iota
sends the component of
P(\Sigma\subset M)
that corresponds to
\alpha^{-1}(\sigma(i))
to
P_{i}
.
We further let

\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}):=\sep^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(M;P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\sslash\Diff^{+}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n})

denote the corresponding subspace.

Both
\sep^{\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}
were functors in
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
and these subspaces
\sep^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
are still functorial in the more restrictive category
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
.
The canonical inclusions of the subspaces yield a natural transformation.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}(M)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{-}(P_{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{-}_{-}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\Uparrow}

Now suppose we fix
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
, a graph labelled by diffeomorphism classes of irreducible manifolds.
Then we call an injection
\sigma\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\hookrightarrow V_{\Gamma}

compatible
if it sends
i
to a vertex labelled by the diffeomorphism class
[P_{i}]
.
By construction of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
, we can recover
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}
by taking the disjoint union over all choices of compatible
\sigma
.
That is, the combined map

\alpha^{\Gamma}=\amalg_{\sigma}\alpha^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}\colon\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\hookrightarrow V_{\Gamma}\\
\text{compatible}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\cong\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n})

is a homeomorphism.
This implies that our new functor still has the same homotopy colimit.

Lemma 4.19
.

The natural transformation
\alpha
induces a homeomorphism

\hocolim_{(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}}\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\cong\hocolim_{\Lambda\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{F}^{\Lambda}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}).

Proof.

For the purpose of this proof, it will be useful to think of an object of
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
as a graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
together with an compatible map
\sigma\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\to V_{\Gamma}
.
This only changes the category up to isomorphism, as the
3
-manifold marking on
\Gamma
is uniquely determined by
\sigma
.

Let
E_{\bullet}\to E_{\bullet}^{\prime}
be the map (induced by
\alpha
) between simplicial spaces that compute the two homotopy colimits as in
2.19
.
In simplicial degree
k
this map is

\coprod_{(\Gamma_{0},\sigma_{0})\to\dots\to(\Gamma_{k},\sigma_{k})}\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma_{0}}_{\sigma_{0}}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\longrightarrow\coprod_{\Gamma_{0}\to\dots\to\Gamma_{k}}\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma_{0}}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}).

In the chain of maps
(\Gamma_{0},\sigma_{0})\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{f_{k}}(\Gamma_{k},\sigma_{k})
the markings
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}
are completely determined by
\sigma_{0}
as we must have
\sigma_{i}=f_{i}\circ\sigma_{i-1}
.
Hence we can decompose the left term as

\coprod_{\Gamma_{0}\to\dots\to\Gamma_{k}}\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\hookrightarrow V_{\Gamma}\\
\text{compatible}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma_{0}}_{\sigma_{0}}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\longrightarrow\coprod_{\Gamma_{0}\to\dots\to\Gamma_{k}}\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma_{0}}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}),

which is a homeomorphism by the display above the lemma.
This shows that
E_{\bullet}\cong E_{\bullet}^{\prime}
and thus the homotopy colimits are homeomorphic.
∎

Remark 4.20
.

From a categorical perspective, the above lemma follows from the fact that
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}\to\mathrm{Gr}(M)
is (equivalent to) a left fibration whose fibre at
\Gamma
is the finite set of compatible marking of
\Gamma
.

Definition 4.21
.

For
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
as before and
g\geq 0
, we define the space of
genus
g

1
-handle attachments
to
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
to be the homotopy colimit

\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}):=\hocolim_{(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}}\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}).

Corollary 4.22
.

For every compact, connected, oriented 3-manifold
M
that is not diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times S^{2}
, there is a homotopy fibre sequence

\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{\mathbb{W}}B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n}).

4.6.
Geometric description of
\mathbb{W}
and
\pi_{1}
-surjectivity

Since the existence of
\mathbb{W}
in
4.16
was derived from the homotopy colimit, its construction may seem somewhat opaque. The purpose of this section is to provide a more geometric interpretation of
\mathbb{W}
, and give some applications to mapping class groups.

Our aim will be to explicitly describe the restriction of
\mathbb{W}
to
\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)
. Consider the chain of homomorphisms

\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\to\Diff^{+}(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)\rightarrow\Diff^{+}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime})

obtained by cutting along
\Sigma
and then restricting to the prime components. Smale’s theorem
[
47
]
tells us we can extend any fixed element of
\Diff^{+}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime})
, or indeed any
S^{d}
-family of such diffeomorphisms over
\sqcup_{k}D^{3}
to obtain a family of diffeomorphisms of
P_{M}
. Thus, we should expect to obtain a map on the level of classifying spaces:
B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\to B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
. To make this discussion more concrete, we introduce the following subgroup of
\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)
.

Definition 4.23
.

Fix a collar
C_{\Sigma}\cong[-1,1]\times\Sigma
of
\Sigma
, where
\Sigma
is identified with
\Sigma\times\{0\}
. If we equip
C_{\Sigma}
with the product of the standard Euclidean metric on
[-1,1]
and the round metric on each sphere
S\in\Sigma
, its orientation-preserving isometry group can be written as

(5)

\Isom^{+}(C_{\Sigma})=\left(\prod_{S\in\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}\Or(3)\right)\rtimes\Sym_{\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}=\Isom(\Sigma)

where
\Sym_{\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}
is the group of permutations of
\pi_{0}(\Sigma)
, and each orientation-reversing element of
\Or(3)
acts in the usual way on
S\in\pi_{0}(\Sigma)
but as
-1
on
[-1,1]
. Define
\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)\subset\Diff(M,\Sigma)
to be the subgroup of diffeomorphisms which agree with an element of
\Isom^{+}(C_{\Sigma})
on
C_{\Sigma}
.

Lemma 4.24
.

The inclusion
\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)\hookrightarrow\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)
is a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram of fibre sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Diff^{+}_{\Sigma}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Diff(\Sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Diff_{C_{\Sigma}}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Isom(\Sigma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Both the top and bottom horizontal sequences come from restriction to
\Sigma
, while the vertical maps are all inclusions. In the bottom sequence, we have identified the fibre with those diffeomorphisms that act trivially on
C_{\Sigma}
and the image with
\Isom(\Sigma)
via the isomorphism in
Eq.

5
.

By Smale’s theorem, the right hand vertical map is a homotopy equivalence. In general, neither
\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\to\Diff(\Sigma)
nor
\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)\to\Isom(\Sigma)
will be surjective since only those permutations induced by
\Aut(G_{\Sigma})
can be realised. Nevertheless, they both surject onto exactly the same components, and hence the middle vertical map will be a homotopy equivalence as long as the left hand vertical map is a homotopy equivalence.

To see this, let
\Coll_{\Sigma}:=\emb^{+}_{\Sigma\times\{0\}}(\Sigma\times[-1,1],M)
be the space of collars of
\Sigma
,
i.e.
the space of those orientation-preserving embeddings
\Sigma\times[-1,1]\hookrightarrow M
that are the identity on
\Sigma\cong\Sigma\times\{0\}
.
This space is contractible by a result of Cerf
[
8
, 5.2.1, Corollaire 1]
.
The action of
\Diff_{\Sigma}^{+}(M)
on
\Coll_{\Sigma}
is locally retractile and transitive, hence
by taking the stabiliser at some collar
C_{\Sigma}\in\Coll_{\Sigma}
we obtain a fibre sequence

\Diff_{C_{\Sigma}}(M)\hookrightarrow\Diff_{\Sigma}^{+}(M)\rightarrow\Coll_{C_{\Sigma}}(\Sigma).

where the base is contractible and hence the fibre inclusion is an equivalence, as desired.
∎

We now define a homomorphism
\Phi\colon\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)\to\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
by first cutting to get
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
, capping each
S^{2}
component of the boundary with a round
D^{3}
to get
\smash{\widehat{M\!\mid\!\Sigma}}
, then forgetting
S^{3}
components to get
P_{M}
. The fact that each element of
\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)
agrees with an element of
\Or(3)
in a neighbourhood of
\Sigma
guarantees that this extension is smooth.
See
Fig.

1
in the introduction.

Definition 4.25
.

We define the
cut-and-cap map

\kappa\colon B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\to B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
to be the composition of the equivalence
B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\simeq B\!\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)
from
4.24
with the induced map
B\Phi\colon B\!\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)\rightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
.

The next proposition gives a geometric interpretation to the restriction of the splitting map
\mathbb{W}
to
B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)
in terms of the capping map
\kappa
:

Proposition 4.26
.

The following diagram commutes up to homotopy

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\!\Diff^{+}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}}

Proof.

To define the splitting map, we introduced the intermediate space
\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})
which has an action of
\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
. From the construction of the homomorphism
\Phi
, we have a fixed
\Phi
-equivariant embedding
\iota_{0}\colon(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime}\rightarrow P_{M}
. By
4.14
, there are equivalences

\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)\simeq\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\simeq\emb^{\widehat{=}}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime},P_{M})/\!\!/(\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M})).

It follows that we get a homotopy commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)\simeq\ast/\!\!/\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\emb^{\widehat{=}}((M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime},P_{M})/\!\!/(\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ast/\!\!/\Diff^{+}(P_{M})=B\!\Diff(P_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\simeq\displaystyle\hocolim_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)}\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*/\!\!/\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}/\!\!/(\rm{Id}\times\Phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:rmC-simpler}}~\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}}

By definition, the equivalence
B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\simeq B\!\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)
followed by
B\Phi=\ast/\!\!/\Phi
in the diagram is
\kappa
, while going the other way around the diagram is the map
B\!\Diff^{+}(M,\Sigma)\rightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
induced by inclusion followed by
\mathbb{W}.

∎

Our main application of the previous proposition will be to mapping class groups.
Recall that for
N
an oriented manifold, the
mapping class group
of
N
is the group of components
\pi_{0}(\Diff^{+}(N))
.

Because
P_{M}
is a disjoint union, we can decompose
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
, and hence the mapping class group of
P_{M}
, as follows. Partition
P_{M}=(\sqcup^{n_{1}}Q_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup(\sqcup^{n_{k}}Q_{k})
where for
i\neq j
,
Q_{i}
is not orientation-preserving diffeomorphic to
Q_{j}
. This yields a product decomposition

(6)

\Diff^{+}(P_{M})\cong\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\left(\prod^{n_{1}}\Diff^{+}(Q_{i})\right)\rtimes\Sym_{n_{i}}\right),

and hence a corresponding decomposition of mapping class groups.

Since
\pi_{0}(\Diff^{+}(M))\cong\pi_{1}(B\!\Diff^{+}(M))
, Proposition
4.26
will imply the following surjectivity result for mapping class groups.

Corollary 4.27
.

The splitting map induces a surjection

\pi_{1}\mathbb{W}\colon\pi_{0}(\Diff^{+}(M))\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(\Diff^{+}(P_{M}))

from the mapping class group of
M
onto the mapping class group of
P_{M}
.

Proof.

Let
P_{M}=(\sqcup^{n_{1}}Q_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup(\sqcup^{n_{k}}Q_{k})
be as above. In each
Q_{i}
, choose a disc
D_{i}\cong D^{3}
as well as a collar
C_{i}\cong D^{3}
containing
D_{i}
. Consider the subgroup
H\leq\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
defined in terms of the decomposition in
Eq.

6
by

H=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\left(\prod^{n_{1}}\Diff_{C_{i}}^{+}(Q_{i})\right)\rtimes\Sym_{n_{i}}\right).

Since every diffeomorphism of
Q_{i}
can be isotoped to fix
C_{i}
pointwise, the inclusion of
H
into
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
is
\pi_{0}
-surjective.

Let
n=\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
and let
M_{0}=(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp^{g}}\setminus(\sqcup_{n}\smash{\mathring{D}}^{3}).
We can build
M
by attaching
n_{i}
copies of
Q_{i}\setminus\smash{\mathring{D}}_{i}
along boundary spheres of
M_{0}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. Let
\Sigma
be the separating system formed by a choice sphere in each
(S^{1}\times S^{2})
-factor together with the
n
spheres coming from the
\partial D_{i}
. In particular,
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)^{\rm prime}=\sqcup_{i=1}^{k}(\sqcup_{n_{i}}(Q_{i}\setminus\smash{\mathring{D}}_{i}))
.

Now choose a collar
C_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
that agrees with
C_{i}\setminus\smash{\mathring{D}}_{i}
on each prime component. With respect to
C_{\Sigma}
, we obtain a homomorphism
\Phi\colon\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)\rightarrow\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
. Let
\widehat{H}\leq\Diff^{\Or(3)}(M,\Sigma)
consist of those diffeomophisms that restrict to
C_{\Sigma}
as a permutation in
S_{\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}\leq\Isom^{+}(C_{\Sigma})
. Since every such permutation can be achieved,
\Phi
maps
\widehat{H}
surjectively onto
H
, and hence
\pi_{0}(\widehat{H})
surjects onto
\pi_{0}(\Diff^{+}(P_{M}))
. On the other hand, by
4.26
, the restriction of
\mathbb{W}
to
B\widehat{H}
agrees with
B\Phi
, so
\pi_{1}\mathbb{W}
is surjective.
∎

4.7.
The splitting map and boundary

In this subsection we construct variants of the splitting map that fix parts of the boundary of
M
.
All of the boundary of
M
comes from its prime pieces, so we have an identification
\partial M=\partial P_{M}
.
For every submanifold
A\subset\partial M=\partial P_{M}
we will construct a variant of the splitting map

\mathbb{W}_{A}\colon B\!\Diff_{A}(M)\longrightarrow B\!\Diff_{A}(P_{M})

and show that its homotopy fibre is equivalent to that of
\mathbb{W}
and can hence be described by the same homotopy colimit.
The key observation that allows us to do this is that
\mathbb{W}
commutes with restriction to the boundary in the following sense.

Proposition 4.28
.

There is a homotopy commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

where the vertical arrows restrict to the boundary and the bottom equality comes from the identification
\partial M=\partial P_{M}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be the space of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms from
\partial M
to
\partial P_{M}
.
Given our canonical identification
\partial M=\partial P_{M}
we can identify
\mathcal{D}
with either
\Diff^{+}(\partial M)
or
\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M})
.
However, it will be convenient to think of it not as a group but rather as a space with commuting actions of
\Diff^{+}(\partial M)
and
\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M})
.
For every
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
we have a
\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
-equivariant map

\delta\colon\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}

defined by restricting the maximal prime marking
\iota\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)\hookrightarrow P_{M}
to the diffeomorphism it induces between
\partial M\subset\partial P(\Sigma\subset M)
and
\partial P_{M}
.
Taking homotopy orbits by
\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
on the source and by
\Diff^{+}(\partial M)\times\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M})
on the target, we obtain a map (natural in
\Gamma
)

\delta^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})\sslash\Diff^{+}(P_{M})=\sep^{\Gamma}(M;P_{M})\sslash(\Diff^{+}(M)\times\Diff^{+}(P_{M}))\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}\sslash(\Diff^{+}(\partial M)\times\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M})).

Note that the target of this map is equivalent both to
*\sslash\Diff^{+}(\partial M)
and to
*\sslash\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M})
by sending
\mathcal{D}\to*
and projecting to one of the factors.
This
\delta^{\prime}
fits into a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}(P_{M})\sslash\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*\sslash\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*\sslash\Diff^{+}(\partial M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\sslash(\Diff^{+}(\partial M)\times\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*\sslash\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Taking homotopy colimits over
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(M)
the top row recovers the zig-zag that defines
\mathbb{W}
while the bottom row recovers the equivalence between
B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial M)
and
B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M})
.
∎

We let
\Diff^{+}(\partial M,[M])\leq\Diff^{+}(\partial M)
denote the subgroup of those diffeomorphisms that can be extended to the interior.
This is always a union of path components, as any isotopy on
\partial M
can be pushed to a collar of the boundary in
M
.
The advantage of this subgroup is that we have a homotopy fibre sequence

B\!\Diff^{+}_{\partial}(M)\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial M,[M]).

Note that a diffeomorphism of
\partial M=\partial P_{M}
extends to
M
if and only if it extends to
P_{M}
:
this is the claim that the vertical maps in
4.28
have the same image in
\pi_{1}
, which is indeed true as
\mathbb{W}
is
\pi_{1}
-surjective by
4.27
.
Therefore, the square from
4.28
lifts to a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial M,[M])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M},[P_{M}]).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

We can now use this modified square to obtain the variant
\mathbb{W}_{A}
of the splitting map where we fix a submanifold of the boundary.

Corollary 4.29
.

For any submanifold
A\subset\partial M
of the boundary, there is a variant
\mathbb{W}_{A}
of the splitting map for diffeomorphisms that fix
A
, and there is a map of homotopy fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{hofib}(\mathbb{W}_{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{A}^{+}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{A}^{+}(P_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}}

such that the map on fibres is an equivalence.

Proof.

Let
\Diff^{+}_{A}(\partial M,[M])\leq\Diff^{+}(\partial M)
denote the subgroup of those diffeomorphisms that fix
A
pointwise and also can be extended to the interior of
M
, and similarly for
P_{M}
.
Consider the cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}_{A}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{A}^{+}(\partial M,[M])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial M,[M])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}_{A}(P_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}_{A}(\partial P_{M},[P_{M}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(\partial P_{M},[P_{M}])}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}}

where the front face is obtained from
4.28
and the top and bottom faces are given by restrictions and subgroup inclusions.
Both the top face and the bottom face are homotopy pullback squares, as can be seen by comparing their horizontal homotopy fibers:
In the case of the top face both horizontal homotopy fibers are
B\!\Diff_{\partial}^{+}(M)
and for the bottom face both are
B\!\Diff_{\partial}^{+}(P_{M})
.
We therefore obtain the dashed map
\mathbb{W}_{A}
as the induced map on the homotopy pullbacks.

Finally, consider all the vertical homotopy fibres of the cube: these also must form a homotopy pullback square.
The homotopy fibres of the two vertical maps on the right are contractible, so it follows that the homotopy fibres of
\mathbb{W}_{A}
and
\mathbb{W}
are equivalent, as claimed.
∎

5.
Describing the fibre of the splitting map

The purpose of this section is to describe
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
, and thus the fibre of the splitting map, in more detail.
We will give a concrete formula for
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
as the homotopy quotient of an action of a compact Lie group on a product of framed configuration spaces.
For
n>0
we will see that this action is free and argue that the homotopy quotient is equivalent to the strict quotient, which admits a compactification as a manifold (with corners).
This will allow us to conclude that for
n>0
the fibre of the splitting map is homotopy finite.
For general
n
, we will also use the formula for
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
to exhibit it as a homotopy pullback and thus set up an explicit spectral sequence converging to its cohomology.

Given
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
and oriented irreducible 3-manifolds
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
, our goal will be to parametrise the minimal data necessary to prescribe a gluing of the
P_{i}
and copies of
S^{3}
in a pattern described by
(\Gamma,\sigma)
. We think of each vertex
v\in V_{\Gamma}
as being labelled by some
P_{i}
or
S^{3}
according to
\sigma
. Then for each edge
e\in E_{\Gamma}
, a gluing is specified by a choice of two points in the manifold corresponding to the endpoints of
e
, together with an orientation-reversing isomorphism between the tangent spaces at these chosen points.

We are thus led to define a space of framed configurations (
5.11
):

\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{i})\times\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\Conf_{H_{v}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(S^{3}),

where
\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{A}(M)
denotes the space of positively framed configurations of a finite set
A
in
M
, and for
v\in V_{\Gamma}
,
H_{v}
is the set of half-edges at
v
. This configuration space comes naturally equipped with an action of a group

\left(\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})\right)\ltimes\SO(\Gamma,\sigma),

where
\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)
is the compact Lie group
\smash{\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)\times\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4)}
.
(More generally, this extends to an action of
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\GL_{3}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\times\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\Diff^{+}(S^{3})
, but we act with the smaller group so that later we can restrict the action to orthogonal frames.)
The finite group
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
acts by permuting the framed points via their corresponding half-edges, the
\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
and
\SO(4)
-factors act on the framed points in each
P_{i}
and copy of
S^{3}
, respectively, and the
\SO(3)
-factors act by simultaneously rotating the framings at either end of an edge. Acting by
\SO(3)\simeq\Diff^{+}(S^{2})
identifies equivalent gluings along each edge, while acting by
\SO(4)\simeq\Diff^{+}(S^{3})
accounts for equivalent configurations in the
S^{3}
-factors. After modding out by the action of
\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)
, we obtain:

Proposition 5.1
.

For all
(\Gamma,\sigma)
and
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
as above,
there is an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
equivariant zig-zag of homotopy equivalences

\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\simeq\dots\simeq\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})/\!\!/\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)

Together with
4.22
this implies our main theorem.

Theorem 5.2
.

For every compact, connected, oriented 3-manifold
M
that is not diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times S^{2}
, there is a homotopy fibre sequence

\hocolim_{(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}}\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(M)\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{\mathbb{W}}B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup P_{n})

where for each
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
there is a homotopy equivalence

\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\simeq\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\sslash\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)\times\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4).

We will prove
5.1
in subsection
5.4
, but first we record some consequences.

Corollary 5.3
.

The space
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
is connected.

Proof.

The space of
positively
framed configurations on a connected oriented
3
-manifold is always connected and hence so is the homotopy quotient in
5.1
.
∎

This yields another proof of
4.27
.

Corollary 5.4
.

The fibre of the splitting map
\mathbb{W}
is connected, and thus
\pi_{1}\mathbb{W}
is surjective.

Proof.

By
4.16
and
4.19
the fibre of
\mathbb{W}
is a homotopy colimit, over
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
, of the spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
, which are connected by
5.3
.
Since
\pi_{0}
of a homotopy colimit is the colimit of the
\pi_{0}
’s, the connectedness of the fibre follows from the connectedness of
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
in
2.30
(iii).
Surjectivity of
\pi_{1}\mathbb{W}
then results from the long exact sequence of homotopy groups.
∎

It turns out that when
M
has at least one prime factor that is not
S^{1}\times S^{2}
,
i.e.
when there is at least one
P_{i}
, the configuration space model is particularly well-behaved. More precisely, we will show that the fibre of
\mathbb{W}
is homotopy finite in this case. This applies to the majority of 3-manifolds; indeed, the only cases not covered occur when
M=U_{g}=(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}
and hence
P_{M}=\emptyset
. However, in these cases the target of
\mathbb{W}
is a point, whence the fibre of the splitting map is just all of
B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{g})
. At the other extreme, when
g=0
and
n=1
,
M=P_{M}
is irreducible and the fibre of
\mathbb{W}
is a point, hence finite. For all remaining cases, we have:

Proposition 5.5
.

For
n>0
and
n+g>1
, the action in
5.11
is free and principal, and hence its strict quotient is equivalent to the homotopy quotient.
This strict quotient is homotopy equivalent to a compact manifold with corners of dimension at most
15(g-1)+12n
and thus to a finite cell complex.

As a consequence, we obtain the following finiteness result:

Corollary 5.6
.

For
n>0
and
n+g>1
,
the fibre
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
of the splitting map
\mathbb{W}
is homotopy equivalent to a finite cell complex of dimension at most
17(g-1)+13n
.

Proof.

According to
4.16
and
4.19
, the fibre of the
\mathbb{W}
can be written as a homotopy colimit of spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
over the finite EI-category
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
. The depth of
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
is
2(g-1)+n
by
2.30
(i), while the dimension of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})/\!\!/\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
is at most
15(g-1)+12n
by
5.1
and
5.5
. Combined with the estimate in
2.25
, this yields a bound of
17(g-1)+13n
for the dimension of the fibre.
∎

To illustrate this finiteness, consider the case where all of the
P_{i}
are hyperbolic manifolds.

Example 5.7
.

If
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
are hyperbolic manifolds, then
\Diff^{+}(P_{M})
is equivalent to the finite groups of isometries
\Isom(P_{M})
by work of Gabai
[
21
]
.
Therefore the “fibre inclusion” map from
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
to
B\!\Diff(M)
is equivalent to a finite cover with fibre
\Isom(P_{M})
.
Together with
5.6
, this shows that
B\!\Diff(M)
admits a finite cover that is equivalent to a finite CW complex of dimension at most
17(g-1)+13n
.

5.1.
Connected sums of two prime manifolds

To underscore the applicability of
5.1
, we explicitly describe the homotopy type of
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
in the two simplest situations where
n>0
,
i.e.
when
n=2,~g=0
and
n=1,~g=1
.
Applying
5.1
, in both of these cases the homotopy colimit is taken over one labelled graph, hence
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
is equivalent to one of the compact manifolds given in
5.5
.

Previously, Hatcher
[
26
,
28
]
proved that when
M=P_{1}\sharp P_{2}
, the space
\sep^{\nparallel}(M)
is contractible, which can be leveraged to get information about the homotopy type and compute cohomology of
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
in this case; for instance, see work of Lelkes
[
35
]
. In contrast, our description for
M=P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})
seems to be wholly new. We will also get a complete description of
B\!\Diff_{0}(M)
(and hence, after looping,
\Diff_{0}(M)
), provided that the irreducible factors are aspherical and not homeomorphic to
S^{1}\times D^{2}
. In this setting, we will extend some results of Kalliongis–McCullough
[
32
]
on
\pi_{2}(B\!\Diff_{0}(M))
in the case where
M
has two prime factors. For the
B\!\Diff_{0}(M)
computation, we will need the following lemma:

Lemma 5.8
.

If
P
is aspherical and not diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, then
P/\!\!/\Diff_{0}(P)
is aspherical
and its fundamental group is the quotient of
\pi_{1}(P)
by its centre.

Proof.

Since
P
is aspherical it is irreducible. By results of Hatcher
[
25
]
and Ivanov
[
31
]
in the Haken case, and Gabai
[
21
]
and McCullough–Soma
[
39
]
in the non-Haken case,
\Diff_{0}(P)\simeq\SO(2)^{r}
where
0\leq r\leq 3
is equal to the rank of the centre of
\pi_{1}(P)
(except when
P=S^{1}\times D^{2}
, which is in part why this is excluded).

Let
\pi=\pi_{1}(P)
and let
Z\cong\mathbb{Z}^{r}
denote the centre of
\pi
. If
r=0
, then
P/\!\!/\Diff_{0}(P)\simeq P
, which is aspherical by assumption. If
r>0
, then
P
is Seifert-fibred and there is a free
\SO(2)^{r}
-action on
P
. When
r=1
, apart from two cases, there is a unique fibring up to isotopy and
\SO(2)
acts by a full rotation of the generic fibre, which represents
Z\leq\pi
. The two exceptional cases occur when
P=K\widetilde{\times}I
, the twisted
I
-bundle over the Klein bottle, and its double
D(K\widetilde{\times}I)
. For
K\widetilde{\times}I
, the
\SO(2)
-action extends the
\SO(2)
-action on
K
, and represents a full rotation along the central element in
\pi_{1}(K)
. Similarly, for
D(K\widetilde{\times}I)
, the
\SO(2)
-actions on each
K\widetilde{\times}I
match along the boundary, hence glue up to an
\SO(2)
-action on the double which rotates along the common central elements (which have been identified) on both sides.
When
r=2
, the only possibility is
T^{2}\times I
and for
r=3
, the only possibility is
T^{3}
, with
\SO(2)^{r}
acting in the obvious way.

In each case, the
\SO(2)^{r}
-action is covered by a free and proper
\mathbb{R}^{r}
-action on the universal cover
\smash{\widetilde{P}}
, which commutes with the action of
\pi
and satisfies
\pi\cap\mathbb{R}^{r}=Z.
In particular, as
\widetilde{P}
is contractible, so is the quotient
\widetilde{F}:=\widetilde{P}/\mathbb{R}^{r}
, which inherits a properly discontinuous action by
\pi^{\prime}:=\pi/Z
. Therefore we obtain:

P/\!\!/\Diff_{0}(P)\simeq P/\!\!/\SO(2)^{r}\simeq\widetilde{P}/\!\!/(\mathbb{R}^{r}\cdot\pi)\simeq\widetilde{F}/\!\!/\pi^{\prime}\simeq*/\!\!/\pi^{\prime}=K(\pi^{\prime},1).\qed

Example 5.9

(
n=2
,
g=0
)
.

Up to labelled graph isomorphism,
\mathrm{Gr}_{2,0}
has exactly one graph
(\Gamma,\sigma)
, consisting of a single edge with both endpoints labelled. Thus,
\mathrm{Gr}_{2,0}
has no non-trivial morphisms, and in particular,
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)=\{1\}
. If
M=P_{1}\sharp P_{2}
, then, after choosing a framing on the
P_{i}
,

\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},P_{2})\simeq\Conf_{1}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{1})\times\Conf_{1}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{2})\simeq(P_{1}\times\SO(3))\times(P_{2}\times\SO(3)),

while
\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)\cong\SO(3)
, which acts diagonally on the two framings on the right.
Consequently, after choosing a framing on the
P_{i}
, we have:

\mathcal{H}_{0}(P_{1},P_{2})=\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},P_{2})\simeq\frac{\Conf_{1}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{1})\times\Conf_{1}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{2})}{\SO(3)}\\
\simeq P_{1}\times P_{2}\times\SO(3),

where the resulting
\SO(3)
-factor records the difference of the two framings. The prime decomposition fibre sequence is:

P_{1}\times P_{2}\times\SO(3)\hookrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sharp P_{2})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{W}}B\!\Diff^{+}(P_{1}\sqcup P_{2}),

which has fibre a compact 9-manifold.

Recall now that
B\!\Diff_{0}(M)
is the universal cover of
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
. By
5.1
, we can identify the intermediate cover of
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
corresponding to the surjection
\pi_{1}\mathbb{W}
with the homotopy quotient
(P_{1}\times P_{2}\times\SO(3))/\!\!/\Diff_{0}(P_{1}\sqcup P_{2})
. The projection onto the first two factors is equivariant with respect to the action of
\Diff_{0}(P_{1}\sqcup P_{2})\cong\Diff_{0}(P_{1})\times\Diff_{0}(P_{2})
, which yields a fibre sequence:

\SO(3)\hookrightarrow\frac{\underline{P_{1}\times P_{2}\times\SO(3)}}{\Diff_{0}(P_{1}\sqcup P_{2})}\rightarrow\frac{P_{1}}{\overline{\Diff_{0}(P_{1})}}\times\frac{P_{2}}{\overline{\Diff_{0}(P_{2})}}.

When the
P_{i}
are aspherical, and neither is diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times D^{2}
,
5.8
implies the base of this fibration is aspherical. Passing to universal covers, we see that
B\!\Diff_{0}(P_{1}\sharp P_{2})
is homotopy equivalent to the universal cover of
\SO(3)\cong\mathbb{RP}^{3}
, namely
S^{3}
.
In particular,
\Diff_{0}(P_{1}\sharp P_{2})\simeq\Omega S^{3}
.

1
(\Lambda,\rho)

1
(\Delta,\tau)

Figure 5
.
The two objects of
\mathrm{Gr}_{1,1}
are pictured. The graph
(\Lambda,\rho)
at the left has a redundant edge and admits an edge collapse onto the graph
(\Delta,\tau)
at the right, which has no redundant edges.

Example 5.10

(
n=1
,
g=1
)
.

There are only two labelled graphs
(\Lambda,\rho),(\Delta,\tau)
in
\mathrm{Gr}_{1,1}
(as pictured in Figure
5
), but only
(\Delta,\tau)
is in
\mathrm{Gr}_{1,1}^{\rm nr}
. In this case
\Aut(\Delta,\tau)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, which acts by inverting the loop. If
M=P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2})
, then:

\displaystyle\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Delta,\tau)}(P)=\Conf_{2}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P)

\displaystyle\simeq\Conf_{2}(P)\times\SO(3)\times\SO(3)

\displaystyle=\{((p_{1},p_{2}),\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\mid p_{1}\neq p_{2}\in P,~\theta_{i}\in\SO(3)\},

where
\SO(\Delta,\tau)\cong\SO(3)
again acts diagonally on the framings on the right. After quotienting by the action of
\SO(\Delta,\tau)
, we can write:

\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{2}(P)/\SO(\Delta,\tau)\simeq\Conf_{2}(P)\times\SO(3)=\{((p_{1},p_{2},\eta)\in\Conf_{2}(P)\times\SO(3)\},

where
\eta=\theta_{1}\circ\theta_{2}^{-1}
measures the difference between the two framings, and is invariant under the action of
\SO(\Delta,\tau)
by construction. The automorphism group
\mathbb{Z}/2
acts freely on
\Conf_{2}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P)
by exchanging points
p_{1}\leftrightarrow p_{2}
and frames
\theta_{1}\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}
, hence it acts in the same way (freely) on
\Conf_{2}(P)
but by
\eta\mapsto\eta^{-1}
on the
\SO(3)
-factor. Thus the prime decomposition fibre sequence is:

\frac{\Conf_{2}(P)\times\SO(3)}{\mathbb{Z}/2}\hookrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2}))\rightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(P).

This fibre is noncompact, so we now describe the construction of the compact manifold arising in
5.5
that is equivalent to the fibre. The configuration space
\Conf_{2}(P)
is the interior of a smooth 6-manifold with boundary, denoted by
\Conf_{2}[P]
, which can be obtained blowing up the diagonal of
P\times P
to a copy of the unit tangent bundle of
P
(identified with the unit normal bundle to the diagonal). The action of
\mathbb{Z}/2
extends to the blown up manifold by taking its derivative along the diagonal, which is the antipodal map on each sphere of the unit normal bundle. This action is clearly free on
\Conf_{2}(P)
as well as on the boundary of
\Conf_{2}[P]
, since the antipodal map acts freely on
S^{2}
. Thus the action of
\mathbb{Z}/2
is free on
\Conf_{2}[P]\times\SO(3)
and the quotient is a compact 9-manifold with boundary, homotopy equivalent to the fibre
\mathcal{H}_{1}(P)
.

For the computation of
B\!\Diff_{0}(M)
, we follow the same argument as in
5.9
. Now the cover of
B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
corresponding to
\pi_{1}\mathbb{W}
is the homotopy quotient of
\Conf_{2}(P)\times\SO(3)
by
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\Diff_{0}(P).

We can pass to the further 2-fold cover which just the quotient
(\Conf_{2}(P)\times\SO(3))/\!\!/\Diff_{0}(P).

Projecting onto the first of the two configuration points in
\Conf_{2}(P)
is
\Diff_{0}(P)
-equivariant, hence yields a fibre sequence:

(7)

(P\setminus*)\times\SO(3)\hookrightarrow\frac{\underline{\Conf_{2}(P)\times\SO(3)}}{\Diff_{0}(P)}\rightarrow\frac{P}{\overline{\Diff_{0}(P)}}.

Setting
\pi=\pi_{1}(P)
, the universal cover of
(P\setminus*)
is
\widetilde{P}\setminus\pi
, where
\pi
is identified with the orbit of a base-point in the interior of
\smash{\widetilde{P}}
under the deck group action. Assuming that
P
is aspherical and not
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, the base of the above fibre sequence is aspherical by
5.8
, hence passing to universal covers we obtain:

B\!\Diff_{0}(P\sharp(S^{1}\times S^{2}))\simeq\left(\widetilde{P}\setminus\pi\right)\times S^{3}\simeq\left(~\bigvee_{i=1}^{\infty}S^{2}\right)\times S^{3},

where the second equivalence comes from the fact that
\pi
is infinite and the interior of
\widetilde{P}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
It follows that
\pi_{2}B\!\Diff^{+}(M)=\pi_{2}B\!\Diff_{0}(M)=H_{2}(\widetilde{P}\setminus\pi)\cong\mathbb{Z}\pi
, regarded as a module over the integral group ring of
\pi=\pi_{1}(P\setminus*)
.
This recovers the result of
[
32
]
that
\pi_{2}
is infinitely generated in this case.
Note that, by comparison, we are able to calculate
\pi_{2}
exactly, and since
\pi=\pi_{1}(P\setminus*)
injects into the mapping class group
\pi_{1}B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
by
Eq.

7
, our computation additionally shows that
\pi_{2}B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
is finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}\pi_{1}B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
-module.
In fact, it is a cyclic
\mathbb{Z}\pi_{1}B\!\Diff^{+}(M)
-module.

5.2.
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
in terms of configuration spaces

Fix
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
and
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
irreducible manifolds that are neither
D^{3}
nor
S^{3}
. Via the labelling
\sigma
, we associate the oriented diffeomorphism class
[P_{i}]
to the vertex
\sigma(i)
. For each half-edge
h\in H_{\Gamma}
we set
P_{r(h)}=P_{i}
if
r(h)=\sigma(i)
and
P_{r(h)}=S^{3}
otherwise.

For an oriented 3-manifold
M
, let
\Fr^{+}(M)
denote the space of positively oriented frames on
M
. Thus,
\Fr^{+}(M)
is the total space of a principal
\GL_{3}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
-bundle
\pi\colon\Fr^{+}(M)\rightarrow M
. For a finite set
A
we define the configuration space of
A
in
M
to be

\Conf_{A}(M):=\left\{(p_{a})_{a\in A}\in\prod_{A}M\;\bigg|\;a\neq b\Rightarrow p_{a}\neq p_{b}\right\}.

We define the
space of positively framed configurations of the set
A
in
M
via the pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Conf_{A}^{\mathrm{fr}^{+}}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{A}\Fr^{+}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Conf_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{A}M}

where
\prod_{A}\Fr^{+}(M)\rightarrow\prod_{A}M
is the product bundle. Setwise,
\Conf_{A}^{\mathrm{fr}^{+}}(M)
may be described as follows:

\Conf_{A}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(M)=\left\{(p_{a},\theta_{a})_{a\in A}\in\prod_{A}\Fr(M)\;\bigg|\;a\neq b\Rightarrow p_{a}\neq p_{b}\text{ and }\theta_{a}\text{ is positive}\right\}.

We also write
\Conf_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(M)
when
A=\{1,\dots,n\}
, but it will usually be helpful to keep track of the name of the set we are embedding.
The wreath product
\SO(3)\wr\Aut(A)=\Map(A,\SO(3))\rtimes\Aut(A)
acts on this space by relabelling points and rotating framings.

Definition 5.11
.

For
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
and irreducible manifolds
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
as above we define

\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{i})\times\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\Conf_{H_{v}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(S^{3}).

A point in
\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
is a tuple
\mathbf{x}=(p_{h},\theta_{h})_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}
where
p_{h}\in P_{r(h)}
,
\theta_{h}
is a positive frame at
p_{h}
, and
p_{h}\neq p_{h^{\prime}}
for
h\neq h^{\prime}
. An element
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\gamma_{e})_{e\in E_{\Gamma}}\in\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)
acts on
\mathbf{x}
by pre-composition:

(\boldsymbol{\gamma}.\mathbf{x})_{h}=(p_{h},\theta_{h}\circ\gamma_{[h]}^{-1}),

where
[h]\in E_{\Gamma}
denotes the edge containing the half-edge
h
.
An element
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{v})_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\in\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4)
acts on
\mathbf{x}
via:

(\boldsymbol{\alpha}.\mathbf{x})_{h}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\alpha_{v}(p_{h}),D_{p_{h}}(\alpha_{v})\circ\theta_{h})&r(h)=v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}\\
(p_{h},\theta_{h}),&r(h)\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm prime}\end{array}\right.

where
D_{p_{h}}(\alpha_{v})\colon T_{p_{h}}S^{3}\rightarrow T_{\alpha_{v}(p_{h})}S^{3}
is the derivative. In particular,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
acts trivially on framed points in the
P_{i}
.
Since
\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4)
acts by post-composition, it commutes with the action of
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)
.
We therefore obtain an action of their product which we denote by:

\SO(\Gamma,\sigma):=\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)\times\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4).

Lastly, an element
\varphi\in\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
permutes the half-edges
H_{\Gamma}
and therefore acts on
\mathbf{x}
by

(\varphi.\mathbf{x})_{h}=(p_{\varphi(h)},\theta_{\varphi(h)}).

Since the action of
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
preserves both
E_{\Gamma}
and
V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}
, it normalises the actions of
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)
and
\prod_{V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4)
. Explicitly, we compute that

(\varphi\circ\boldsymbol{\gamma}\circ\varphi^{-1})_{e}=\gamma_{\varphi(e)}~~\text{ and }~~(\varphi\circ\boldsymbol{\alpha}\circ\varphi^{-1})_{v}=\alpha_{\varphi(v)}.

In summary, we obtain a group action

\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\ltimes\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)\curvearrowright\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}).

Moreover, this action commutes with the
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
-action on
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{i})
.

Remark 5.12
.

Regarding
S^{3}
as the unit quaternions, the left action of
S^{3}
on itself induces a faithful representation
\ell\colon S^{3}\hookrightarrow\SO(4)
,
p\mapsto\ell_{p}
.
Equip
S^{3}
with the Lie group framing.
Identifying the
\SO(4)
-stabiliser of
1\in S^{3}
with
\SO(3)
, an explicit formula for the derivative of
\alpha\in\SO(4)
at a point
p\in S^{3}
is given by:

D_{p}(\alpha)=\ell_{\alpha(p)}^{-1}\circ\alpha\circ\ell_{p}.

Note that for example that
D_{p}(\ell_{q})=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
as we used the left-multiplication to identify the tangent spaces with
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

We will break up the proof of
5.5
into several steps.

Lemma 5.13
.

For
n>0
, the action of
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\ltimes\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)
on
\Conf_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
is free.

Proof.

Using the semi-direct product decomposition, any element
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\ltimes\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)
can be written uniquely as
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\varphi;\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, where
\varphi\in\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\gamma_{e})_{e\in E_{\Gamma}}
,
and
\smash{\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{v})_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}}
. Suppose
\mathbf{x}=(p_{h},\theta_{h})_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}
is a point in
\smash{\Conf_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})}
such that
\boldsymbol{\mu}.\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}
. We will show that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is trivial.

First, let
v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm prime}
, and let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{d}
be the half-edges based at
v
. By definition,
\varphi(v)=v
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
acts trivially on
p_{h_{j}}
for
1\leq j\leq d
. Since
p_{h_{1}},\ldots,p_{h_{d}}
are pairwise distinct, we must have
\varphi(h_{j})=h_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq d
. Lastly, since
\gamma_{[h]}
acts freely on
\theta_{h}
for each
h\in H_{\Gamma}
, we see that
\gamma_{e_{j}}=1
for the edges
e_{j}=[h_{j}]
incident at
v
.

Set
V_{0}=V_{\Gamma}^{\rm prime}
and for
k\geq 1
, inductively define
V_{k}=\{v\in V_{\Gamma}\mid\textrm{dist}_{\Gamma}(v,V_{k-1})\leq 1\}
. We will show, by induction on
k
, that for each
v\in V_{k}
and each half-edge
h
based at
v
:

(1)

\varphi(v)=v
and
\varphi(h)=h
,

(2)

\alpha_{v}=1
and
\gamma_{[h]}=1.

The base case
k=0
of this induction was proved in the previous paragraph. For the inductive step, let
v\in V_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
. In particular,
v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}
. Let
h_{0}
be a half-edge at
v
such that
w=r(h_{0}^{\dagger})\in V_{k-1}
.
Since
\varphi(w)=w
and
\varphi(h_{0}^{\dagger})=h_{0}^{\dagger}
, we see that
\varphi(v)=v
and
\varphi(h_{0})=h_{0}
.
Because
\varphi
fixes
h_{0}
we must have
\alpha_{v}(p_{h_{0}})=p_{h_{0}}
.
But the
\SO(4)
-stabiliser of
p_{h_{0}}
acts freely on frames, so we get that
D_{p_{h_{0}}}(\alpha_{v})=1
as well. Since
\alpha_{v}
fixes the point
p_{h_{0}}\in S^{3}
and the frame
\theta_{h_{0}}
at
p_{h_{0}}
, it must be the identity. It follows that for each half-edge
h
based at
v
we have
(\boldsymbol{\mu}.p)_{h}=p_{\varphi(h)}
, and since the points
p_{h}
are pairwise distinct, we conclude that
\varphi(h)=h
for every
h
based at
v
.

We have thus proved (1) and the first half of (2) holds at
v
. Then for each half-edge
h
based at
v
, we have:

(\boldsymbol{\mu}.\mathbf{x})_{h}=(p_{h},\theta_{h}\circ\gamma_{[h]}^{-1}).

Again using that
\gamma_{[h]}
acts freely on
\theta_{h}
, we obtain that
\gamma_{[h]}=1
, which proves the second half of (2) and completes the inductive step.
Since
\Gamma
is finite and connected,
V_{\Gamma}=V_{R}
for some
R\leq\textrm{diam}(\Gamma)
, and thus we have proved that
\boldsymbol{\mu}=1
when
k=R
.
∎

Definition 5.14
.

For a finite set
A
and an oriented 3-manifold
M
equipped with a Riemannian metric define the subspace
\Conf_{A}^{\SO}(M)\subset\Conf_{A}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(M)
to be

\Conf_{A}^{\SO}(M)=\{(p_{a},\theta_{a})\in\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{A}(M)\mid\theta_{a}\text{ is an isometry}\},

meaning that each
\theta_{a}
defines an orthonormal frame with respect to the metric.
We then obtain a corresponding subspace of
\Conf^{\mathrm{fr}+}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
:

\Conf^{\SO}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots P_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}^{\SO}(P_{i})\times\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\Conf_{H_{v}}^{\SO}(S^{3}).

The action of
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\ltimes\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)
preserves
\Conf^{\SO}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
and there is a strong
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\ltimes\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)
-equivariant deformation retraction of
\Conf_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
onto
\Conf^{\SO}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
,
i.e.

\Conf_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})\simeq\Conf^{\SO}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
.

The framed configuration space
\Conf_{A}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(M)
is the total space of a principal
\prod_{A}\GL_{3}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
-bundle over
\Conf_{A}(M)
. By replacing each
\GL_{3}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
with
\SO(3)
, we have at least made the fibres of this bundle compact. However, the base, being a configuration space, is still fundamentally noncompact.

Recall that
\Conf_{A}(M)
embeds as the interior of a compact manifold with corners
\Conf_{A}[M]
known as the
Fulton–MacPherson compactification
. Roughly speaking, a point in the boundary of
\Conf_{A}[M]
corresponds to a sequence of points in
\Conf_{A}(M)
which limit to the big diagonal in
\prod_{A}M
. Each boundary stratum keeps track of the relative rate and tangent direction at which these points collide. For more details and properties of this construction we refer the reader to Sinha’s article
[
46
]
. For us, the most relevant properties will be:

(1)

\Conf_{A}[M]
is a compact manifold with corners and
\Conf_{A}(M)\hookrightarrow\Conf_{A}[M]
embeds as its interior. In particular, this inclusion is a homotopy equivalence
[
46
, Theorem 4.4 and Corollary 4.5]
.

(2)

There is a quotient map
\rho\colon\Conf_{A}[M]\rightarrow\prod_{A}M
which restricts to a homeomorphism on
\Conf_{A}(M)
(this follows directly from
[
46
, Definition 1.3]
).

(3)

\Conf_{A}[M]
is functorial in the sense that if
f\colon M\rightarrow N
is an embedding, then there is an embedding
\Conf_{A}[M]\rightarrow\Conf_{A}[N]
. In particular,
\Diff(M)
acts on
\Conf_{A}[M]
by diffeomorphisms
[
46
, Corollary 4.9]
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
(M,g)
be an oriented Riemannian 3-manifold. For any finite set
A
