, there is a compactification of
\Conf_{A}^{\SO}(M)
as the interior of a manifold with corners
\Conf^{\SO}_{A}[M]
that fits into a composition of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Conf_{A}^{\SO}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Conf^{\SO}_{A}[M]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{A}\Fr^{\SO}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Conf_{A}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Conf_{A}[M]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{A}M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

In particular,
\pi\colon\Conf_{A}^{\SO}[M]\rightarrow\Conf_{A}[M]
is a principal
\prod_{A}\SO(3)
-bundle over
\Conf_{A}[M]
. Moreover, the action of
\Isom^{+}(M,g)
on
\Conf^{\SO}_{A}(M)
extends smoothly to
\Conf^{\SO}_{A}[M]
and acts equivariantly on the diagram.

Proof.

Let
j\colon\Conf_{A}(M)\hookrightarrow\Conf_{A}[M]
be one inclusion, and let
i\colon\Conf_{A}(M)\hookrightarrow\prod_{A}M
be the other. By property (ii) above, there is a natural projection
\rho\colon\Conf_{A}[M]\rightarrow\prod_{A}M
which restricts to a homeomorphism on the interior, hence yields a factorisation
i=\rho\circ j
. We can regard
\Conf_{A}^{\SO}(M)
as the pullback of the principal
\prod_{A}\SO(3)
-bundle
\pi\colon\prod_{A}\Fr^{\SO}(M)\rightarrow\prod_{A}M
along
i
, where
\Fr^{\SO}(M)
is the space of positive orthonormal frames on
M
. Thus if we define
\Conf_{A}^{\SO}[M]
as the pullback of
\pi
along
\rho
, then we obtain a composition of pullback squares as in the statement of the proposition.
In particular,
\Conf_{A}^{\SO}[M]
is a compact manifold with corners whose interior is
\Conf_{A}^{\SO}(M)
, and
\pi\colon\Conf_{A}^{\SO}[M]\rightarrow\Conf_{A}[M]
is a principal
\prod_{A}\SO(3)
-bundle. By property (iii), the action of
\Diff^{+}(M)
, and hence
\Isom^{+}(M,g)
, extends to
\Conf_{A}[M]
. On the other hand, the
\Isom^{+}(M,g)
action on
\Conf_{A}(M)
also lifts to
\Conf_{A}^{\SO}(M)
, acting on fibres via the derivative map. By continuity we obtain a smooth extension to
\Conf_{A}^{\SO}[M]
making the left hand square
\Isom^{+}(M,g)
-equivariant. Equivariance of the outer square is clear, so the proposition follows.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

Since
\pi\colon\Conf_{A}^{\SO}[M]\rightarrow\Conf_{A}[M]
is a principal
\prod_{A}\SO(3)
-bundle, it comes with a free right action of
\prod_{A}\SO(3)
.

The last ingredient we will need to prove
5.5
is the following general fact about actions of compact Lie groups on manifolds with corners.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group acting smoothly on a compact manifold with corners
M
.
If
G
acts freely on the interior of
M
, then
G
acts freely on
M
and the quotient map
M\to M/G
is a principal
G
-bundle.
Moreover,
M/G
is a compact manifold with corners and hence both it and
M\sslash G
are homotopy finite.

Proof.

Since
G
is compact and
M
is Hausdorff, the action is automatically proper (see
e.g.

[
4
, Ch.III.4, Proposition 2]
. We first show that the action of
G
is free on all of
M
. Suppose
\xi\in\partial M
is a corner of dimension
k
, and let
G_{\xi}
be the stabiliser of
\xi
. By
[
1
, Proposition 5.1]
, there exists a chart
U\ni\xi
diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n,k}
on which the action of
G_{\xi}
is linear, and in fact orthogonal. Thus, on
U
, the action of
G_{\xi}
preserves the
k
-simplex of inward pointing unit vectors. In particular,
G_{\xi}
fixes the ray corresponding to the barycenter of this simplex pointwise. Since these points are interior, we conclude that
G_{\xi}=\{1\}
. Thus the action of
G
on
M
is free and proper.

Since
G
is compact, it has only finitely many connected components. Let
G_{0}
be the connected component of the identity. We claim that
G_{0}
satisfies the conclusion of the theorem. Indeed, as
G_{0}
is connected, the action of
G_{0}
on
M
is boundary-intersection free in the sense of
[
1
, Definition 1.4]
. Let
\xi\in M
be a corner of dimension
k
. We apply the tube theorem
[
1
, Proposition 5.3]
with
G_{\xi}=\{1\}
to find a
G_{0}
-invariant neighbourhood
U
of
\xi
, an
m
-submanifold with corners
V^{+}\cong\mathbb{R}^{m,k}
(for
m=n-\dim(G_{0})
) containing
\xi
with trivial
G_{0}
-stabiliser and a
G_{0}
-equivariant diffeomorphism
\psi\colon G_{0}\times V^{+}\rightarrow U
. This provides a local trivialisation for the quotient map
q_{0}\colon M\rightarrow M_{0}:=M/G_{0}
in a neighbourhood of
q_{0}(\xi)
, which maps
V^{+}
diffeomorphically to
M_{0}
. Since
\xi
was arbitrary,
M_{0}
has the structure of a smooth manifold with corners and
q_{0}
is a principal
G_{0}
-bundle.

To finish the proof, observe that
M_{0}
inherits a free action of the finite group
F:=G/G_{0}
. Since
M_{0}
is Hausdorff, the quotient
q_{1}\colon M_{0}\rightarrow M_{1}=M_{0}/F
is automatically a covering action. Therefore
M_{1}
is also a manifold with corners and composition
q=q_{1}\circ q_{0}\colon M\rightarrow M_{1}
is a principal
G
-bundle, as required.
∎

Proof of
5.5
.

By compactifying each factor in the product, we obtain a homotopy equivalent compactification of
\Conf^{\SO}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
as:

\Conf^{\SO}_{(\Gamma,\sigma)}[P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}]:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Conf^{\SO}_{H_{\sigma(i)}}[P_{i}]\times\prod_{v\in V^{\rm sph}_{\Gamma}}\Conf^{\SO}_{H_{v}}[S^{3}],

which is a smooth manifold with corners of dimension

6\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|H_{\sigma(i)}|+\sum_{v\in V^{\rm sph}_{\Gamma}}|H_{v}|\right)=12|E_{\Gamma}|.

By
5.15
and
5.16
for
\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)
and by
[
46
, Theorem 4.10]
for
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
, the action of
\Aut(\Gamma,\Sigma)\ltimes\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)
extends smoothly to this compactification. By
5.13
and
5.17
, the extension is free and principal, hence the quotient is a compact manifold with corners. In particular, the homotopy quotient is equivalent to the strict quotient, which, being a homeomorphic to a compact manifold with boundary, has the homotopy type of a finite CW complex.

Recalling that
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
is discrete and that as a Lie group there is an isomorphism
\SO(\Gamma,\sigma)\cong\prod_{e\in E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)\times\prod_{v\in V^{\rm sph}_{\Gamma}}\SO(4)
, the quotient has dimension:

\displaystyle 12|E_{\Gamma}|-(3|E_{\Gamma}|+6|V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}|)

\displaystyle=9|E_{\Gamma}|-6|V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}|

\displaystyle=9|E_{\Gamma}|-6|V_{\Gamma}|+6n

\displaystyle=9|E_{\Gamma}|-6(|E_{\Gamma}|-(g-1))+6n

\displaystyle=3|E_{\Gamma}|+6(g-1)+6n,

where we have used that
1-g=\chi(\Gamma)=|V_{\Gamma}|-|E_{\Gamma}|
on the third line. The maximal number of edges
\Gamma
can have is
3(g-1)+2n
by
2.30
(i), hence the dimension of the quotient is at most
15(g-1)+12n
, as claimed.
∎

5.3.
Thickened separating systems

To prove
5.1
and certain other descriptions of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
, we will construct a map that roughly takes a separating system
\Sigma\subset M
and sends it to the various path components of
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
.
In defining this on the level of moduli spaces we run into a technical issue:
points in
B\!\Diff
can be described as unparametrised submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, but if
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, then there is no canonical way for us to think of
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
as a submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
.
We will resolve this by remembering for each separating system
\Sigma\subset M
a parametrised tubular neighbourhood
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}
of
\Sigma
in
M
, so that instead of
M\!\mid\!\Sigma
we can simply take the diffeomorphic manifold
M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}
that is literally a submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
.
This is a purely technical point and will not really affect any of our arguments.

Definition 5.18
.

A
thickened separating system
is a separating system
\Sigma
together with an equivalence class of embeddings
i\colon\Sigma\times[-1,1]\hookrightarrow M
satisfying
i(x,0)=x
for all
x\in\Sigma
.
Here we consider two embeddings equivalent if they differ by flipping the
[-1,1]
factor for some of the components of
\Sigma
.
We let
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\circ}:=i(\Sigma\times(-1,1))\subset M
and
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}:=i(\Sigma\times[-1,1])
denote the open and closed tubular neighbourhoods obtained from the thickening.
By abuse of notation, we often write
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}
to denote the entire datum
[\Sigma,i]
.

Definition 5.19
.

A diffeomorphism
\psi\in\Diff^{+}(\Sigma\times[-1,1])
is called
cylindrical
if
\psi(x,t)=(\psi_{0}(x),t)
for some
\psi_{0}\in\Diff^{+}(\Sigma)
.
We denote the subgroup of cylindrical diffeomorphisms of
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\cong\Sigma\times[-1,1]
by
\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
.
Given a thickened separating system
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M
, we let

\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})\leq\Diff(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})

denote the subgroup of those diffeomorphisms that are cylindrical on
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}
,
i.e.
those diffeomorphisms such that
i^{-1}\circ\varphi_{|\Sigma^{\varepsilon}}\circ i
is cylindrical. In particular,
\varphi(\Sigma)=\Sigma.

That is, we take those
\varphi\colon M\cong M
such that
\varphi(\Sigma)=\Sigma
and
\varphi(i(x,t))=i(\varphi(x),t)
holds for
i
part of the thickening.
(Note that it does not matter which
i
we pick from the equivalence class of embeddings
\Sigma\times[-1,1]\hookrightarrow M
.)

Lemma 5.20
.

The subgroup inclusion
\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})\subset\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)
is admissible and a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

As in the proof of
4.24
we let
\Coll_{\Sigma}:=\emb^{+}_{\Sigma\times\{0\}}(\Sigma\times[-1,1],M)
be the (contractible) space of orientation-preserving collars of
\Sigma
.
The group
\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)
acts on
\Coll(\Sigma)
by

\varphi.\iota:=\varphi\circ\iota\circ(\varphi_{|\Sigma}^{-1}\times\mathrm{id}_{[-1,1]}).

Note this indeed preserves the condition that
\iota_{|\Sigma}=\mathrm{id}_{\Sigma}
.
Restricting to the subgroup
\Diff^{+}_{\Sigma}(M)
this action becomes the usual action by post-composition, which we know to be locally retractile.
Using the same local retraction it follows that the action of the larger group
\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)
is also locally retractile.
The stabiliser of this action at some point
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\in\Coll(\Sigma)
is exactly the group of cylindrical diffeomorphisms, so this subgroup is admissible by
2.4
.
Moreover, we have a fibre sequence

\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})\longrightarrow\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}(M,\Sigma)\longrightarrow\Coll(\Sigma)

and as the base is contractible it follows that the subgroup inclusion is an equivalence.
∎

From
5.20
and
2.4
we can deduce that the restriction of a principal
\Diff^{+}(M)
-action to
\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
is always principal.

Corollary 5.21
.

The action of
\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
on
\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
is principal.

We use the thickened separating systems to define a variant
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
.
It will be convenient to define this variant directly rather than as a homotopy orbit construction of a variant of
\sep^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(M)
, as this removes the reference to
M
and also makes it easier to describe the
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-action.

Notation 5.22
.

Consider a separating system
\Sigma\subset M
with dual graph
G_{\Sigma\subset M}
.
For every vertex
v
of
G_{\Sigma\subset M}
we let
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)_{v}\subset M\!\mid\!\Sigma
denote the component corresponding to
v
.
If the vertex
v
is such that
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)_{v}
is not a punctured sphere, we also write
P(\Sigma\subset M)_{v}\subset P(\Sigma\subset M)
for the component of the maximal prime piece that contains
(M\!\mid\!\Sigma)_{v}
.

Definition 5.23
.

A point in
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
consists of the data

•

N\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
a compact, oriented,
3
-dimensional submanifold,

•

\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset N
a thickened separating system,

•

\alpha\colon\Gamma\cong G_{\Sigma\subset N}
an isomorphism of graphs such that for every vertex
v\in V_{\Gamma}
the component

(N\!\mid\!\Sigma)_{\alpha(v)}
is abstractly diffeomorphic to
S^{3}\setminus\amalg_{k}\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}}
if
v
is unmarked and to
P_{i}\setminus\amalg_{k}\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}}
if
v=\sigma(i)
.

•

an embedding
\iota\colon P(\Sigma\subset N)\hookrightarrow\coprod_{i=1}^{n}P_{i}
sending the component
P(\Sigma\subset N)_{\alpha(\sigma(i))}
to
P_{i}
.

If we pick a thickened separating system
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M
and a graph isomorphism
\alpha\colon G_{\Sigma\subset M}\cong\Gamma
, then we can parametrise this space via the map

\displaystyle q\colon\frac{\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb(P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i},P_{i})}{\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{\cong}\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})

\displaystyle(j\colon M\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\infty},(\iota_{i})_{i=1}^{n})

\displaystyle\longmapsto(j(M),j(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}),\alpha\circ j_{G}^{-1},(\sqcup_{i=1}^{n}\iota_{i})\circ j_{P}^{-1})

where
j_{G}\colon G_{\Sigma\subset M}\cong G_{j(\Sigma)\subset j(M)}
is the graph isomorphism induced by
j
and
j_{P}\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)\cong P(j(\Sigma)\subset j(M))
is the diffeomorphism induced on maximal prime pieces.

Lemma 5.24
.

The map
q
is a bijection and the topology it induces on
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
is independent of the choice of
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M
and
\alpha
.

Proof.

For surjectivity, let
(N,\Upsilon^{\varepsilon},\beta,\nu)\in\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
.
The composite
\beta\circ\alpha^{-1}
defines an isomorphism of dual graphs
G_{\Sigma\subset M}\cong G_{\Upsilon\subset N}
and hence
3.11
constructs a diffeomorphism
\psi\colon M\cong N
with
\psi(\Sigma)=\Upsilon
such that the induced map on dual graphs is exactly
\beta\circ\alpha^{-1}
.
In fact, the proof of
3.11
constructs
\psi
such that it preserves the thickenings.
Then we have

q(\psi\colon M\cong N\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\nu\circ\psi_{P})=(\psi(M),\psi(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}),\alpha\circ\psi_{G}^{-1},(\nu\circ\psi_{P})\circ\psi_{P}^{-1})=(N,\Upsilon^{\varepsilon},\beta,\nu),

which shows that
q
is surjective.
Similarly, we see that the quotient topology does not depend on the choice of
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M
, as any other choice differs by a diffeomorphism that preserves the thickened separating system.

Two pairs
(j,\iota)
and
(j^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
map to the same point in
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
if and only if
j(M)=j^{\prime}(M)
, and the thickened sphere systems, identification of dual graphs, and the maximal prime marking agree.
Hence we can find a diffeomorphism
\varphi\in\Diff^{+}(M)
with
j^{\prime}=j\circ\varphi
.
This diffeomorphism automatically lies in
\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
because it must preserve the thickened sphere systems and identification of dual graphs.
Moreover,
\iota\circ j_{P}^{-1}=\iota^{\prime}\circ(j^{\prime})_{P}^{-1}
implies that
\iota\circ\varphi_{P}=\iota^{\prime}
, so we have
(j^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})=(j\circ\varphi,\iota\circ\varphi_{P})
.
This shows that
q
is a bijection.
∎

The groups
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
and
\prod_{i}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
act on the space
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
by post-composition on the graph isomorphism and the maximal prime embedding, respectively.
The map

(8)

\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})

that forgets the thickening, sending
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}
to
\Sigma
, is equivariant for the action of these groups.

Corollary 5.25
.

The map in
Eq.

8
is a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

By choosing some
(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon},\alpha)
we can write this map as

\frac{\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb(P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i},P_{i})}{\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})}\longrightarrow\frac{\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb(P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i},P_{i})}{\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(M,\Sigma)}.

On both sides the action of the group on the numerator is principal:
on the left this follows from
5.21
and on the right it follows from
3.19
.
The claim now follows from
2.6
and
5.20
.
∎

5.4.
Proving the configuration space description

We will now construct the zig-zag of equivalences between
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
and the configuration space model, as promised in
5.1
.

We will not be able to directly map from
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
to the configuration space model, but if we construct more flexible models for the configuration spaces, we will be able to map into those.
Instead of considering configurations of points, we will consider configurations of
2
-spheres that can be filled, which will give an equivalent model via Hatcher’s proof of the Smale conjecture
[
27
]
.

Definition 5.26
.

For a finite set
A
and an irreducible
3
-manifold
P
we let

\emb^{\rm D}(A\times S^{2},P)\subset\emb(A\times S^{2},P)

denote the subspace of those embeddings
\iota\colon A\times S^{2}\hookrightarrow P
that extend to an
orientation preserving
embedding of
A\times D^{3}
.
Since
P
is irreducible we already know that each of the
\iota_{a}(S^{2})
bounds a ball, but the above condition requires that it bounds an oriented ball with respect to the preferred orientation on
S^{2}
.

Lemma 5.27
.

For any
A
and
P
as above, there is an
\Aut(A)\wr\SO(3)
and
\Diff^{+}(P)
equivariant zig-zag of equivalences

\emb^{\rm D}(A\times S^{2},P)\xleftarrow{\simeq}\emb^{+}(A\times D^{3},P)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Conf_{A}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P).

Proof.

The left map is surjective by definition of
\emb^{\rm D}(\dots)
in
5.26
.
It is a Serre fibration by
[
41
,
8
]
and its fibres are
\Diff_{\partial}(A\times D^{3})
, which is contractible by the Smale conjecture
[
27
]
.
The right map is obtained by taking the derivative at the centre of each disc and is an equivalence by a standard argument: we can construct a homotopy inverse by choosing a metric and using exponential maps. Equivariance is clear by inspection.
∎

We will also need a specific model for the moduli space of punctured
3
-spheres.

Definition 5.28
.

For
A
a finite set we define
B\!\Diff_{A}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})
to be the space of pairs
(W,\psi)
of
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
a compact oriented manifold abstractly diffeomorphic a
3
-sphere with open balls removed and
\psi\colon\partial W\cong A\times S^{2}
an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism.
To topologise this, we pick an embedding
\iota\colon A\times D^{3}\hookrightarrow S^{3}
, and require that the canonical bijection

B\!\Diff_{A}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})\cong\frac{\emb(S^{3}\setminus\iota(A\times\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}}),\mathbb{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff_{\partial}(S^{3}\setminus\iota(A\times(D^{3})^{\circ}))}

be a homeomorphism.

By construction,
B\!\Diff_{A}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})
is equivalent to
B\!\Diff_{\partial}(S^{3}\setminus\iota(A\times\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}}))
, but we chose the above construction to make it independent of a choice of embedding
\iota
and to make explicit the action

\Diff^{+}(A\times S^{2})=\Aut(A)\ltimes\Map(A,\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))\curvearrowright B\!\Diff_{A}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})

by changing the parametrisation.
Writing
V:=S^{3}\setminus\iota(A\times\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}})
, we can identify the above action with the quotient of the principal action
\Diff^{+}(V)\curvearrowright\emb(V,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
by the admissible, normal subgroup
\Diff_{\partial}(V)\unlhd\Diff^{+}(V)
,
and as a consequence the above action is principal by
2.4
.
3
.

Lemma 5.29
.

There is an
\Aut(A)\wr\SO(3)
equivariant zig-zag of equivalences

B\!\Diff_{A}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})\xleftarrow{\simeq}\frac{\emb^{+}(A\times D^{3},S^{3})\times\emb(S^{3},\mathbb{R}^{\infty})}{\SO(4)}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Conf_{A}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(S^{3})\sslash\SO(4)

Proof.

The right arrow is induced by the homotopy equivalence
\emb^{+}(A\times D^{3},S^{3})\to\Conf_{A}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(S^{3})
that records the derivative at centre of each disc, as in
5.27
.
We construct the left arrow as

[\iota\colon A\times D^{3}\hookrightarrow S^{3},j\colon S^{3}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\infty}]\longmapsto(j(S^{3}\setminus\iota(A\times\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}})),(j\circ\iota)_{|A\times S^{2}}^{-1}),

which is
\Aut(A)\wr\SO(3)
-equivariant by inspection.
To see that this is an equivalence, fix some
\iota\colon A\times D^{3}\hookrightarrow S^{3}
and factor the above map as

\displaystyle\frac{\emb^{+}(A\times D^{3},S^{3})\times\emb(S^{3},\mathbb{R}^{\infty})}{\SO(4)}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\emb^{+}(A\times D^{3},S^{3})\times\emb(S^{3},\mathbb{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff^{+}(S^{3})}\cong\frac{\emb(S^{3},\mathbb{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff_{\iota(A\times D^{3})}(S^{3})}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\emb(S^{3}\setminus\iota(A\times\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}}),\mathbb{R}^{\infty})}{\Diff_{\partial}(S^{3}\setminus\iota(A\times(D^{3})^{\circ}))}\cong B\!\Diff_{A}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})

where the first map is an equivalence by the Smale conjecture,
the second map is a homeomorphism, and the third map is an equivalence as both numerators are contractible and the map on denominators is an equivalence.
∎

Having established these alternative models for configuration spaces and punctured spheres, we can now compare
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
to a quotient defined in terms of these.
The final piece of notation needed is the group

\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))\leq\Map(H_{\Gamma},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))=\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}\Diff^{+}(S^{2})

whose elements are those families of diffeomorphisms
(\gamma_{h})_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}
such that
\gamma_{h^{\dagger}}=(-\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}})\circ\gamma_{h}\circ(-\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}})
holds for every half-edge
h
, where
(-\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}})
denotes the antipodal map on
S^{2}
.
If we choose a preferred half-edge in each edge,
i.e.
an orientation of the graph, then we obtain an isomorphism between this group and
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\Diff^{+}(S^{2})
, but this identification is not compatible with the
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-action. Note that, homotopically we can make this identification canonical: the group
\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))
is equivalent to its subgroup
\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\SO(3))
, and this subgroup is just
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)
because the antipodal map is central in
\Or(3)
.

Lemma 5.30
.

There is an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
-equivariant homotopy equivalence

\Theta\colon\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\longrightarrow\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb^{\rm D}(S^{2}\times H_{\sigma(i)},P_{i})}{\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))}

where the action by
\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))
re-parametrises, for each
h\in H_{\Gamma}
, the
h
-labelled sphere in either
B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})
if
r(h)=v\in H_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}
, or in
\emb(S^{2}\times H_{\sigma(i)},P_{i})
if
r(h)=\sigma(i)
.

Proof.

We begin by constructing
\Theta
as a map of sets (continuity is checked below)

[\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\alpha,\iota\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)\hookrightarrow\amalg_{i}P_{i}]\longmapsto[((M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon})_{\alpha(v)},\amalg_{h\in H_{v}}\psi_{h})_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}},(\iota\circ(\amalg_{h\in H_{\sigma(i)}}\psi_{h}))_{i=1}^{n}]

where we chose orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\psi_{h}\colon S^{2}\cong\Sigma_{\alpha(h)}
for all
h\in H_{\Gamma}
such that
\psi_{h}\circ(-\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}})=\psi_{h^{\dagger}}
.
The map does not depend on the choice of the
\psi_{h}
because we take the quotient by the group
\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))
. It follows from the above formula that the map is automatically equivariant for the action of
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
(which just reindexes) and
\prod_{i}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
(which acts on the embeddings into the
P_{i}
).

To check that the map is continuous and that it is a weak equivalence, we pick a preferred
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M
, which will allow us to write
\underline{\mathcal{H}}
as a quotient of embedding spaces by
\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
.
Similarly, we can write
\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))\cong\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
.
We further pick
\psi_{h}\colon S^{2}\cong\Sigma_{h}
as before.
In this context the map
\Theta
described above is

\frac{\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb(P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i},P_{i})}{\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})}\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{\Theta}\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb^{\rm D}(S^{2}\times H_{\sigma(i)},P_{i})}{\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})},

which comes from a map on the numerators that is equivariant for the denominators.
Indeed, the map on numerators is

[j\colon M\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\infty},(\iota_{i}\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i}\hookrightarrow P_{i})_{i=1}^{n}]\longmapsto[(j((M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon})_{\alpha(v)}),\amalg_{h\in H_{v}}j\circ\psi_{h})_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}},(\iota_{i}\circ(\amalg_{h\in H_{\sigma(i)}}\psi_{h}))_{i=1}^{n}].

This is continuous, and hence so is the map on quotients.
To prove that the map on quotients is an equivalence, we will compute its homotopy fibre.
The space on the right is connected and
both group-actions are principal (see
5.20
and the text below
5.28
). Hence, by
2.6
, the homotopy fibre of our map is the quotient of the fibre of the numerators by the fibre of the denominators.

On denominators we have a homotopy fibre sequence

\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}}\Diff_{\partial}((M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\circ})_{v})\simeq\Diff_{\Sigma^{\varepsilon}}(M)\longrightarrow\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(M,\Sigma^{\varepsilon})\longrightarrow\Diff^{\mathrm{cyl}}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}).

On numerators the map splits as a product of the map on the left factor and the map on the right factor.
For the left factor we pick a base-point consisting of some
(W_{v},\varphi_{v})_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}
in
\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})
and we have a homotopy fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\emb_{\partial}((M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\circ})_{v},W_{v})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\emb(M,\mathbb{R}^{\infty})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})}}

For the right term we pick as a base-point embeddings
\iota_{i}^{0}\colon S^{2}\times H_{\sigma(i)}\hookrightarrow P_{i}
and we have a homotopy fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb_{\iota_{i}^{0}}(P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i},P_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb(P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i},P_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb^{\rm D}(H_{\sigma(i)}\times S^{2},P_{i})}}

where the embeddings
\iota\colon P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i}\hookrightarrow P_{i}
must agree with
\iota_{i}^{0}
on the boundary.
Let
P_{i}^{\prime}\subset P_{i}
be the submanifold obtained by removing the open discs bounded by
\iota_{i}^{0}(H_{\sigma(i)}\times S^{2})
– then
\iota
exactly induces a diffeomorphism
P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i}\cong P_{i}
with a prescribed value on the boundary.
In summary the homotopy fibre of
\Theta
can be written as the quotient

\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\emb_{\partial}((M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\circ})_{v},W_{v})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb_{\partial}(P(\Sigma\subset M)_{i},P_{i}^{\prime})}{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}}\Diff_{\partial}((M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\circ})_{v})}.

Here numerator and denominator are equivalent via the action map, so the quotient is contractible,
which shows that
\Theta
is a homotopy equivalence.
∎

Remark 5.31
.

In the above lemma we crucially used that there is an isomorphism
\Or(3)\cong\SO(3)\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, which allowed us to pick a central orientation reversing element of order
2
– the antipodal map.
In even dimensions we would instead have to pick a reflection
R
, choose the maps
\psi_{h}\colon S^{2}\cong\Sigma_{\alpha(h)}
to satisfy
\psi_{h}\circ R=\psi_{h^{\dagger}}
, and instead of taking the quotient by
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\Diff^{+}(S^{2})
we would have to quotient by the group of families
(\gamma_{h})_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}
with
\gamma_{h^{\dagger}}=R\circ\gamma_{h}\circ R
.
Luckily, in dimension
3
we can just take
R
to be the antipodal map, which is central in
\Or(3)
.

We are now ready to prove
5.1
.

Proof of
5.1
.

By
5.25
and
5.30
we already have equivalences

\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\leftarrow\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\to\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\emb^{\rm D}(H_{\sigma(i)}\times S^{2},P_{i})}{\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\Diff^{+}(S^{2})}

that are
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i}\Diff(P_{i})
equivariant.
We can further replace the group in the denominator by the equivalent group
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)
.
Then we can use
5.29
and
5.27
to rewrite the quotient as

\frac{\underline{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\left(\Conf_{H_{v}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(S^{3})\sslash\SO(4)\right)\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{i})}}{\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)}.

This is then equivalent to

\frac{\underline{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\Conf_{H_{v}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(S^{3})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}^{\mathrm{fr}+}(P_{i})}}{\prod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\SO(4)\times\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)},

which is the desired configuration space model.
∎

5.5.
C^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
as a homotopy pullback

From our discussion in the previous subsection, we can construct two types of maps out of
\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}
.

(1)

For every
v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}
we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{v}\colon\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})}{\Map(H_{v},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))}\simeq\dots\simeq\Conf_{H_{v}}(S^{3})\sslash\SO(4)

\displaystyle[\Sigma\subset W,\alpha,\iota]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[(W\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\circ})_{\alpha(v)},\alpha_{|H_{v}}]

that takes the component of
W\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\circ}
corresponding to
v
and remembers the identification of its set of boundary components with the set of half-edges
H_{v}
at
v
.

(2)

For
1\leq i\leq n
we define a map

\displaystyle\psi_{i}\colon\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\frac{\emb^{\rm D}(H_{\sigma(i)}\times S^{2},P_{i})}{\Map(H_{\sigma(i)},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))}\simeq\dots\simeq\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i})

\displaystyle[\Sigma\subset W,\alpha,\iota]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\iota(\amalg_{h\in H_{\sigma(i)}}\Sigma_{\alpha(h)})]

that records the (unparametrised) bounding spheres obtained as the image of
\Sigma
in
P_{i}
.

(3)

Similarly to
5.30
, we have a map

\displaystyle\xi\colon\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))

\displaystyle[\Sigma\subset W,\alpha,\iota]

\displaystyle\longmapsto(h\mapsto\Sigma_{h})

that records the oriented sphere associated to each half-edge, with the orientation being induced by the coorientation and the orientation of
W
.
Here
C_{2}
acts on
B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})
by orientation reversal.

Together, these maps form the top map in a homotopy pullback square describing
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
in terms of more local data associated to vertices and edges of
\Gamma
.
This pullback square is closely related to the pullback square that witnesses the Segal condition for the modular
\infty
-operads of
3
-manifolds under connected sum.

Proposition 5.32
.

For all
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
and
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
, the
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
-equivariant commutative square

(9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{v\in V^{\rm sph}}\frac{B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})}{\Map(H_{v},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))}\times\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\emb^{\rm D}(H_{\sigma(i)}\times S^{2},P_{i})}{\Map(H_{\sigma(i)},\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\pm}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\prod_{v}\varphi_{v},\prod_{i}\psi_{i})}

is a homotopy pullback square.
Here, the right vertical map records boundary spheres.

Proof.

The square is commutative and equivariant by inspection.
Indeed, the action of
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
is given by permuting
S^{3}
-vertices and half-edges and does not change the underlying manifolds, and the action of
\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
is trivial on the spaces in the bottom row.

To see that the square is a homotopy pullback, it suffices to compare the homotopy fibres of the vertical maps.
The spaces in the bottom row (and in fact also those in the top row) are connected, so it will suffice to consider the homotopy fibre at one point.
As
B(-)
commutes with finite products we may rewrite
\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})\cong B\big(\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}\Diff^{+}(S^{2})\big)
and similarly for
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}
.
Next we replace
\underline{\mathcal{H}}
by the homotopy equivalent quotient from
5.30
– the maps in the square clearly factor through this.
Now both vertical maps are of the form
X\to*/\!\!/G
for a principal
G
-action on
X
and as such their (homotopy) fibre is
X
.
In both cases the space
X
is the same, namely the product of the
B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})
and the
\emb^{\rm D}(H_{\sigma(i)}\times S^{2},P_{i})
, and in particular the fibres are indeed the same.
∎

By taking the homotopy fibre of the top map in
5.32
, we can fit
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(\dots)
into a homotopy fibre sequence that will be useful for computations.
To better understand the resulting fibre sequence and spectral sequence we first consider the homotopy fibre sequence coming from the bottom map in
5.32
.

Let
\rho\in H^{4}(B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))
denote the generator that corresponds to the first Pontryagin class on
B\!\SO(3)
, and let
\beta\in H^{3}(\SO(3);\mathbb{Q})
denote the generator that transgresses to the first Pontryagin class.
(That is,
\beta
is a fixed non-zero multiple of the dual of the fundamental class of
\SO(3)\cong\mathbb{RP}^{3}
, but the exact prefactor will not matter.)

Lemma 5.33
.

The bottom map in
5.32
fits into an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-equivariant homotopy fibre sequence

\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\SO(3))\longrightarrow\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{\Delta^{\pm}}\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2}),

where
C_{2}
acts on
\SO(3)
by inverting.
The fibre is (non-canonically) equivalent to
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)
.
The cohomology of the fibre and base are

\frac{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\beta_{h}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\}}{\langle\beta_{h}+\beta_{h^{\dagger}}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\rangle}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathbb{Q}[\rho_{h}:h\in H_{\Gamma}].

In the Serre spectral sequence for this fibre sequence
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
are trivial, and
d_{4}
is determined by
d_{4}(\beta_{h})=\rho_{h}-\rho_{h^{\dagger}}
.

Proof.

The map
\Delta^{\pm}
is a subspace inclusion, namely the subspace of those
H_{\Gamma}
-tuples of oriented spheres
(S_{h})_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}
where for every half-edge
h\in H_{\Gamma}
we have
S_{h^{\dagger}}=S_{h}^{-}
,
i.e.

S_{h}
and
S_{h^{\dagger}}
are the same submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, but with opposite orientation.
To compute the homotopy fibre of
\Delta^{\pm}
, we factor it
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-equivariantly as a composite of an equivalence and a Serre fibration

\displaystyle\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow X=\{(S_{h},\psi_{h})_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}\;|\;S_{h}\in B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2}),\psi_{h}\colon S_{h}\cong S_{h^{\dagger}}^{-},\psi_{h^{\dagger}}=\psi_{h}^{-1}\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\Delta^{\prime}}\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})

where the first map includes the subspace where
\psi_{h}
is the identity map for all
h
, and the second map forgets the
\psi_{h}
.
(To see that the inclusion is an equivalence, note that, after choosing an orientation of the graph,
X
is equivalent
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})
.)
We can now compute the homotopy fibre of
\Delta^{\pm}
as the strict fibre of
\Delta^{\prime}
.

As a base-point in
\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})
we choose the constant tuple at the standard sphere
S^{2}
with its standard orientation, this is an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
fixed point and hence the fibre

F=\{(\psi_{h})_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}\in\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}\Diff(S^{2})\;|\;\psi_{h}\text{ orientation-reversing and }\psi_{h^{\dagger}}=\psi_{h}^{-1}\}.

still has an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-action.
Using that the antipodal map
(-\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}})
is a central element in
\Or(3)
, we can construct an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-equivariant map

\displaystyle\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\SO(3))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow F

\displaystyle(\gamma_{h})_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\psi_{h}=\gamma_{h}\circ(-\mathrm{id}_{S^{2}}))_{h\in H_{\Gamma}}

which is moreover a homotopy equivalence.
Here, as in the statement of the lemma,
C_{2}
acts on
\SO(3)
by inverting.
This identifies the homotopy fibre
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-equivariantly, as claimed.

To compute the cohomology, recall that

H^{*}(\SO(3))\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\beta\rangle

and

H^{*}(B\!\SO(3))\cong\mathbb{Q}[\rho]
.
The inversion map
(-)^{-1}\colon\SO(3)\to\SO(3)
acts by
\beta\mapsto-\beta
on the cohomology: we can compute its degree by noting that its derivative at the identity is
(-1)\colon\mathfrak{so}(3)\to\mathfrak{so}(3)
.
The description of the cohomology of the base and fibre now follows from the Künneth formula.

In the Serre spectral sequence we must have
d_{2}(\beta_{i})=0
and
d_{3}(\beta_{i})=0
, which via the Leibniz rule implies
d_{2}\equiv 0\equiv d_{3}
.
To compute the
d_{4}
-differentials, it will similarly suffice to determine
d_{4}(\beta_{h})
.
Choose an orientation on each edge and let
H_{\Gamma}^{+}\subset H_{\Gamma}
be the subset of positively oriented half-edges.
Consider the map of homotopy fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{H_{\Gamma}^{+}}\SO(3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{H_{\Gamma}^{+}}B\!\SO(3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\SO(3))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where the map
j
labels every negative half-edge by the standard sphere and
P
denotes the pullback.
The top fibre sequence is equivalent to the product, over the set of edges, of the standard fibre sequence
\SO(3)\to\mathrm{ESO}(3)\to B\!\SO(3)
.
We defined
\beta
such that it transgresses to
\rho
in this fibre sequence, so for every
h\in H_{\Gamma}^{+}
we have
j^{*}d_{4}(\beta_{h})=d_{4}(i^{*}\beta_{h})=\rho_{h}
.
On cohomology
j^{*}
sends
\rho_{h}
to
\rho_{h}
if
h\in H_{\Gamma}^{+}
and
0
otherwise.
Therefore, we get
d_{4}(\beta_{h})=\rho_{h}+\varepsilon
where
\varepsilon
is in the span of
\{\rho_{h^{\prime}}\;|\;h^{\prime}\in H_{\Gamma}^{-}\}
.
Repeating the same argument for
H_{\Gamma}^{-}
in place of
H_{\Gamma}^{+}
we see
d_{4}(\beta_{h^{\dagger}})=\rho_{h^{\dagger}}+\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
Since
\beta_{h^{\dagger}}=-\beta_{h}
, this implies
d_{4}(\beta_{h})=\rho_{h}-\rho_{h^{\dagger}}
as claimed.
∎

Corollary 5.34
.

For every
(\Gamma,\sigma)
and
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
, there is a homotopy fibre sequence

\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\SO(3))\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\longrightarrow\prod\limits_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\Conf_{H_{v}}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)\times\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i})

whose fibre is (non-canonically) equivalent to
\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)
.

Proof.

Because the square in
5.32
is a homotopy pullback, the horizontal homotopy fibres are equivalent, and we can read of the homotopy fibre of the bottom map from
5.33
.
∎

The Serre spectral sequence for the fibre sequence in
5.34
will be an effective tool for computing the (rational) cohomology of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
.
In
5.36
we record the basic structure of this spectral sequence and show that it is
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i}\pi_{0}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
-equivariant.
First, we establish a lemma about suitable generalities of such spectral sequences.

Lemma 5.35
.

Let
G
be a topological group and suppose we have a
G
-equivariant square of spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

that is also a homotopy pullback square.
Suppose further that
B^{\prime}
is simply connected and admits a
G
-fixed point
b_{0}\in B^{\prime}
, so the homotopy fibre
\hofib_{b_{0}}(\pi^{\prime})
inherits a
G
-action.
Then there is a convergent multiplicative spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(B;H^{q}(\hofib_{b_{0}}(\pi^{\prime}))\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(E)

and this spectral sequence is
\pi_{0}(G)
-equivariant for the diagonal
\pi_{0}(G)
-action on
H^{p}(B;H^{q}(\hofib_{b_{0}}(\pi^{\prime}))
and the usual action on
H^{p+q}(E)
.

Proof.

For every map
\pi\colon E\to B
there is a convergent multiplicative spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(B;H^{q}(\pi))\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(E)

where
H^{q}(\pi)
is the local system on
B
that assigns to each point
b\in B
the abelian group
H^{q}(\hofib_{b}(\pi))
, see
[
16
]
.
Note that we do not have to pick a base-point in
B
to make sense of this spectral sequence.
This spectral sequence is natural by construction:
any square as above (without assuming that it is a homotopy pullback square) induces a map of local coefficient systems
\alpha_{f}\colon f^{*}H^{q}(\pi^{\prime})\to H^{q}(\pi)
and a map on
E_{2}
-pages

H^{p}(B^{\prime};H^{q}(\pi^{\prime}))\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{f^{*}}H^{p}(B;f^{*}H^{q}(\pi^{\prime}))\xrightarrow[\hskip 16.38895pt]{(\alpha_{f})_{!}}H^{p}(B;H^{q}(\pi)),

which induces a map of spectral sequences compatible the the map
F^{*}
in the abutment.

Via naturality, left-multiplication
\lambda_{g}
by an element
g\in G
induces an automorphism of the spectral sequence.
These assemble into the desired
\pi_{0}(G)
-action on the aforementioned spectral sequence.
The action on the
E_{2}
-page comes from maps
\alpha_{\lambda_{g}}\colon\lambda_{g}^{*}H^{q}(\pi)\to H^{q}(\pi)
.
(In other words,
H^{q}(\pi)
has
\pi_{0}(G)
-fixed point data in the
\pi_{0}(G)
-category
\mathrm{Fun}(\Pi B,\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Q}})
of local systems on
B
.)
To prove the lemma, it will suffice to show that the local coefficient system
H^{p}(\pi)
on
B
is the trivial coefficient system with value
H^{q}(\hofib_{b_{0}}(\pi))
and the maps
\alpha_{\lambda_{g}}
are just the maps coming from acting with
g
on
\hofib_{b_{0}}(\pi)
.

The map
f
induces a map of local coefficient systems
\beta\colon f^{*}H^{q}(\pi^{\prime})\to H^{q}(\pi)
.
Because we assumed that the square is a homotopy pullback square,
f
induces a homotopy equivalence
\hofib_{b}(\pi)\simeq\hofib_{f(b)}(\pi^{\prime})
for all
b\in B
, which implies that
\beta
is an isomorphism.
But since
B^{\prime}
is assumed to be simply connected,
H^{q}(\pi^{\prime})
is a constant coefficient system and hence its pullback is also the constant coefficient system with the same value.
It remains to determine the
\pi_{0}(G)
-action on this coefficient group.
Because
b_{0}
is fixed by
G
we have a
G
-equivariant homotopy pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hofib_{b_{0}}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{b_{0}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

where the induced map in local coefficient systems is a
\pi_{0}(G)
-equivariant isomorphism
i^{*}H^{q}(\pi^{\prime})\cong H^{q}(\hofib_{b_{0}}(\pi^{\prime}))
.
This shows that
H^{q}(\pi)
is indeed the constant coefficient system at
H^{q}(\hofib_{b_{0}}(\pi^{\prime}))
with its usual
\pi_{0}(G)
-action.
∎

Proposition 5.36
.

There exists a multiplicative
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i}\pi_{0}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
-equivariant Serre spectral sequence converging to
H^{*}(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n});\mathbb{Q})
, with
E_{4}
-page

E_{4}^{*,*}\cong\frac{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\beta_{h}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\}}{\langle\beta_{h}+\beta_{h^{\dagger}}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\rangle}\otimes\bigotimes_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}H^{*}\left(\frac{\underline{\Conf_{H_{v}}(S^{3})}}{\SO(4)};\mathbb{Q}\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}H^{*}(\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i});\mathbb{Q})

where each
\beta_{h}
has bidegree
(0,3)
and the other tensor factors are contained in the
0
th row
E_{4}^{*,0}
.
The
d_{4}
differential is determined by

d_{4}(\beta_{h})=\delta_{r(h)}-\delta_{r(h^{\dagger})}

where we set
\delta_{v}=0
if
v
is a marked vertex, and otherwise
\delta_{v}
is the pullback of the first Pontryagin class
p_{1}\in H^{4}(B\!\SO(4))
along the map
\Conf_{H_{v}}(S^{3})\sslash\SO(4)\to*\sslash\SO(4)
.

Proof.

We apply
5.35
to the square from
Eq.

9
, by taking vertical homotopy fibres in
5.32
and taking
G:=\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
.
Note that
\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})
is indeed simply connected because
B\!\SO(3)
is, and that we can pick a
G
-invariant base-point
b_{0}
in the bottom right by assigning the same oriented sphere to all half-edges, so that
\mathrm{hofib}(\Delta^{\pm})
inherits a
G
-action.
(However, we will not generally be able to pick a
G
-invariant base-point in the top right, and thus the top map does not give a (homotopy) fibre sequence in spaces with
G
-action.)
Nevertheless,
5.35
applies and we obtain a
\pi_{0}(G)
-equivariant multiplicative Serre spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(B;H^{q}(\mathrm{hofib}_{b_{0}}(\Delta^{\pm});\mathbb{Q}))\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(E)

We saw in
5.33
that for the bottom fibre sequence we have

\hofib(\Delta^{\pm})\simeq\frac{\prod_{H_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)}{\prod_{E_{\Gamma}}\SO(3)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad H^{*}(\hofib(\Delta^{\pm});\mathbb{Q})\cong\frac{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\beta_{h}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\}}{\langle\beta_{h}+\beta_{h^{\dagger}}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\rangle}.

We thus get a
\pi_{0}(G)
-equivariant spectral sequence converging to the cohomology of
\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
, with
E_{2}
-page

E_{2}^{*,*}\cong\frac{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\beta_{h}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\}}{\langle\beta_{h}+\beta_{h^{\dagger}}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\rangle}\otimes\bigotimes_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}H^{*}\left(\frac{\underline{\Conf_{H_{v}}(S^{3})}}{\SO(4)};\mathbb{Q}\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}H^{*}(\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i});\mathbb{Q}).

Here, to describe the cohomology of the base we used the same identifications as before and then applied the Künneth theorem.
Note that
\pi_{0}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
only acts on
\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i})
.

The
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
differentials are
0
on
\beta_{h}
for degree reasons, and thus the Leibniz rule implies that all
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
differentials are
0
, so the
E_{4}^{*,*}
are still of the above form.
On the
E_{4}
-page all differentials are determined, through the Leibniz rule, by
d_{4}(\beta_{h})
, which we will now describe.
For the bottom fibre sequence, we saw in
5.33
that
d_{4}(\beta_{h})=\rho_{h}-\rho_{h^{\dagger}}
.

It hence remains to verify that the pullback of each
\rho_{h}
gives
\delta_{v}
for
v=r(h)
as defined in the statement.
For this, we have to take the pullback of the first Pontryagin class in
H^{4}(B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))
(the factor corresponding to
h
) under the right vertical map in
5.32
.
First consider the case of an unmarked vertex.
We have a homotopy commutative diagram where
s_{h}
records the sphere associated to
h\in H_{v}
(we are trying to show
s_{h}^{*}\rho_{h}=\delta_{v}
) and
T_{h}
records the tangent space at the configuration point associated to
h
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{B\!\Diff_{H_{v}}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})}{\prod_{H_{v}}\Diff^{+}(S^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Conf_{H_{v}}(S^{3})\sslash\SO(4)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*\sslash\SO(4)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\SO(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\SO(4)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\oplus\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

To construct the left square, we proceed as follows:
the bottom map sends an oriented
3
-dimensional sub vector space
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
to its unit sphere
S(V)
.
A point in
\Conf_{H_{v}}(S^{3})
can be thought of as an oriented
4
-dimensional sub vector space
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
together with an embedding
\iota\colon H_{v}\hookrightarrow S(W)
.
The top map takes such a tuple
(W,\iota)
and sends it to the submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
obtained by removing open
\varepsilon
-discs around each point of the configuration.
(We have to vary
\varepsilon
continuously with the configuration, but this is not an issue.)
The middle vertical map sends
(W,\iota)
to the tangent space
T_{\iota(h)}S(W)
of
S(W)
at the point
\iota(h)
.
The right square commutes up to homotopy because the two ways of going around the square either send
(W,\iota)
to
W
or to
T_{\iota(h)}S(W)\oplus\mathbb{R}
, but these are canonically isomorphic as the normal bundle of
S(W)
in
W
is trivial.
On cohomology this diagram shows us that
s_{h}^{*}(\rho_{h})
is indeed the pullback of the first Pontryagin class.

For marked vertices we can similarly argue that the map

s_{h}\colon\frac{\emb^{\rm D}(H_{\sigma(i)}\times S^{2},P_{i})}{\prod_{H_{\sigma(i)}}\Diff^{+}(S^{2})}\longrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(S^{2})

is equivalent to the map
\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i})\to B\!\SO(3)
that records the tangent space
T_{\iota(h)}P_{i}
.
Like every orientable
3
-manifold,
P_{i}
admits a framing, so we can choose a (non-canonical) null-homotopy of this map and thus
s_{h}^{*}(\rho_{h})=0
in this case.
∎

6.
Rational cohomology computations

The aim of this section is to lay the foundations for a computational framework that can be used to calculate the rational cohomology of the fibre
\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
of
\mathbb{W}
. More precisely, we provide explicit presentations for the rational cohomology ring of
\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
as an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{0}\Diff^{+}(P_{i})
-module, together with a description of the induced maps from morphisms in
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
. Throughout this section, all cohomology groups will be with rational coefficients, unless otherwise noted.

We begin by describing the rational cohomology rings of three spaces, with each description building on the previous. First, in
Section

6.1
, we consider the cohomology ring of the ordered configuration space
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})
as a representation of the symmetric group
\Sym_{d}
. Then in
Section

6.2
, we use this to compute the cohomology ring of the classifying space of the diffeomorphism group of
S^{3}
fixing
d
points, which can be identified with the homotopy quotient of
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)
.
Thirdly, in
Section

6.3
, we input the previous step into the spectral sequence from
5.36
to obtain a presentation for the rational cohomology of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
in
6.11
. The final section describes the effect of an edge collapse in terms of the generators of the cohomology ring. This is most concrete in the case when there are no irreducible prime factors (see
6.15
), and in
Section

7
, we will apply the results obtained here to calculate the rational cohomology ring of
B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2})
, where
U_{g}=(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}
.

Taking a broader view, configuration spaces of points in
S^{3}
assemble into a functor

\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3})\colon\mathrm{FI}^{\rm op}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Top},\qquad A\mapsto\emb(A,S^{3})

from the opposite of the category of finite sets and injections to the category of topological spaces.
This functor sends the set
\{1,\dots,d\}
to the ordered configuration space
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})
, and remembers the
\Sym_{d}
-action given by permuting points, as well as the maps
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})\to\Conf_{k}(S^{3})
given by forgetting some of the points.
Taking cohomology we obtain a functor

H^{*}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3}))\colon\mathrm{FI}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ring},

i.e.
an
\mathrm{FI}
-ring.
Similarly,
H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))\cong H^{*}(B\!\Diff^{+}_{\{1,\dots,d\}}(S^{3}))
forms an
\mathrm{FI}
-ring that we study in
Section

6.2
.
While the study of configuration spaces of points in manifolds is classical and dates back to early last century (see, for example,
[
18
]
and the references therein), the
\mathrm{FI}
-perspective is relatively recent
[
12
,
11
]
. In fact, one knows from general results of Church–Ellenberg–Farb
[
12
]
that the
\mathrm{FI}
-ring
H^{*}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3}))
is finitely generated and for each fixed degree
q
the module
H^{q}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3}))
exhibits representation stability as a representation of the symmetric group
\Sym_{d}
.
Although we will not explicitly rely on such results here, the
\mathrm{FI}
-perspective is useful to keep in mind as a guiding principle.
We will periodically return to it throughout the section, recasting results in these terms.
For example, we give presentation of
H^{*}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3}))
as an
\mathrm{FI}
-ring with two generators and four relations in
6.4
.

6.1.
The rational cohomology ring of
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})

We first calculate the rational cohomology of the configuration space of
d
ordered points
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})
as a representation of the symmetric group
\Sym_{d}
. Let the
d
marked points be
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\}
. Recalling that
S^{3}\cong\SU_{2}
, we can use the left action of
\SU_{2}
on itself to obtain diffeomorphisms

(10)

\varphi_{i}\colon\Conf_{d}(S^{3})\rightarrow S^{3}\times\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

where the
S^{3}
-factor corresponds to
x_{i}
, while the remaining coordinates in
\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
correspond to
x_{i}^{-1}x_{j}
for
i\neq j
. We will refer to the
\varphi_{i}
as
trivialisations
.

For
1\leq i\leq d
we also have projections
\pi_{i}\colon\Conf_{d}(S^{3})\rightarrow\Conf_{1}(S^{3})\cong S^{3}
that forget all but the point
x_{i}
. Let
\alpha_{i}=\pi_{i}^{*}(\mu)\in H^{3}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3}))
be the pullback of the fundamental class
\mu\in H^{3}(S^{3}).

Lemma 6.1
.

The classes
\alpha_{i}
,
\alpha_{j}
are equal for all
i,j
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the lemma when
d=2
and the two marked points are
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
. The trivialisations
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
in Equation
10
yield diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{3}\times S^{3}\cong\Conf_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times S^{3}\xleftarrow{\varphi_{2}}\Conf_{2}(S^{3})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}}S^{3}\times\Conf_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cong S^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}

Using the trivialisation
\varphi_{1}
, the class
\alpha_{1}
is the pullback of
\mu
under projection onto the
S^{3}
-factor. Hence
\alpha_{1}
is hom dual to the class
S^{3}\times\{0\}\subseteq S^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In
\Conf_{2}(S^{3})
, this corresponds to the 3-sphere
\{(x_{1},-x_{1})\mid x_{1}\in S^{3}\}
, which projects diffeomorphically under
\varphi_{2}
to
\{0\}\times S^{3}
. Since the latter is dual to
\alpha_{2}
, we just need to compute the degree of the induced map on
S^{3}
. From the description above the composite map
S^{3}\rightarrow S^{3}
is given by
x_{1}\mapsto-x_{1}
,
i.e.
the antipodal map. This has degree 1 since
S^{3}
is odd-dimensional. Therefore,
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
, as claimed.
∎

For
i,j,k
pairwise distinct consider the composition

\Conf_{d}(S^{3})\rightarrow\Conf_{3}(S^{3})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{k}}S^{3}\times\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\rightarrow\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

where the first map forgets all but
x_{i}
,
x_{j}
and
x_{k}
, and the
S^{3}
-factor in the trivialisation corresponds to
x_{k}
. In other words,
x_{k}
corresponds to the point at infinity in
\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, while the two points correspond to
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
. Using the action of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
on itself by translation, we can move the image of
x_{i}
to the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
to obtain a further trivialisation

\displaystyle\tau_{i}^{k}\colon\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\})

\displaystyle(v_{i},v_{j})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(v_{i},v_{j}-v_{i})

where
v_{i}
,
v_{j}
are the image of
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
respectively. Similarly, we have a trivialisation
\tau_{j}^{k}
translating the image of
x_{j}
to the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}.
Let
\pi_{ij}^{k}
denote the composition

\Conf_{d}(S^{3})\rightarrow\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\xrightarrow{\tau_{i}^{k}}\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\})

If
\omega\in H^{2}(S^{2})\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\})
is the fundamental class, we define
\omega_{ij}^{k}=(\pi_{ij}^{k})^{*}(\omega)
to be the pullback. Concretely,
\omega_{ij}^{k}
takes that value 1 on the 2-sphere that separates
x_{i}
from
x_{k}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
with the outward orientation. In particular, we have that

(11)

\omega_{ij}^{k}=-\omega_{kj}^{i}

Moreover, the two trivialisations
\tau_{i}^{k},\tau_{j}^{k}
induce a diagram

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{3}

\displaystyle\xleftarrow{\tau_{j}^{k}}\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\xrightarrow{\tau_{i}^{k}}\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\})

\displaystyle(v_{i}-v_{j},v_{j})

\displaystyle\longmapsfrom(v_{i},v_{j})\longmapsto(v_{i},v_{j}-v_{i})

Thus the induced map on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
is multiplication by
-1
, and we obtain

(12)

\omega_{ji}^{k}=-\omega_{ij}^{k}

Therefore any permutation of
\{i,j,k\}
acts via the sign representation on
\omega_{ij}^{k}.

Lemma 6.2
.

The second cohomology group
H^{2}(\Conf_{4}(S^{3}))
is 3-dimensional and generated by the four classes
\omega_{12}^{3}
,
\omega_{12}^{4}
,
\omega_{13}^{4}
and
\omega_{23}^{4}
with the relation

(13)

\omega_{12}^{4}+\omega_{23}^{4}=\omega_{13}^{4}+\omega_{12}^{3}

Proof.

The trivialisation
\varphi_{4}
identifies
\Conf_{4}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
S^{3}\times\Conf_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, where
x_{4}
corresponds to the point at infinity in
\mathbb{R}^{3}.
Moving
x_{3}
to the origin, we have a further identification:

\Conf_{4}(S^{3})\cong S^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\})

We now use the action of the positive reals
\mathbb{R}^{+}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
by scaling to move
x_{2}
onto the unit sphere
S^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
. The resulting projection

\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\})\rightarrow S^{2}

is a fibre bundle with fibre
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{\text{2 pts}\})
. The associated cohomology spectral sequence degenerates on the
E_{2}
-page, hence
H^{2}(\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}))
is generated by the pullback of the fundamental class from
S^{2}
, and
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{\text{2 pts}\})
. The pullback of the class on
S^{2}
comes from forgetting
x_{1}
, hence is just the class
\omega_{32}^{4}
.

The fibre
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{\text{2 pts}\}
parametrises the position of
x_{1}
in the complement of
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
. Let
S_{2}
be a 2-sphere containing
x_{2}
in its interior, let
S_{3}
be a 2-sphere with
x_{3}
in its interior, and let
S_{4}
be a 2-sphere with both
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
in its interior. The three homology classes
[S_{2}]
,
[S_{3}]
, and
[S_{4}]
generate
H_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{\text{2 pts}\})
but since
S_{2}\cup S_{3}\cup S_{4}
bounds 3-dimensional pair of pants, we have a relation

[S_{4}]=[S_{3}]+[S_{2}]

Exchanging the roles of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, we can also consider spheres
T_{2}
,
T_{3}
, and
T_{4}
, by letting
x_{2}
vary in a sphere containing
x_{1}
,
x_{3}
, and both
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
, respectively, in their interior. As above we have

[T_{4}]=[T_{1}]+[T_{3}]

and moreover
[T_{1}]=-[S_{2}]
. Therefore two generate all of
H_{2}(\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}))
we will consider the three classes
[S_{2}]
,
[S_{3}]
and
[T_{3}]
.

Recall that
\omega_{ij}^{k}
takes the value 1 on a sphere formed by
x_{j}
separating
x_{i}
from
x_{k}
with outward normal oriented toward
x_{k}
, and that by Equations
11
and
12
, any odd permutation of the indices induces a sign change. Thus we have

†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
pNiceMatrix (nicematrix)

from which we conclude

\omega_{21}^{4}+\omega_{32}^{4}=\omega_{31}^{4}+\omega_{21}^{3}.

Equation
13
now follows from Equation
12
.
∎

We now describe the rational cohomology ring
H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3}))
as a representation of
\Sym_{d}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

The rational cohomology of the configuration space of
d
points in
S^{3}
is given by

H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3}))\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\alpha\}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[\omega_{ij}^{k}:1\leq i,j,k\leq d]/I

where
\alpha
is of degree
3
,
the
\omega_{ij}^{k}
are of degree
2
,
and
I
is the ideal generated by the relations

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}^{k}

\displaystyle=-\omega_{ji}^{k}=\omega_{jk}^{i},

\displaystyle(\omega_{ij}^{k})^{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}^{k}

\displaystyle=\omega_{ij}^{l}+\omega_{jk}^{l}+\omega_{ki}^{l}.

The action of
\Sym_{d}
is given by permuting the indices.

Remark 6.4
.

In terms of
\mathrm{FI}
-representations, the statement of
6.3
may be rephrased as follows.
The
\mathrm{FI}
-ring
H^{*}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3}))
is generated by two classes

\alpha\in H^{3}(\Conf_{1}(S^{3}))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\omega_{12}^{3}\in H^{2}(\Conf_{3}(S^{3})).

Moreover,
H^{*}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3}))
is the free
\mathrm{FI}
-ring on these classes, subject to the following relations:

(1)

the images of
\alpha
under the two maps
H^{3}(\Conf_{1}(S^{3}))\to H^{3}(\Conf_{2}(S^{3}))
agree,

(2)

\omega_{12}^{3}\in H^{2}(\Conf_{3}(S^{3}))
transforms as the sign representation of
\Sym_{3}
,

(3)

(\omega_{12}^{3})^{2}=0
, and

(4)

\omega_{12}^{3}=\omega_{12}^{4}+\omega_{23}^{4}+\omega_{31}^{4}
holds in
H^{2}(\Conf_{4}(S^{3}))
.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

We define a ring homomorphism

\Psi\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\alpha\}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[\omega_{ij}^{k}:1\leq i,j,k\leq n]\to H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3}))

by sending
\alpha
and
\omega_{ij}^{k}
to the classes we defined before.
Next, we need to check that the ideal
I
lies in the kernel of
\Psi
.
By
Eq.

12
and
Eq.

11
, the identities
\omega_{ij}^{k}=-\omega_{ji}^{k}=\omega_{jk}^{i}
hold in
H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3}))
.
By
6.2
we have that

\omega_{12}^{4}+\omega_{23}^{4}=\omega_{13}^{4}+\omega_{12}^{3}\qquad\text{ and hence }\qquad\omega_{12}^{3}=\omega_{12}^{4}+\omega_{23}^{4}+\omega_{31}^{4}

and it also follows that this relation holds if we replace
\{1,2,3,4\}
by any other set of
4
distinct indices.
Finally, the relation
(\omega_{ij}^{k})^{2}=0
holds since
\omega_{ij}^{k}
is pulled back from
\Conf_{3}(S^{3})\simeq S^{3}\times S^{2}
, where
H^{4}=0
.
Therefore
\Psi
descends to a map on the quotient:

\overline{\Psi}\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\alpha\}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[\omega_{ij}^{k}:1\leq i,j,k\leq d]/I\to H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3}))

In order to show that
\overline{\Psi}
is an isomorphism, we will first have to algebraically rewrite the polynomial algebra
\mathbb{Q}[\omega_{ij}^{k}]/I
.
Firstly, we can only consider generators
\omega_{ij}^{k}
where
1\leq i<j<k\leq d
by using the permutation relation.
Next, we can use the relation
\omega_{ij}^{k}=\omega_{ij}^{l}+\dots

to reduce to those generators
\omega_{ij}^{k}
with
k=d
.
This shows

\mathbb{Q}[\omega_{ij}^{k}:1\leq i,j,k\leq d]/I\cong\mathbb{Q}[\omega_{ij}^{d}:1\leq i<j<d]/J

where
J
is the ideal generated by
(\omega_{ij}^{k})^{2}
for all
i\leq j\leq k
.
In the case that
k\neq d
this relation amounts to:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\omega_{ij}^{k})^{2}=(\omega_{ij}^{n}+\omega_{jk}^{d}+\omega_{ki}^{d})^{2}=\omega_{ij}^{d}\omega_{jk}^{d}+\omega_{ij}^{d}\omega_{ki}^{d}+\omega_{jk}^{d}\omega_{ki}^{d}

\displaystyle=\omega_{ij}^{d}\omega_{jk}^{d}-\omega_{ij}^{d}\omega_{ik}^{d}-\omega_{jk}^{d}\omega_{ik}^{d}

By
[
19
, Proposition 3.1]
,
the rational cohomology algebra of
\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is

H^{*}(\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cong\mathbb{Q}[\omega_{ij}:1\leq i<j<d]/((\omega_{ij})^{2},\omega_{ij}\omega_{jk}-\omega_{ij}\omega_{ik}-\omega_{jk}\omega_{ik})

The trivialisation
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})\cong S^{3}\times\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is an isomorphism on cohomology and it sends
\omega_{ij}
to
\omega_{ij}^{d}
.
Therefore we can conclude that
\overline{\Psi}
is an isomorphism.
∎

6.2.
The cohomology of the moduli space of punctured
3
-spheres

We now compute the rational cohomology of the homotopy quotient of
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})
by the action of
\SO(4)
. Equivalently, if
\Diff_{\{1,\dots,d\}}^{+}(S^{3})
denotes the
\Diff^{+}(S^{3})
-stabiliser of
d
distinct points
x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\in S^{3}
, then by Hatcher’s theorem
[
27
]
, we have:

B\!\Diff^{+}_{\{1,\dots,d\}}(S^{3})\simeq\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4).

This homotopy quotient arose naturally in the base of fibre sequence in
5.34
obtained by forgetting the framing at the foot of each handle attachment in
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
.

The action of
\SO(4)
on
\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3})
is compatible with the functoriality in
\mathrm{FI}^{\rm op}
, the homotopy quotient
\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)
also defines a functor
\mathrm{FI}^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Top}
.
This means that the cohomology ring

H^{*}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{4})/\!\!/\SO(4))\colon\mathrm{FI}\to\mathrm{Ring}

is also an
\mathrm{FI}
-ring. Our goal will be to give generators and relations for this ring, just as in
6.3
.
Note that the fibre sequence

\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3})\to\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)\to B\!\SO(4)

induces maps of
\mathrm{FI}
-rings on cohomology, where
H^{*}(B\!\SO(4))
is the constant
\mathrm{FI}
-ring
\mathbb{Q}[p_{1},e]
.

Before we begin, it is useful to note that
in degree
2
the restriction map

H^{2}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))\longrightarrow H^{2}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3}))

is an isomorphism.
This follows from the Serre spectral sequence since
B\!\SO(4)
is simply connected and has no rational cohomology below degree
4
.

Definition 6.5
.

We define the classes that will be the generators as follows:

(1)

The class
c_{12}^{3}\in H^{2}(\Conf_{3}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))
is the unique class that restricts to
2\cdot\omega_{12}^{3}
under the map

\Conf_{3}(S^{3})\hookrightarrow\Conf_{3}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4).

(This is well-defined because we observed that this map induces an isomorphism in second rational cohomology.)

(2)

The class
\delta\in H^{4}(\Conf_{0}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))\cong H^{4}(B\!\SO(4))
is the first Pontrjagin class.

We identify
S^{3}=\SU_{2}
and
\SO(4)=(\SU_{2}\times\SU_{2})/(-1,-1)
.
The standard action of
\SO(4)
on
S^{3}
may then be written as the conjugation action:

(g,h).v:=g\cdot v\cdot h^{-1}.

Each factor of
\SU_{2}
acts freely, transitively on
S^{3}
and the stabiliser of any point is isomorphic to
\SO(3)
. Recall the trivialisation
\varphi_{1}\colon\Conf_{d}(S^{3})\rightarrow S^{3}\times\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
from Equation
10
. Under this identification, the
\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-coordinate corresponds to a collection of distinct vectors in the tangent space of the
S^{3}
-coordinate. The action of
\SU_{2}\times\SU_{2}
translates as follows

\textstyle{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{(g,h)\cdot}
\textstyle{(x_{1},x_{1}^{-1}x_{2},\cdots,x_{1}^{-1}x_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g,h)\cdot}
\textstyle{(gx_{1}h^{-1},\ldots,gx_{d}h^{-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{1}}
\textstyle{(gx_{1}h^{-1},hx_{1}^{-1}x_{2}h^{-1},\ldots,hx_{1}^{-1}x_{d}h^{-1})}

In particular, the first factor of
\SU_{2}
acts trivially on
\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
while the second factor acts by the conjugation action on each tangent space.
Since the first factor of
\SU_{2}
clearly acts freely on
S^{3}
and the conjugation action is equivalent to the defining action of
\SO(3)
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we can first quotient by the action of the left-hand
\SU_{2}
to get

\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)\simeq(\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\SU_{2})/\!\!/\SO(3)\simeq\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})/\!\!/\SO(3).

Lemma 6.6
.

For
d=3
let we have a homotopy equivalence
\Conf_{3}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)\simeq B\!\SO(2)
such that
c_{12}^{3}
goes to the Euler class in
H^{2}(B\!\SO(2))
.
In particular,
c_{12}^{3}
comes from an integral class.

Proof.

Let
p=(0,e_{1},\infty)\in\Conf_{3}(S^{3})
be the configuration of three points given by the origin, the first basis vector, and the point at
\infty
in
S^{3}=(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{+}
.
Let
\SO(2)\subset\SO(4)
be the subgroup of rotations around the first coordinate axis.
Because
\SO(2)
fixes
p
, we get a map

B\!\SO(2)=\{p\}/\!\!/\SO(2)\longrightarrow\Conf_{3}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4),

which we will show is a homotopy equivalence.
Consider the inclusion
S^{2}\subset\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\subset\Conf_{3}(S^{3})
defined by
v\mapsto(0,v)
and
(a,b)\mapsto(a,b,\infty)
, respectively.
Then we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Conf_{3}(S^{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{p\}/\!\!/\SO(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{2}/\!\!/\SO(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})/\!\!/\SO(3)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Conf_{3}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(-;\mathbb{Z})\text{-iso}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(-;\mathbb{Q})\text{-iso}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where first bottom map is an equivalence by the homotopical orbit-stabiliser lemma (
[
6
, Lemma 2.10]
), the second one is the
\SO(3)
-equivariant equivalence
S^{2}\simeq\Conf_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and the third map is an equivalence by the preceding discussion.

The left-most vertical map
i
is the fibre inclusion of a fibration
S^{2}/\!\!/\SO(3)\rightarrow B\!\SO(3)
. From the long exact sequence on homotopy groups, we see that, since
\pi_{2}B\!\SO(3)=\mathbb{Z}/2
, the map
i
must induce multiplication by
2
on
\pi_{2}
and hence on
H_{2}
.
Therefore, the generator of
H^{2}(S^{2}/\!\!/\SO(3);\mathbb{Z})
(which is the Euler class in
B\!\SO(2)
) pulls back to twice a generator on
S^{2}
.
The above diagram hence shows that under the isomorphism
H^{2}(B\!\SO(2);\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{2}(\Conf_{3}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4);\mathbb{Z})
the Euler class maps to some
e^{\prime}
whose pullback to
\Conf_{3}(S^{3})
is
2\cdot\omega_{12}^{3}
.
By the definition of
c_{ij}^{k}
we must have
e^{\prime}=c_{12}^{3}
.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

There is a well-defined map of graded rings

(14)

\Phi\colon\mathbb{Q}[\delta,c_{ij}^{k}:1\leq i,j,k\leq d]/I_{\delta}\longrightarrow H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))

where
I_{\delta}
is the ideal generated by the relations

\displaystyle c_{ij}^{k}

\displaystyle=-c_{ji}^{k}=c_{jk}^{i},

\displaystyle(c_{ij}^{k})^{2}

\displaystyle=\delta

\displaystyle c_{ij}^{k}

\displaystyle=c_{ij}^{l}+c_{jk}^{l}+c_{ki}^{l}.

Proof.

We need to show that the classes
c_{ij}^{k}
and
\delta
that we define above indeed satisfy these relations.
Since the restriction map

H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))\longrightarrow H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3}))

is an isomorphism for
*=2
, the linear relations between the
c_{ij}^{k}
follow from the relations we know for
\omega_{ij}^{k}
.
It remains to check that
(c_{12}^{3})^{2}=\delta
holds in
H^{*}(\Conf_{3}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))
.
By
6.6
we can verify this relation in
H^{*}(B\!\SO(2))
, which is a polynomial ring
\mathbb{Q}[e]
generated by the Euler class
e
corresponding to
c_{12}^{3}
under the isomorphism.
The class
\delta
is the restriction of the first Pontryagin class along the stabilisation map
B\!\SO(2)\to B\!\SO(4)
, which gives
p_{1}=e^{2}=(c_{12}^{3})^{2}
.
The case for general
i,j,k
follows by restriction along maps that forget and relabel points.
∎

Let
R_{\delta}
denote the ring
\mathbb{Q}[\delta,c_{ij}^{k}:1\leq i,j,k\leq d]/I_{\delta}
appearing in
Eq.

14
. In order to establish that
\Phi
is an isomorphism, we will need the following property of
R_{\delta}
:

Lemma 6.8
.

The element
\delta\in R_{\delta}
is cancellable,
i.e.
it is not a zero-divisor.

Proof.

Eliminating all linear relations and the relation
\delta=(c_{d-2,d-1}^{d})^{2}
in the presentation of
R_{\delta}
, we observe that
R
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Q}[c_{ij}^{d}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq d-1]/J
, where
J
is the ideal generated by the set
G
consisting of

\begin{array}[]{cc}(c_{ij}^{d})^{2}-(c_{d-2,d-1}^{d})^{2},&c_{ij}^{d}c_{jk}^{d}-c_{ij}^{d}c_{ik}^{d}-c_{jk}^{d}c_{ik}^{d}-(c_{d-2,d-1}^{d})^{2}\end{array}

Order the generators so that
c_{ij}^{d}>c_{pq}^{d}
if and only if
i<p
, or
i=p
and
j<q
. With respect to the resulting monomial order,
G
is a Gröbner basis. The verification of this fact is routine and left to the reader (see
[
13
]
).
To simplify notation we denote the minimal generator by
z=c_{d-2,d-1}^{d}
. Note that
z^{2}
represents
\delta
.

To prove that
\delta=[z^{2}]
is not a zero-divisor in
\mathbb{Q}[c_{ij}^{d}]/J
we need to argue that
p\cdot z^{2}\in J
implies
p\in J
for any polynomial
p\in\mathbb{Q}[c_{ij}^{d}]
.
To show this, note that no multiple of
z
is the leading term of an element of
G
.
Therefore the leading term of
pz^{2}
is divisible by the leading term of an element of
G
if and only if the leading term of
p
is.
Moreover, when performing a
G
-reduction for such a leading term the result will still be of the form
p^{\prime}z^{2}
.
Hence
pz^{2}
can be
G
-reduced to 0 if and only if
p
can be
G
-reduced to 0.
Since
G
is a Gröbner basis, this shows
p\in J\Leftrightarrow pz^{2}\in J
, as claimed.
∎

We now show that
\Phi
is in fact an isomorphism.
This means that the
\mathrm{FI}
-ring
H^{*}(\Conf_{\bullet}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))
is generated by
c_{12}^{3}
and
\delta
subject to the relations listed above.

Proposition 6.9
.

The map
\Phi
in
Eq.

14
is an isomorphism of graded rings.
Therefore the rational cohomology of
B\!\Diff^{+}_{\{1,\dots,d\}}(S^{3})\simeq\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)
is

H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))\cong\mathbb{Q}[\delta,c_{ij}^{k}:1\leq i,j,k\leq d]/I_{\delta}

and the action of
\Sym_{d}
is given by permuting the indices.

Proof.

We have already constructed the (
\Sym_{d}
-equivariant) morphism of graded rings from
R_{\delta}
to the cohomology of
\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4)
, so we only need to show that it is an isomorphism of vector spaces.
Consider the Serre spectral sequence associated to the fibration

\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\to\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})/\!\!/\SO(3)\to B\!\SO(3).

It has signature

E_{2}^{p,q}\cong H^{q}(\Conf_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\otimes H^{p}(B\!\SO(3))\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})/\!\!/\SO(3)).

The
E_{2}
-page is even and therefore the spectral sequence collapses:
E_{2}^{p,q}=E_{\infty}^{p,q}
.
The
E_{\infty}
-page is the associated graded of a decreasing filtration

\dots\subseteq F_{a}^{*}\subseteq\dots\subseteq F_{0}^{*}=H^{*}(\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})/\!\!/\SO(3))

with
F_{a}^{*}=0
for
a>*
and
F_{a}^{a}\subseteq H^{a}(\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})/\!\!/\SO(3))
being the image of the cohomology of the base
H^{a}(B\!\SO(3))
.
This filtration is compatible with the multiplicative structure in the sense that
F_{a}^{*}\cdot F_{b}^{*}\subseteq F_{a+b}^{*}

and its associated graded is isomorphic, as a bi-graded ring, to:

\bigoplus_{p}F_{p}^{*}/F_{p+1}^{*}\cong\bigoplus_{p}H^{*}(\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\otimes H^{p}(B\SO(3))

The class
\delta\in H^{4}(\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})/\!\!/\SO(3))
comes from a cohomology class in the base
H^{4}(B\SO(3))
and therefore it is in filtration degree
4
.

We equip the ring
R_{\delta}
with the “
\delta
-adic filtration” where an element
x\in R_{\delta}
is in
F_{4m}^{*}
if and only if we can write
x=y\delta^{m}
.
Then
\Phi
is compatible with the filtration and the associated graded of the
\delta
-adic filtration on
R_{\delta}
is isomorphic to
(R_{\delta}/\delta R_{\delta})\otimes\mathbb{Q}[\delta]
because
\delta
is cancellable by
6.8
.
The map induced by
\Phi
on the associated graded is

\mathrm{gr}(\Phi)\colon(R_{\delta}/\delta R_{\delta})\otimes\mathbb{Q}[\delta]\to H^{*}(\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\otimes H^{*}(B\!\SO(3)).

This is an isomorphism since
H^{*}(B\!\SO(3))\cong\mathbb{Q}[\delta]
and
R_{\delta}/\delta R_{\delta}=\mathbb{Q}[c_{ij}^{k}]/I\cong H^{*}(\Conf_{d-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
by
6.3
.
It follows that the original ring homomorphism
\Phi
was an isomorphism as claimed.
∎

Corollary 6.10
.

The ring
H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))
is a free graded
\mathbb{Q}[\delta]
-module.

Proof.

We know that
H^{*}=H^{*}(\Conf_{d}(S^{3})/\!\!/\SO(4))
is a (connective) graded
\mathbb{Q}[\delta]
-module on which multiplication by
\delta
is injective.
Therefore, we can construct a basis inductively, by picking in each degree
k
a basis of the complement of
\delta H^{k-4}\subset H^{k}
.
∎

6.3.
The cohomology of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}

We now describe the rational cohomology ring of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
functorially in automorphisms in
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
.
We are mainly interested in the example of
M=U_{g}:=(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp g}
, where
n=0
and
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}=\mathcal{S}(\Gamma)
.
In this case we get a complete description in terms of generators and relations.
When
n>0
we still obtain a description in terms of the cohomology of the configuration spaces of the
P_{i}
.
The functoriality in edge contractions will be discussed in the next subsection (see
6.16
).

In the cohomology of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
we will have a class
c_{ij}^{k}
for every triple of distinct half-edges
(i,j,k)\in H_{\Gamma}
that are incident at the same vertex.
It will hence be useful to introduce the notation

H_{\Gamma}^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}:=\{(i,j,k)\in H_{\Gamma}^{3}\;|\;r(i)=r(j)=r(k)\}=\coprod_{v\in V_{\Gamma}}(H_{v})^{3}.

We will also write
H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)
for the first cellular homology of
