\Gamma
relative to the subspace
\{\sigma(1),\dots,\sigma(n)\}
, which is an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-representation.

Theorem 6.11
.

For
(\Gamma,\sigma)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
there is an
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-equivariant isomorphism of graded rings

\displaystyle H^{*}(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}^{\Gamma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)[3]\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Q}[\delta,c_{ij}^{k}:(i,j,k)\in H_{\Gamma}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}}]/I^{\Gamma}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}H^{*}(\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i}))

where
H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)[3]
denotes the first homology of the graph
\Gamma
relative to the subset of marked vertices, shifted to have degree 3.
The ideal
I^{\Gamma}
is generated by

\displaystyle I^{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\langle c_{ij}^{k}=-c_{ji}^{k}=c_{jk}^{i},c_{ij}^{k}=c_{ij}^{l}+c_{jk}^{l}+c_{ki}^{l},(c_{ij}^{k})^{2}=\delta\rangle&\text{ for }n=0\\
\langle c_{ij}^{k}=-c_{ji}^{k}=c_{jk}^{i},c_{ij}^{k}=c_{ij}^{l}+c_{jk}^{l}+c_{ki}^{l},(c_{ij}^{k})^{2}=\delta=0\rangle&\text{ for }n>0\end{cases}

where the indices
i,j,k
and
l
run over distinct half-edges incident at the same vertex.

Remark 6.12
.

Conceptually, the reason that we have
\delta=0
for
n>0
is that
\delta
represents the
0
th homology group of
\Gamma
relative to the set of marked points, which is trivial once there is at least one marked point.

Example 6.13
.

For
n=0
and
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,0}
we have
\Aut(\Gamma)
-equivariant isomorphisms

\displaystyle H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Gamma))\cong H^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma})

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle H_{1}(\Gamma)[3]\rangle\otimes\frac{\mathbb{Q}[\delta,c_{ij}^{k}:(i,j,k)\in H_{\Gamma}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}}]}{\langle c_{ij}^{k}=-c_{ji}^{k}=c_{jk}^{i},c_{ij}^{k}=c_{ij}^{l}+c_{jk}^{l}+c_{ki}^{l},(c_{ij}^{k})^{2}=\delta\rangle},

which in low degrees gives

\displaystyle H^{1}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma})

\displaystyle\cong 0,

\displaystyle H^{2}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma})

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{Q}\langle H_{\Gamma}^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\rangle}{\langle c_{ij}^{k}=-c_{ji}^{k}=c_{jk}^{i},c_{ij}^{k}=c_{ij}^{l}+c_{jk}^{l}+c_{ki}^{l}\rangle},

\displaystyle H^{3}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma})

\displaystyle\cong H_{1}(\Gamma).

We first establish a general lemma about spectral sequences that we will use in the proof of
6.11
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Suppose
(E_{*},d_{*})
is a multiplicative cohomological spectral sequence with

E_{4}^{*,*}\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}(E_{4}^{0,3})\otimes E_{4}^{*,0}

and assume that
E_{4}^{*,0}
is free as a module over
\mathbb{Q}[V]
where
V=\mathrm{Im}(d_{4}\colon E_{4}^{0,3}\to E_{4}^{4,0})
.
Then the
E_{\infty}
-page is

E_{\infty}^{*,*}\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}(\ker(d_{4}\colon E_{4}^{0,3}\to E_{4}^{4,0}))\otimes R

where
R
is the quotient of the ring
E_{4}^{0,*}
by the ideal generated by
V\subset E_{4}^{0,4}
.
Moreover, there are no multiplicative extensions.

Proof.

By assumption we can find an isomorphism of graded
\mathbb{Q}[V]
-modules
E_{4}^{*,0}\cong\mathbb{Q}[V]\otimes B
for some graded vector space
B
.
Let
R
be the quotient of the ring
E_{4}^{*,0}
by the ideal generated by
V\subset E_{4}^{4,0}
.
Then the composite
B\hookrightarrow E_{4}^{*,0}\twoheadrightarrow R
is an isomorphism, and we can identify
B\cong R
.
We can non-canonically decompose
E_{4}^{0,3}\cong K\oplus V
where
K=\ker(d_{4}\colon E_{4}^{0,3}\to E_{4}^{4,0})
.
Then the
E_{4}
page is

E_{4}^{*,*}\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}(K^{(0,3)})\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}(V^{(0,3)})\otimes\mathbb{Q}[V^{(4,0)}]\otimes R^{(*,0)}

where the superscripts indicate bidegree.
(Note that this is not an isomorphism of rings, but it respects the module structure over the first three terms.)
The
d_{4}
differential induces the identity isomorphism
d_{4}=\mathrm{id}\colon V^{(0,3)}\to V^{(4,0)}
and is
0
on
K^{(0,3)}
and
R^{(*,0)}
.
Thus, if we think of
E_{4}^{*,*}
as a chain complex (only recording total degree) it decomposes into a tensor product

(E_{4}^{*,*},d_{4})\cong\mathrm{grSym}(V[3]\xrightarrow{d_{4}=\mathrm{id}}V[4])\otimes\left(\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}(K[3])\otimes R,d=0\right)

where
\mathrm{grSym}(-)
takes the graded symmetric powers.
Taking homology, and using that over
\mathbb{Q}
the functor
\mathrm{grSym}(-)
commutes with taking homology, we see that

E_{5}^{*,*}\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}(K^{(3,0)})\otimes R^{(*,0)}.

The ring structure on this is the canonical one, coming from thinking of
R^{(*,0)}
as a quotient of
E_{4}^{(*,0)}
– namely the quotient by the ideal generated by
V
.
The
d_{5}
-differential is trivial on
K^{(3,0)}
and on
R^{(*,0)}
for degree reasons, so the multiplicative structure implies that
d_{5}\equiv 0
and similarly for all higher differentials.
This shows that the
E_{\infty}
-page has the desired structure.
Finally, we note that there cannot be any multiplicative extensions because
E_{\infty}^{0,*}
is a free graded symmetric algebra.
∎

Proof of
6.11
.

We will use the spectral sequence described in
5.36
.
Using
6.9
the
E_{2}
-page of this spectral sequence is

\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}[\beta_{h}:h\in H_{\Gamma}]/\langle\beta_{h^{\dagger}}=-\beta_{h}\rangle\otimes\bigotimes_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\mathbb{Q}[\delta_{v},c_{ij}^{k}:(i,j,k)\in H_{v}]/I_{\delta}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}H^{*}(\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i})).

From
5.36
we also know that the first non-zero differentials are the
d_{4}
differentials, and that on the generators
\beta_{h}
these are given by

d_{4}(\beta_{h})=\delta_{r(h)}-\delta_{r(h^{\dagger})}.

The notation here indeed matches that of
5.36
: we interpret
\delta_{v}=0
whenever
v
is a marked vertex.
The first interesting differential is then

d_{4}\colon E_{4}^{0,3}\cong\mathbb{Q}\langle\beta_{h}:h\in H_{\Gamma}\rangle/\langle\beta_{h^{\dagger}}+\beta_{h}\rangle\longrightarrow E_{4}^{4,0}\cong\mathbb{Q}\langle\delta_{v}:v\in V_{\Gamma}\rangle\oplus(\text{other terms}).

In fact the formula
5.36
gives for
d_{4}(\beta_{h})
tells us that this map lands in the
\mathbb{Q}\langle\delta_{v}:v\in V_{\Gamma}\rangle
summand and gives us a copy of the relative cellular chain complex

\partial\colon C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)=\frac{\mathbb{Q}\langle H_{\Gamma}\rangle}{[h]=-[h^{\dagger}]}\longrightarrow C_{0}(\Gamma,\sigma)=\frac{\mathbb{Q}\langle V_{\Gamma}\rangle}{\mathbb{Q}\langle\sigma(1),\dots,\sigma(n)\rangle}

of
\Gamma
within the spectral sequence.
We thus see the relative homology of
\Gamma
on the
E_{5}
-page as

E_{5}^{0,3}\cong H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad E_{5}^{0,3}\cong H_{0}(\Gamma,\sigma).

To determine the remainder of the spectral sequence, we use
6.14
.
We already know that
E_{4}^{0,*}
is an exterior algebra on
C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)[3]
.
To describe the row
E_{4}^{*,0}
, recall from
6.10
that the tensor factor corresponding to a spherical vertex
v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}
is free over
\mathbb{Q}[\delta_{v}]
, and thus
E_{4}^{0,*}
is free as a module over
\mathbb{Q}[\delta_{v}:v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}]
.
This also implies that
E_{4}^{0,*}
is also free as a module over
\mathrm{grSym}_{\mathbb{Q}}(d_{4}(E_{4}^{0,3}))
, as
d_{4}(E_{4}^{0,3})
is a subvector space (of codimension
0
or
1
) of
\mathbb{Q}\langle\delta_{v}:v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}\rangle
.

6.14
now gives us a formula for the
E_{5}
-page and tells us that the spectral sequence collapses and has no multiplicative extensions, so that there are isomorphisms of rings with
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-action

H^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))\cong E_{\infty}^{*,*}\cong E_{5}^{*,*}\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)[3]\rangle\otimes R

where
R
is the ring obtained as the quotient of
E_{4}^{*,0}
by the ideal generated by the image
d_{4}(E_{4}^{0,3})\subset E_{4}^{4,0}
.
Inserting our previous description of the cohomology of the base we get

R=\frac{\bigotimes_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}\mathbb{Q}[\delta_{v},c_{ij}^{k}:(i,j,k)\in H_{v}]}{d_{4}(E_{4}^{0,3})+\sum_{v\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}}I_{\delta_{v}}}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}H^{*}(\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i})).

We need to check that the ideal we quotient out in the first term is exactly
I_{\Gamma}
.
When
n>0
the cellular boundary map
\partial\colon C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)\to C_{0}(\Gamma,\sigma)
is surjective, so that
d_{4}(E_{4}^{0,3})
contains all
\delta_{v}
.
If instead
n=0
, then the image of
\partial
has codimension
1
so that taking the quotient by
d_{4}(C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma))
exactly identifies all the
\delta_{v}
.
∎

Remark 6.15
.

In the proof of
6.11
, the isomorphism
H^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))\cong E_{\infty}^{*,*}
is not necessarily canonical.
Nevertheless, it is still compatible with the multiplicative structure (as
E_{\infty}^{0,*}
is a free graded symmetric algebra) and the
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-action (as the category of
\mathbb{Q}
-representations over a finite group is semi-simple).
This will be sufficient for our applications.
The subtlety lies in splitting the short exact sequence

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}H^{3}(\Conf_{H_{\sigma(i)}}(P_{i}))\longrightarrow H^{3}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))\longrightarrow H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)

that relates
\bigoplus_{a+b=3}E_{3}^{a,b}
to
H^{3}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma})
.
There always is a splitting as
\Aut(\Gamma,\sigma)
-representations, but this is not necessarily canonical.
(Note that for
n=0
the first group is trivial, resolving this issue.)
However, we believe that one could also obtain this splitting canonically, by constructing for each relative cycle in
(\Gamma,\sigma)
a map
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\to\SO(3)
that records how the framing changes along the path.

6.4.
Functoriality of
\beta_{h}
and
c_{ij}^{k}

In
6.11
we describe the cohomology ring of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
functorially in graph
isomorphisms
.
For our computations it will be necessary to also understand what happens to the generating classes under more general graph maps, such as edge-contractions.
For our applications, we will only need the case when
n=0
, but we will also give a partial description for general
n
.

Let
f\colon\Gamma\to\Lambda
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
.
Then for a vertex
v\in\Lambda
, the preimage
f^{-1}(v)
is necessarily a tree.
Therefore, given three half-edges
i,j,k\in H_{v}
incident at
v
we can find a unique tripod connecting
f^{-1}(i),f^{-1}(j),f^{-1}(k)
in
f^{-1}(v)
.
This tripod contains a unique triple of half-edges
i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime}\in H_{\Gamma}
all incident at the same vertex
u\in\Gamma
, which necessarily satisfies
f(u)=v
.
This defines a map

f^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\colon H_{\Lambda}^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\to H_{\Gamma}^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}

and in fact makes
H_{(-)}^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\colon\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm op}\to\mathrm{Set}
a functor.
(For example, in
Fig.

6
the triple
(i,j,k)
is mapped to
(h,j^{\prime},k^{\prime})
.)

Figure 6
.
An illustration (in
2
D rather than
3
D) of the homotopy commutative diagram in the proof of
6.16
.

Lemma 6.16
.

The map induced by
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\to\mathcal{H}^{\Lambda}_{\rho}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
on cohomology maps the
c_{i,j}^{k}
classes as

c_{(i,j,k)}\mapsto c_{f^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}(i,j,k)}

for all
(i,j,k)\in H_{\Lambda}^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}
.

Proof.

We begin by describing the action on the
c_{ij}^{k}
.
Consider a graph morphism
f\colon\Gamma\to\Lambda
,
w\in V_{\Lambda}^{\rm sph}
an unmarked vertex, and
i,j,k\in H_{w}
adjacent half-edges.
Without loss of generality we may assume that
f^{-1}(w)
consists of a single edge
e=\{h,h^{\dagger}\}
connecting two unmarked vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in V_{\Gamma}^{\rm sph}
.
(If
f^{-1}(w)
is a single vertex, then the claim follows essentially from the definition, and if
f^{-1}(w)
contains multiple edges, we can write
f
as a composite of morphisms that each only collapse one edge, reducing to the case at hand.)
We write
H_{v_{1}}=A\sqcup\{h\}
and
H_{v_{2}}=B\sqcup\{h^{\dagger}\}
where
A
and
B
are finite sets with each at least two elements – then
H_{w}=f(A)\sqcup f(B)
.
Up to symmetry there are two cases we need consider: either
i\in f(B)
and
j,k\in f(A)
, or all half edges are on one side.
We will consider the former case, and leave the easier case to the reader.

We would like to show that
\underline{\mathcal{H}}(f)^{*}(c_{ij}^{k})=c_{hj^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}}
where
j^{\prime},k^{\prime}\in A
are the (unique) preimages of
j
and
k
under
f
.
To show this, we first introduce some notation.
We let
S^{2}\subset S^{3}
be the equator and
D^{3}_{+},D^{3}_{-}\subset S^{3}
the upper and lower hemisphere.
We pick,
D_{A}\subset D^{3}_{+}
and
D_{B}\subset D^{3}_{-}
collections of discs, indexed by
A
and
B
, and we write
D_{i}\subset D_{A}
and
D_{j},D_{k}\subset D_{B}
for the three discs of particular interest.
For
N\subset S^{3}
a compact submanifold (of not necessarily constant dimension) we let
\Diff_{\pi_{0}}(S^{3},N)\subset\Diff(S^{3},N)
denote the subgroup of the diffeomorphisms
\psi
with
\psi(N)=N
that induce the identity permutation on
\pi_{0}(N)
.

With this notation in hand, there is a homotopy commutative diagram as illustrated in
Fig.

6
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}}(S^{3},D_{A}\sqcup D^{3}_{-})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}}(S^{3},D^{3}_{-}\sqcup D_{j}\sqcup D_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}}(S^{3},S^{2}\sqcup D_{A}\sqcup D_{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}}(S^{3},S^{2}\sqcup D_{i}\sqcup D_{j}\sqcup D_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Lambda}_{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}}(S^{3},D_{A}\sqcup D_{B})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}}(S^{3},D_{i}\sqcup D_{j}\sqcup D_{k})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{v_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{H}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{w}}

The maps
\varphi_{v_{i}}
and
\varphi_{w}
were described at the start of
Section

5.5
.
The version used here implicitly uses the inverse of the homotopy equivalence

B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}C}(S^{3},C\times D^{3})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}C}(S^{3}\setminus(C\times\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}}))\cong\frac{B\!\Diff_{C}^{\rm sph}(S^{3})}{\Map(C,\Diff^{+}(S^{2}))}

that cuts out the interiors of the discs
C\times D^{3}
.
The map
\chi
in the above diagram is constructed by taking
(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M,\dots)\in\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
, cutting
M
along all components of
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}
except the one corresponding to
\{h,h^{\dagger}\}
, and then remembering the component corresponding to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
together with the
2
-sphere given by
\Sigma_{\{h,h^{\dagger}\}}
.
(Again, this map really lands in
B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}\partial}(S^{3}\setminus(\smash{\mathring{D_{A}}}\sqcup\smash{\mathring{D_{B}}}),S^{2})
and we use the homotopy inverse to the map that cuts out the interiors.)
The left half of the diagram commutes up to homotopy by inspecting definitions.
(Crucially, the bottom square commutes since
\underline{\mathcal{H}}(f)
is exactly given by forgetting the component of
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}
that corresponds to the edge
\{h,h^{\dagger}\}
.)
The right half of the diagram is constructed by maps that just forget part of the constraints we put on the diffeomorphisms (or project to
*
),
i.e.
they come from subgroup inclusions (or maps to the trivial group), and thus it automatically commutes.
The two vertical maps on the right side are homotopy equivalences as a consequence of the Smale conjecture, since exactly one of the discs is on one side of the sphere – this can be seen in the usual manner by repeatedly applying fibre sequences.

Given the homotopy commutative diagram we argue as follows.
The pullback of the preferred generator of
H^{3}(B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}}(S^{3},D_{i}\sqcup D_{j}\sqcup D_{k}))
along the bottom two maps is by definition
c_{i,j}^{k}
.
On the other hand, the pullback of the preferred generator along the top row is by definition
c_{h,j^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}}
where
f(j^{\prime})=j
and
f(k^{\prime})=k
.
∎

We can also describe the functoriality of the classes
\beta_{a}
for
a\in H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)
.
However, as pointed out in
6.15
for
n>0
these classes are only well-defined up to splitting a short exact sequence, and hence we will have the same ambiguity in describing their functoriality.

Lemma 6.17
.

For every graph map
f\colon(\Gamma,\sigma)\to(\Lambda,\rho)
in
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}
we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(\mathcal{H}^{\Lambda}_{\rho}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(\Lambda,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(f)^{*}}

where the horizontal maps are those discussed in
6.15
and they are isomorphisms for
n=0
.

Proof.

Fix some
(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\alpha,\iota)\in\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})
.
We begin by giving a concrete description of the (homotopy) fibre of the top horizontal map in
5.32
,
which deletes
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M
and records the components of the complement.
Let
X(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M)
be the space of those
embeddings
j\colon M\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\infty}

such that
j_{|M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}}
is the inclusion
M\setminus\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
.
This space is equivalent to
B\!\Diff_{\partial}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
via the map that sends
j
to
j(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
.
(Note that the boundary
\partial j(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
must be equal to
\partial\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
.)

For a graph morphism
f\colon\Gamma\to\Lambda
,
let
(\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}\subset M)
denote the thickened sphere system obtained by forgetting the spheres that correspond to edges collapsed by
f
,
and let
\Xi^{\varepsilon}=\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}
be the union of the deleted thickened spheres.
We have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{\partial}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}\subset M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\!\Diff_{\partial}(\Upsilon^{\varepsilon})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}\subset M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathcal{H}}^{\Lambda}_{\rho}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{H}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cup\mathrm{id}_{\Xi^{\varepsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Here the right horizontal maps are the inclusions of the fibres of the top map in
5.32
.
The middle map takes the union with the missing thickened spheres
\Xi^{\varepsilon}
.
(The right square commutes on-the-nose by inspection.)
Correspondingly, the left vertical map is induced by the group homomorphism
\Diff_{\partial}(\Upsilon^{\varepsilon})\to\Diff_{\partial}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})
that extends a diffeomorphism by the identity.
Recall that we have equivalences
B\!\Diff_{\partial}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon})\simeq\Map^{C_{2}}(H_{\Gamma},\SO(3))
.
In particular, we have a canonical identification between
H^{3}(B\!\Diff_{\partial}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}))
and the cellular
1
-chains
C_{1}(\Gamma)
.
Because
\sigma
consists only of vertices
C_{1}(\Gamma)=C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)
.
Taking third cohomology groups we thus get a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(B\!\Diff_{\partial}(\Sigma^{\varepsilon}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}(\Lambda,\rho)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(B\!\Diff_{\partial}(\Upsilon^{\varepsilon}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{3}(\mathcal{H}^{\Lambda}_{\rho}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(f)^{*}}

where the left map is the map
f\colon\Gamma\to\Lambda
induces on relative cellular
1
-chains.
(Namely, it sends all the edges collapsed by
f
, which correspond to spheres in
\Xi
, to
0
, and is an isomorphism otherwise.)
Finally,
the image of the right vertical map

H^{3}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))\to C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)
,
is exactly the image of the cohomology of the total space in the cohomology of the fibre, for the fibre sequence for the fibre sequence resulting from the top map in
5.32
.
In the Serre spectral sequence this image corresponds to
E_{\infty}^{0,3}\subseteq E_{2}^{0,3}=C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)
, which by the proof of
6.11
, is exactly
H_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)\subseteq C_{1}(\Gamma,\sigma)
.
Hence, the square restricts to the desired commutative square.
∎

6.5.
The spectral sequence

Applying the Bousfield–Kan spectral sequence for the cohomology of a homotopy colimit
[
5
, XII.5.8]
to the space of handle-attachments from
4.21
yields the following.

Corollary 6.18
.

There is a convergent spectral sequence in cohomological Serre grading with

(15)

E_{2}^{p,q}=\operatornamewithlimits{lim^{\it p}}_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm op}}H^{q}(\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\sigma}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n});\mathbb{Q})\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n}))

and this spectral sequence is
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{0}\Diff(P_{i})
equivariant.

By
4.22
the space of handle-attachments is the homotopy fibre of the splitting map
\mathbb{W}
, so there is a Serre spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(B\!\Diff(P_{M});H^{q}(\mathcal{H}_{g}(P_{1},\dots,P_{n});\mathbb{Q}))\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(B\!\Diff(M);\mathbb{Q})

whose
E_{2}
exactly is cohomology with coefficients in the cohomology of
\mathcal{H}_{g}(\dots)
, which is described by
6.18
.
(If we assume for simplicity that none of the two
P_{i}
are diffeomorphic, then
\pi_{1}B\!\Diff(P_{M})
is just
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{0}\Diff(P_{i})
, so
6.18
also describes the action on the coefficients.)

Example 6.19
.

For
n=0
, that is, in the case of
M=U_{g}
, the target of the splitting map is a point and
Eq.

15
already computes the cohomology of
B\!\Diff(U_{g})
.
In this case
6.16
and
6.17
already completely describe the cohomology of
\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}
as a functor of
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,0}
, so the question of determining the
E_{2}
-page in
Eq.

15
is a purely algebraic one.
However, this is still difficult for larger
g
:
for example, the
0
th row is the derived limit of the constant functor, which gives the cohomology of the category
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,0}
.
We have

E_{2}^{p,0}=\operatornamewithlimits{lim^{\it p}}_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}_{g,n}^{\rm op}}H^{0}(\dots)\cong H^{p}(B\mathrm{Gr}_{g,0})\cong H^{p}(B\Out(F_{g}))

where the last isomorphism follows from the contractibility of outer space
[
14
]
.
These groups become very hard to compute for
g\gg 0
, and hence we should not expect a simple description of the
E_{2}
for larger genus.

Remark 6.20
.

In
[
23
]
, Giansiracusa gives a homotopy colimit formula for
B\!\Diff^{+}(K_{g})
where
K_{g}=(S^{1}\times D^{2})^{\natural g}
is the
3
-dimensional handlebody of genus
g
.
In his setting the spectral sequence must collapse on the
E_{2}
-page
[
23
, §7]
because the diagram on
\mathrm{Gr}_{g,0}
that he uses is built from the cyclic operad of framed
2
-discs, which is known to be formal.
As a consequence, he can express the rational homology of
B\!\Diff^{+}(K_{g})
as the homology of a graph complex.
We do not expect a similar result to hold in our setting, as the analogous framed
3
-discs operad is not known to be formal as a cyclic
\infty
-operad.
(However, it was recently shown to be formal as an
\infty
-operad
[
15
]
.)

7.
Extended Example:
B\!\Diff^{+}\!\left((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2}\right)

In this section, we combine the results of the Section
6
with our main theorem to compute the rational cohomology ring of
B\!\Diff^{+}\left(U_{2}\right)
, where
U_{2}=(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2}
.
Note that in this case, since
n=0
, the target of the splitting map is contractible and thus main theorem
5.2
reduces to the homotopy colimit formula
3.24
.
We further have
\mathrm{Gr}(U_{2})=\mathrm{Gr}_{2,0}
and for any dual graph
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr}(U_{2})
the group
\Aut_{\mathrm{Gr}(U_{2})}(\Gamma)
is the usual group of graph symmetries
\Aut(\Gamma)
.

By
2.32
, we can compute the homotopy colimit over the subcategory
\mathrm{Gr}^{\rm nr}_{2,0}
consisting of graphs without redundant edges.
For genus 2, there are exactly two homeomorphism types of graph without redundant edges, namely
\Gamma=R_{2}
, the wedge of two circles (rose with two petals), and
\Gamma=\Theta
, the graph with two vertices connected by three edges. Moreover, there is only one kind of non-invertible morphism up to symmetry, namely the map
\pi\colon\Theta\rightarrow R_{2}
that collapses the middle edge of
\Theta
.
Therefore, the homotopy colimit can be written as a pushout of homotopy orbit constructions, see
2.22
.

\scriptstyle v_{1}
\scriptstyle v_{2}
\scriptstyle e_{1}
\scriptstyle e_{2}
\scriptstyle e_{3}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 1^{\prime}
\scriptstyle 3^{\prime}
\scriptstyle 2^{\prime}

\scriptstyle f_{1}
\scriptstyle f_{2}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle 4

Figure 7
.
Notation for
\Theta
, pictured on the left, and
R_{2}
, on the right.

First we establish notation to refer to the edges and half-edges of these graphs, and compute there automorphism groups. We refer to Figure
7
for a summary. When
\Gamma=R_{2},
there are two edges
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
both of which are loops. Label the half edges
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
,
h_{3}
,
h_{4}
so that
h_{1}=h_{2}^{\dagger}
belong to
f_{1}
while
h_{3}=h_{4}^{\dagger}
belong to
f_{2}
. When
\Gamma=\Theta,
there are two vertices
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
connected by three edges
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
, oriented from
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
. In this case we label the half edge of
e_{i}
at
v_{1}
by
h_{i}
, and the half-edge at
v_{2}
by
h_{i}^{\prime}=h_{i}^{\dagger}
.
Let
\pi\colon\Theta\to R_{2}
be the graph morphism that collapses
e_{3}
.
Concretely, we define
\pi
on vertices and half-edges by

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto v

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto h_{2}

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto h_{4}

\displaystyle h_{3}

\displaystyle\mapsto v

\displaystyle v_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto v

\displaystyle h_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto h_{1}

\displaystyle h_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto h_{3}

\displaystyle h_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto v.

We have that
H_{1}(\Theta)=\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle

where
\beta_{1}=[e_{3}-e_{1}]
and
\beta_{2}=[e_{3}-e_{2}]
.
The map
\pi
is a homotopy equivalence and identifies
H_{1}(\Theta)
with
H_{1}(R_{2})
.
The orientation of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are chosen so that
\pi^{*}
by sends
\beta_{1}=[e_{3}-e_{1}]
to
[f_{1}]
and
\beta_{2}=[e_{3}-e_{2}]
to
[f_{2}]
.
By an abuse of notation we will also write
H_{1}(R_{2})=\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle
where
\beta_{1}=[f_{1}]
and
\beta_{2}=[f_{2}].

The automorphism group of
R_{2}
is isomorphic to
D_{8}
, the dihedral group of order
8
. We regard this as a subgroup of the symmetric group
\Sym_{4}
via its action on the four half-edges
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
,
h_{3}
, and
h_{4}
. Since
h_{1},h_{2}
belong to the edge
f_{1}
and
h_{3},h_{4}
belong to the edge
f_{2}
, we are identifying
D_{8}
with the subgroup of
\Sym_{4}
generated by the two elements
\{(12),(13)(24)\}
.

The automorphism group of
\Theta
is isomorphic to
\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}
. Here the symmetric group
\Sym_{3}
(written in cycle notation) acts by permuting the edges, while
C_{2}\cong\{\pm 1\}
acts by exchanging the two vertices, and reversing each of the edges. The subgroup of automorphisms that commute with the collapse map
\pi\colon\Theta\rightarrow R_{2}
are those which fix
e_{3}
setwise. This subgroup is therefore isomorphic to
C_{2}\times C_{2}
, where the first factor is the subgroup
\langle(12)\rangle\leq\Sym_{3}
which swaps edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, and the second factor inverts each edge.

Since there is one morphism up to isomorphism in
\mathrm{Gr}_{2,0}^{\rm nr}
, Słominska’s formula reduces to a homotopy pushout square as in
2.22
.
Together with the automorphism group calculations above, we obtain:

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
U_{2}=(S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2}
. Then there exists a homotopy pushout square

(16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(\Theta)/\!\!/(C_{2}\times C_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(\Theta)/\!\!/(\Sym_{3}\times C_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(R_{2})/\!\!/D_{8}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Applying rational cohomology, this means
H^{*}(B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2}))
can be computed via a Mayer–Vietoris sequence from the three terms
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}}
,
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{C_{2}\times C_{2}}
, and
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))^{D_{8}}
, where the superscripts denote the invariant part of the cohomology.

As a consequence of Lemma
7.7
below we will see that the map
\pi
in the above diagram is an isomorphism on rational cohomology. This implies that the right hand vertical map is also an isomorphism.
It follows from
Eq.

4
and
3.21
that
\mathcal{S}(\Theta)/\!\!/\Aut(\Theta)
is equivalent to
B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2},\Sigma)
where
\Sigma\subset U_{2}
is any separating system whose dual graph is
\Theta
. We therefore we conclude:

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
\Sigma\subset U_{2}
be any separating system such that
G_{\Sigma}\cong\Theta
. Then the induced map on classifying spaces

B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2},\Sigma)\hookrightarrow B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2})

is a rational homology isomorphism.

7.1.
Presentations of
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Gamma))

We use Theorem
6.11
to find presentations for
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Gamma))
for
\Gamma=\Theta
and
\Gamma=R_{2}
. Since
n=0
, we recall that for every triple of distinct, adjacent half-edges
(i,j,k)
there is a class
c_{ij}^{k}\in H^{2}(\mathcal{S}(\Gamma))
, as well as a unique class
\delta\in H^{4}(\mathcal{S}(\Gamma))
such that
(c_{ij}^{k})^{2}=\delta
for every
(i,j,k)
. We also have
\Aut(\Gamma)
-equivariant isomorphisms
H^{3}(\mathcal{S}(\Gamma))\cong H^{1}(\Gamma)
for both
\Gamma=\Theta
and
\Gamma=R_{2}.

It will be useful to have the following notation.

Definition 7.3
.

For two non-negatively graded
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras
R,Q
with
R_{0}=\mathbb{Q}\langle 1\rangle=Q
, we let
R\vee Q\subset R\times Q
denote the subring with

(R\vee Q)_{n}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Q}\langle 1\rangle&\text{ if }n=0,\\
R_{n}\oplus Q_{n}&\text{ if }n>0.\end{cases}

This is chosen such that for connected spaces
X,Y
we have
H^{*}(X\vee Y)\cong H^{*}(X)\vee H^{*}(Y)
.

\mathbf{\Gamma=\Theta:}
The vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
contribute degree 2 classes
c_{1}=c_{12}^{3}
and
c_{2}=c_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{3^{\prime}}
, respectively, where we recall that swapping any two indices changes the sign. Moreover we have that
c_{1}^{2}=c_{2}^{2}=\delta
. Thus

\displaystyle H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle H_{1}(\Theta)\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Q}[c_{1},c_{2}]/\langle c_{1}^{2}=c_{2}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Q}[c_{1},c_{2}]/\langle c_{1}^{2}=c_{2}^{2}\rangle

where the
\beta_{i}
have degree 3. In order to decompose the
\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}
action, it will be convenient to introduce the following change of basis. Let
u_{1}=(c_{1}+c_{2})/2
and
u_{2}=(c_{1}-c_{2})/2
. Then
u_{1}u_{2}=(c_{1}^{2}-c_{2}^{2})/4=0
.
Hence we can also write

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))=\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle\otimes\left(\mathbb{Q}[u_{1}]\vee\mathbb{Q}[u_{2}]\right).

Since
u_{1},u_{2}
each have degree 2, it follows that in degree
2n
we have

\left(\mathbb{Q}[u_{1}]\vee\mathbb{Q}[u_{2}]\right)_{(2n)}=\langle u_{1}^{n},u_{2}^{n}\rangle

and is zero in all odd degrees.

\mathbf{\Gamma=R_{2}:}
In this case there is only one vertex but four half edges so we have classes
c_{ij}^{k}
, with
1\leq i,j,k\leq 4
pairwise distinct. We recall that
c_{ij}^{k}=-c_{ji}^{k}=c_{jk}^{i}
,
(c_{ij}^{k})^{2}=\delta
and
c_{12}^{3}=c_{12}^{4}+c_{23}^{4}+c_{31}^{4}
. The ideal generated by these relations is
I_{\delta}
and we obtain

\displaystyle H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle H_{1}(R_{2})\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Q}[c_{ij}^{k},\delta]/I_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Q}[c_{ij}^{k},\delta]/I_{\delta}.

As above we seek a basis adapted to the
D_{8}
-action. We rewrite the relation
c_{12}^{3}=c_{12}^{4}-c_{13}^{4}+c_{23}^{4}
as

c_{12}^{3}-c_{12}^{4}=c_{34}^{2}-c_{34}^{1}

Let
w=\frac{c_{12}^{3}-c_{12}^{4}}{2}=\frac{c_{34}^{2}-c_{34}^{1}}{2}
and define

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{12}^{3}+c_{12}^{4}}{2},~~z_{2}=\frac{c_{34}^{2}+c_{34}^{1}}{2}.

Thus
\{w,z_{1},z_{2}\}
are linearly independent and generate
\mathbb{Q}[c_{ij}^{k},\delta]/I_{\delta}
as a ring. Now observe that

\displaystyle z_{1}w

\displaystyle=\frac{(c_{12}^{3})^{2}-(c_{12}^{4})^{2}}{4}=\frac{\delta-\delta}{4}=0

\displaystyle z_{2}w

\displaystyle=\frac{(c_{34}^{2})^{2}-(c_{34}^{1})^{2}}{4}=\frac{\delta-\delta}{4}=0.

Moreover, we have

\frac{\delta-c_{12}^{3}c_{12}^{4}}{2}=w^{2}=\frac{\delta-c_{34}^{2}c_{34}^{1}}{2}

whence
c_{12}^{3}c_{12}^{4}=c_{34}^{2}c_{34}^{1}
. This implies that

z_{1}^{2}=\frac{\delta+c_{12}^{3}c_{12}^{4}}{2}=\frac{\delta+c_{34}^{2}c_{34}^{1}}{2}=z_{2}^{2}.

Thus, every element of degree
2n
can be written as a linear combination of
\{w^{n},z_{1}^{n},z_{1}^{n-1}z_{2}\}
.
By induction it follows that for all
n\geq 1
we have:

w^{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\delta^{\frac{n-1}{2}}w&n\text{ odd}\\
\delta^{\frac{n-2}{2}}w^{2},&n\text{ even}\end{array}\right.\qquad z_{1}^{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\delta^{\frac{n-1}{2}}z_{1}&n\text{ odd}\\
\delta^{\frac{n-2}{2}}z_{1}^{2},&n\text{ even}\end{array}\right.\qquad z_{1}^{n-1}z_{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\delta^{\frac{n-1}{2}}z_{2}&n\text{ odd}\\
\delta^{\frac{n-2}{2}}z_{1}z_{2},&n\text{ even}\end{array}\right.

It is not difficult to verify that
\{w^{2},z_{1}^{2},z_{1}z_{2}\}
is linearly independent when
n=2
, and since
\delta
is not a zero divisor by
6.8
, we conclude that
\{w^{n},z_{1}^{n},z_{1}^{n-1}z_{2}\}
is linearly independent for every
n
. With respect to the generating set
\{w,z_{1},z_{2}\}
we thus obtain a presentation

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))=\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle\otimes\left(\mathbb{Q}[w]\vee\mathbb{Q}[z_{1},z_{2}]/\langle z_{1}^{2}=z_{2}^{2}\rangle\right).

Since
w
,
z_{1}
, and
z_{2}
have degree 2, the degree
2n
part of the righthand factor is

\left(\mathbb{Q}[w]\vee\mathbb{Q}[z_{1},z_{2}]/\langle z_{1}^{2}=z_{2}^{2}\rangle\right)_{(2n)}=\langle w^{n},z_{1}^{n},z_{1}^{n-1}z_{2}\rangle

and is zero in odd degrees.

7.2.
Computing the invariants

We now decompose
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))
and
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))
into irreducible representations, with respect to the actions of
\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}
and
C_{2}\times C_{2}
on the former, and
D_{8}
on the latter.
All notation for the irreducible characters of these groups and their representation rings is contained in Figure
8
.

\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}

\begin{array}[]{c|cccccc}\text{class}&1&(12)&(123)&-1&-(12)&-(123)\\
\hline\cr\text{size}&1&3&2&1&3&2\\
\hline\cr\mathds{1}&1&1&1&1&1&1\\
\sigma&1&-1&1&1&-1&1\\
\tau_{3}&2&0&-1&2&0&-1\\
\rho&1&1&1&-1&-1&-1\\
\sigma\rho&1&-1&1&-1&1&-1\\
\tau_{3}\rho&2&0&-1&-2&0&1\\
\end{array}

(a)

The character table for
\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}
, with
\Sym_{3}
in cycle notation and
C_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
.

•

Relations:
\sigma^{2}=\rho^{2}=\mathds{1}
,
\tau_{3}\sigma=\tau_{3}
,
\tau_{3}^{2}=\mathds{1}+\sigma+\tau_{3}.

•

Exterior powers:
\Lambda^{2}(\tau_{3})=\sigma
.

C_{2}\times C_{2}

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}\text{class}&1&(12)&-1&-(12)\\
\hline\cr\text{size}&1&1&1&1\\
\hline\cr\mathds{1}&1&1&1&1\\
\kappa_{1}&1&-1&1&-1\\
\kappa_{2}&1&1&-1&-1\\
\kappa_{3}&1&-1&-1&1\\
\end{array}

(b)

The character table for
C_{2}\times C_{2}
, regarded as
\langle(12)\rangle\times\{\pm 1\}
.

•

Relations:
\kappa_{1}^{2}=\kappa_{2}^{2}=1
,
\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{3}
.

•

Exterior powers: none.

D_{8}

\begin{array}[]{c|ccccc}\text{class}&1&(12)(34)&(12)&(13)(24)&(1324)\\
\hline\cr\text{size}&1&1&2&2&2\\
\hline\cr\mathds{1}&1&1&1&1&1\\
\lambda_{1}&1&1&-1&1&-1\\
\lambda_{2}&1&1&1&-1&-1\\
\lambda_{3}&1&1&-1&-1&1\\
\tau_{4}&2&-2&0&0&0\\
\end{array}

(c)

The character table for
D_{8}
as a subgroup of
\Sym_{4}
, generated by
(12)
and
(34)
.

•

Relations:
\lambda_{1}^{2}=\lambda_{2}^{2}=1
,
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}
,
\tau_{4}\lambda_{1}=\tau_{4}\lambda_{2}=\tau_{4}
,
\tau_{4}^{2}=\mathds{1}+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}.

•

Exterior powers:
\Lambda^{2}(\tau_{4})=\lambda_{3}
.

Figure 8
.
Character tables and representation rings of
\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}
,
C_{2}\times C_{2}
, and
D_{8}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

As a graded commutative ring, the invariants for the action
\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}
on
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))
has presentation

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}}=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{1},\varepsilon_{1}]/\langle\varepsilon_{1}^{2}\rangle\vee\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{2}]

where
\gamma_{1}=u_{1}^{2}
,
\gamma_{2}=u_{2}^{2}
have degree 4, and
\varepsilon_{1}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}u_{1}
has degree 8. In particular, the Betti numbers are
b_{4}=2
and
b_{4k}=3
for all
k\geq 2.

Proof.

\Sym_{3}
acts by permuting the edges
e_{i}
, while
C_{2}
acts by inverting them. Therefore the 1-chains in
\Theta
form a regular representation of
\Sym_{3}
which restricts to the 2-dimensional subspace of cycles (the orthogonal complement of
e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}
) as the standard representation
\tau_{3}
.

The
C_{2}
-action which inverts each edge acts by
-1
on
H_{1}(\Theta)\cong\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle
;
the corresponding representation of
\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}
on
\Lambda^{1}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle
is therefore
\tau_{3}\rho
, and on
\Lambda^{2}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle=\langle\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\rangle=\Lambda^{2}(\tau_{3}\rho)=\sigma
.
Each permutation in
\Sym_{3}
acts on
c_{i}
by the sign representation
\sigma
.
On the other hand, the
C_{2}
exchanges the two
\Conf_{3}(S^{3})
factors, mapping
c_{1}
to
c_{2}
and vice versa. The subspaces spanned by
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are preserved so the representation on
\langle c_{1},c_{2}\rangle=\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle
decomposes as two 1-dimensional representations
\langle u_{1}\rangle\oplus\langle u_{2}\rangle=\sigma+\sigma\rho
. Each of these 1-dimensional representations squares to the trivial representation
\mathds{1}
. Summarising, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{1}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tau_{3}\rho,

\displaystyle\Lambda^{2}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\rangle=\sigma

\displaystyle\langle u_{1}^{n},u_{2}^{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma+\sigma\rho,&n\geq 1\text{ odd}\\
2\cdot\mathds{1},&n\geq 2\text{ even}\end{array}\right.

From the tensor product description of
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))
we then have:

\displaystyle\tau_{3}\rho\otimes(\sigma+\sigma\rho)

\displaystyle=\tau_{3}\rho+\tau_{3}

\displaystyle\tau_{3}\rho\otimes(2\cdot\mathds{1})

\displaystyle=2\cdot\tau_{3}\rho

\displaystyle\sigma\otimes(\sigma+\sigma\rho)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}+\rho

\displaystyle\sigma\otimes(2\cdot\mathds{1})

\displaystyle=2\cdot\sigma

The trivial summand in
\sigma\otimes(\sigma+\sigma\rho)
comes from
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\otimes u_{1}
. Since
|\beta_{i}|=3
and
u_{1}u_{2}=0
, setting
\gamma_{1}=u_{1}^{2}
,
\gamma_{2}=u_{2}^{2},
and
\varepsilon_{1}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}u_{1}
we have

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}}=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{1},\varepsilon_{1}]/\langle\varepsilon_{1}^{2}\rangle\vee\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{2}].

Since
|\gamma_{1}|=|\gamma_{2}|=4
and
|\varepsilon_{1}|=8
, the degree 4 part is 2-dimensional and spanned by
\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\}
while for all
k\geq 2
degree
4k
part is 3-dimensional and spanned by spanned by
\{\gamma_{1}^{k},\gamma_{1}^{k-2}\varepsilon_{1},\gamma_{2}^{k}\}
which proves that the Betti numbers are as claimed.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

As a graded commutative ring, the invariants for the action of
C_{2}\times C_{2}
on
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))
has presentation

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{C_{2}\times C_{2}}=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{1},\varepsilon_{1}]/\langle\varepsilon_{1}^{2}\rangle\vee\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{2},\mu_{2}]/\langle\mu_{2}^{2}\rangle

where
\gamma_{1}=u_{1}^{2}
and
\gamma_{2}=u_{2}^{2}
have degree 4,
\varepsilon_{1}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}u_{1}
has degree 8, and
\mu_{2}=(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})u_{2}
has degree 5.
The Betti numbers are
b_{4}=2
,
b_{4k+1}=1
for all
k\geq 1
and
b_{4k}=3
for all
k\geq 2.

Proof.

The stabiliser of the edge
e_{3}
in
\Theta
is the subgroup generated by the permutation
(12)
which exchanges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
together with the involution that flips all of the edges. Then (12) switches
e_{1}-e_{3}
with
e_{2}-e_{3}
, while the involution acts by
-1
on both. It follows that
\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle
decomposes as a sum of two 1-dimensional representations
\langle\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\rangle\oplus\langle\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\rangle=\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3}
, and
\langle\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\rangle=\kappa_{2}\cdot\kappa_{3}=\kappa_{1}.
Similarly,
(12)
acts as
-1
on both
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, while the involution that exchanges both sides also exchanges
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
. It follows that
\langle c_{1},c_{2}\rangle
decomposes as
\langle u_{1}\rangle\oplus\langle u_{2}\rangle=\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}
. As each of these are 1-dimensional, we obtain

\langle u_{1}^{n},u_{2}^{n}\rangle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2},&\text{ $n\geq 1$ odd}\\
2\cdot\mathds{1},&\text{ $n\geq 2$ even.}\end{array}\right.

Considering tensor products we have

\displaystyle(\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3})\otimes(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{1}+\mathds{1}

\displaystyle(\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3})\otimes(2\cdot\mathds{1})

\displaystyle=2\cdot\kappa_{2}+2\cdot\kappa_{3}

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\otimes(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}+\kappa_{2}

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\otimes(2\cdot\mathds{1})

\displaystyle=2\cdot\kappa_{1}.

The trivial summand in
(\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3})\otimes(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3})
is the 5-dimensional class
(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})\otimes u_{2}
, while the trivial summand in
\kappa_{1}\otimes(\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3})
is the class
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}u_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}
found previously.
The other generators are
\gamma_{1}=u_{1}^{2}
and
\gamma_{2}=u_{2}^{2}
.
Defining
\mu_{2}=(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})u_{2}
, since
u_{1}u_{2}=0
and
\mu_{2}^{2}=0
(because it has odd degree), we obtain the presentation

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{C_{2}\times C_{2}}=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{1},\varepsilon_{1}]/\langle\varepsilon_{1}^{2}\rangle\vee\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{2},\mu_{2}]/\langle\mu_{2}^{2}\rangle

where
|\gamma_{1}|=|\gamma_{2}|=4
,
|\varepsilon_{1}|=8
, and
|\mu_{2}|=5
. For the Betti numbers, we obtain additional classes of the form
\gamma_{2}^{k-1}\mu_{2}
in degree
(4k+1)
for all
k\geq 1
. The other Betti numbers agree with the
\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}
invariants.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

As a graded commutative ring, the invariants for the action of
D_{8}
on
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))
has presentation

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))^{D_{8}}=\mathbb{Q}[\alpha_{1},\eta_{1}]/\langle\eta_{1}^{2}\rangle\vee\mathbb{Q}[\alpha_{2},\nu_{2}]/\langle\nu_{2}^{2}\rangle

where
\alpha_{1}=w^{2}
and
\alpha_{2}=z_{1}^{2}=z_{2}^{2}
have degree 4,
\eta_{1}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}w
has degree 8, and
\nu_{2}=\beta_{1}z_{1}+\beta_{2}z_{2}
has degree 5. The Betti numbers are
b_{4}=2
,
b_{4k+1}=1
for all
k\geq 1
and
b_{4k}=3
for all
k\geq 2.

Proof.

With respect to the basis
\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}
, the action of
D_{8}
has the following representatives for the four nontrivial conjugacy classes:

\begin{array}[]{rcrc}(12)\colon&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)&(13)(24)\colon&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\\
&&&\\
(12)(34)\colon&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)&(1324)\colon&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\end{array}

which clearly corresponds to the standard 2-dimensional representation
\tau_{4}
. It follows that
\Lambda^{2}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle=\Lambda^{2}(\tau_{4})=\lambda_{3}
as a
D_{8}
-representation.

On
\langle w,z_{1},z_{2}\rangle
, the
D_{8}
-action preserves the subspace spanned by
w
as the 1-dimensional representation
\lambda_{3}
, while it acts on
\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle
according to the same matrices above (
z_{i}\leftrightarrow\beta_{i}
). Thus
\langle w,z_{1},z_{2}\rangle=\lambda_{3}+\tau_{4}
. It follows that
\langle w^{2}\rangle=\lambda_{3}^{2}=\mathds{1}
, while
\langle z_{1}^{2}=z_{2}^{2}\rangle=\mathds{1}
and
\langle z_{1}z_{2}\rangle=\lambda_{1}
(note the difference from
\langle\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\rangle
since
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
commute). Hence we have

\langle w^{n},z_{1}^{n},z_{1}^{n-1}z_{2}\rangle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\lambda_{3}+\tau,&\text{ $n\geq 1$ odd}\\
2\cdot\mathds{1}+\lambda_{1},&\text{ $n\geq 2$ even}\end{array}\right.

Incorporating tensor products with
\Lambda^{1}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle=\tau
and
\Lambda^{2}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle=\lambda_{3}
we have

\displaystyle\tau\otimes(\lambda_{3}+\tau)

\displaystyle=\tau+(\mathds{1}+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3})

\displaystyle\tau\otimes(2\cdot\mathds{1}+\lambda_{1})

\displaystyle=3\cdot\tau

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}\otimes(\lambda_{3}+\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}+\tau

\displaystyle\lambda\otimes(2\cdot\mathds{1}+\lambda_{1})

\displaystyle=2\cdot\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{2}

The additional trivial summand on the first line comes from
\beta_{1}z_{1}+\beta_{2}z_{2}
since
D_{8}
acts the same way on
\beta_{i}
as
z_{i}
. The trivial summand on line three is
\beta_{1}\beta_{2}w
. Therefore if we write
\alpha_{1}=w^{2}
,
\eta_{1}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}w
,
\alpha_{2}=z_{1}^{2}=z_{2}^{2}
, and
\nu_{2}=\beta_{1}z_{1}+\beta_{2}z_{2}
we obtain the presentation

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))^{D_{8}}=\mathbb{Q}[\alpha_{1},\eta_{1}]/\langle\eta_{1}^{2}\rangle\vee\mathbb{Q}[\alpha_{2},\nu_{2}]/\langle\nu_{2}^{2}\rangle

since
z_{1}w=z_{2}w=0
and the
\beta_{i}
have odd degree. Here
|\alpha_{i}|=4,

|\eta_{1}|=8
and
|\nu_{2}|=5.
Since this ring is clearly isomorphic to
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{C_{2}\times C_{2}}
, the Betti numbers are the same in each degree.
∎

7.3.
Finishing the computation

We can now complete the calculation of
H^{*}(B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2}))
, using the pushout in
7.1
. This is simplified considerably by the following lemma, which describes the effect of
\pi^{*}
on cohomology.

Lemma 7.7
.

The map
\pi\colon\Theta\rightarrow R_{2}
induces an isomorphism of rings

\pi^{*}\colon H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))^{D_{8}}\rightarrow H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{C_{2}\times C_{2}}.

Proof.

Recall that
\pi\colon\Theta\rightarrow R_{2}
is the map which collapses the edge
e_{3}
.
We have set up our notation so that
\pi^{*}
sends
\beta_{i}\in H^{3}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))
to
\beta_{i}\in H^{3}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))
, as this is exactly the map
\pi^{*}\colon H^{1}(R_{2})\rightarrow H^{1}(\Theta)
according to
6.17
.
It remains to compute the effect of
\pi^{*}
on the
c_{ij}^{k}
.
For notational clarity we will write
d_{1}=d_{12}^{3}
and
d_{2}=d_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{3^{\prime}}
for the two 2-dimensional classes of
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))
.
Thus
u_{1}=(d_{1}+d_{2})/2
and
u_{2}=(d_{1}-d_{2})/2
.

The functoriality of the classes
c_{ij}^{k}
is described in
6.16
in terms of the map
\pi^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}
on triples of half-edges.
Let
i,j,k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
be distinct, corresponding to half-edges in
R_{2}
, each of which has a unique preimage under
\pi
.
(Recall that
\pi
maps
h_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto h_{1}
,
h_{1}\mapsto h_{2}
,
h_{2}^{\prime}\mapsto h_{3}
, and
h_{2}\mapsto h_{4}
.)
Of the three preimages, two are at the same vertex; for these we take the preimage and for the remaining half-edge we take either
h_{3}
or
h_{3}^{\prime}
, depending on which vertex the other half-edges are at.
That is, the map
\pi^{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to5.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.62271pt\lower-2.66382pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 3.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -1.97 -3.41}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}
is

\displaystyle(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})

\displaystyle\mapsto(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{3}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle(h_{1},h_{2},h_{4})

\displaystyle\mapsto(h_{3},h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle(h_{3},h_{4},h_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto(h_{3},h_{2},h_{1})

\displaystyle(h_{3},h_{4},h_{1})

\displaystyle\mapsto(h_{2}^{\prime},h_{3}^{\prime},h_{1}^{\prime}),

which on cohomology classes gives

\displaystyle c_{12}^{3}

\displaystyle\mapsto d_{1^{\prime}3^{\prime}}^{2^{\prime}}=-d_{2},

\displaystyle c_{12}^{4}

\displaystyle\mapsto d_{31}^{2}=d_{1},

\displaystyle c_{34}^{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto d_{32}^{1}=-d_{1},

\displaystyle c_{34}^{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto d_{2^{\prime}3^{\prime}}^{1^{\prime}}=d_{2}.

Recalling now that
w=(c_{12}^{3}-c_{12}^{4})/2
,
z_{1}=(c_{12}^{3}+c_{12}^{4})/2
, and
z_{2}=(c_{34}^{2}+c_{34}^{1})/2
, we see that
\pi^{*}(w)=(-d_{2}-d_{1})/2=-u_{1}
,
\pi^{*}(z_{1})=(-d_{2}+d_{1})/2=u_{2}
, and
\pi^{*}(z_{2})=(-d_{1}+d_{2})/2=-u_{2}
.

It follows that
\pi^{*}\colon H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))^{D_{8}}\rightarrow H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{C_{2}\times C_{2}}
is defined on generators by

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi^{*}(w^{2})=(-u_{1})^{2}=\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle\eta_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi^{*}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2}w)=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}(-u_{1})=-\varepsilon_{1}

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi^{*}(z_{1}^{2})=\pi^{*}(z_{2}^{2})=u_{2}^{2}=\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle\nu_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi^{*}(\beta_{1}z_{1}+\beta_{2}z_{2})=\beta_{1}u_{2}+\beta_{2}(-u_{2})=(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})u_{2}=\mu_{2}

which is clearly an isomorphism of rings by Lemmas
7.5
and
7.6
.
∎

Theorem 7.8
.

The rational cohomology ring of
B\!\Diff^{+}\!\left((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2}\right)
has presentation

H^{*}\left(B\!\Diff^{+}\!\left((S^{1}\times S^{2})^{\sharp 2}\right)\right)\cong\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{1},\varepsilon]/\langle\varepsilon^{2}\rangle\vee\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{2}]

where
|\gamma_{1}|=|\gamma_{2}|=4
and
|\varepsilon|=8
.

Proof.

Applying cohomology to the homotopy pushout in
Eq.

16
, we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{C_{2}\times C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(R_{2}))^{D_{8}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}\left(B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}

The maps in this diagram can be assembled into a Mayer-Vietoris-type long exact sequence.
The left vertical map
\pi^{*}
is an isomorphism by Lemma
7.7
, hence the right vertical map is an isomorphism as well. By Lemma
7.4
, we conclude that

H^{*}\left(B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2})\right)\cong H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))^{\Sym_{3}\times C_{2}}=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{1},\varepsilon]/\langle\varepsilon^{2}\rangle\vee\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{2}]

as desired.
∎

7.4.
Circle actions

Cohomology classes on
B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2})
define characteristic classes for oriented smooth
U_{2}
bundles.
In this section we evaluate them in two examples that distinguish
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
.
Concretely, we construct two smooth circle actions
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\colon\SO(2)\to\Diff^{+}(U_{2})
on
U_{2}
, which yield
U_{2}
bundles
\pi_{i}\colon E_{i}\to B\!\SO(2)
, and we calculate the pullback of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
to the cohomology of
B\!\SO(2)
.

Definition 7.9
.

Let
M:=D^{3}\setminus(D^{3}\amalg D^{3})
be the manifold obtained by removing two small disjoint open balls on the first coordinate axis of
D^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
The group
\SO(2)
acts on
M
by rotations around the first coordinate axis.
We define

N_{1}:=M\cup_{\amalg_{3}S^{2}}M^{-}\qquad\text{and}\qquad N_{2}:=M\cup_{\amalg_{3}S^{2}}^{\varphi}M^{-}

where in the first case we double
M
by gluing along
\mathrm{id}_{\partial M}
, and in the second case we glue along the diffeomorphism
\varphi
that swaps the inner two spheres.
(Note that the right copy
M^{-}
of
M
must have the reversed orientation in both cases.)
Both
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are diffeomorphic to
U_{2}
and after fixing identifications the smooth
\SO(2)
-actions on
N_{i}
induces continuous group homomorphisms

\xi_{1}\colon\SO(2)\longrightarrow\Diff^{+}(N_{1})\cong\Diff^{+}(U_{2})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\xi_{2}\colon\SO(2)\longrightarrow\Diff^{+}(N_{2})\cong\Diff^{+}(U_{2}).

Remark 7.10
.

Note that the
\SO(2)
-actions on
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are indeed qualitatively different: on
N_{1}
the fixed submanifold consists of three circles, and on
N_{2}
it consists of one circle.

The maps on classifying spaces
B\xi_{i}\colon B\!\SO(2)\to B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2})
, classify
U_{2}
-bundles over
B\!\SO(2)
, which we can describe concretely as

E_{i}:=(S^{\infty}\times N_{i})/\SO(2)\longrightarrow S^{\infty}/\SO(2)=\mathbb{CP}^{\infty}

where
S^{\infty}
is the unit sphere in
\mathbb{C}^{\infty}
, on which
\SO(2)
acts by complex multiplication.
Note that the restriction

(E_{i})_{|\mathbb{CP}^{n}}:=(S^{2n+1}\times N_{i})/\SO(2)\longrightarrow S^{2n+1}/\SO(2)=\mathbb{CP}^{n}

is a smooth submersion from a closed
(2n+3)
-manifold to a
2n
-manifold with fibre
U_{2}
.
The proposition below evaluates the characteristic classes for
U_{2}
-bundles,
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\varepsilon
on these two bundles.

Proposition 7.11
.

Under the maps
B\xi_{i}\colon B\!\SO(2)\to B\!\Diff^{+}(U_{2})
the cohomology classes from
7.8
restrict as

\displaystyle B\xi_{1}^{*}(\gamma_{j})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&j=1,\\
e^{2}&j=2,\end{cases}

\displaystyle B\xi_{2}^{*}(\gamma_{j})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e^{2}&j=1,\\
0&j=2,\end{cases}

where
e
is the Euler class in
H^{*}(B\!\SO(2))=\mathbb{Q}[e]
.
The class
\varepsilon
restricts to
0
in both cases.

Proof.

By construction
N_{i}
has a separating system
\Sigma_{i}\subset N_{i}
consisting of the three boundary spheres of
M
.
We identify the dual graph in each case with
\Theta
in such a way that in both cases the spheres on the the left copy of
M\sqcup M^{-}\cong(N_{i}\!\mid\!\Sigma)
are are identified with the half-edges
\{1,2,3\}
in the same way.
(This means that the identification of the spheres on the right copy with
\{1^{\prime},2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\}
is the identity for
N_{1}
and a transposition for
N_{2}
.)
We let
r\colon M\cong M^{-}
denote the reflection of the third coordinate axis, this is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism as we have the opposite orientation on
M^{-}
, but it reverses the rotation.

The rotations preserve these sphere systems and their dual graphs, so they define maps
B\!\SO(2)\to B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(N_{i},\Sigma_{i})\simeq\mathcal{S}(\Theta)
.
Using the notation from
Section

7.1
, we need to determine the ring homomorphism

H^{*}(\mathcal{S}(\Theta))\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Q}[c_{1},c_{2}]/\langle c_{1}^{2}=c_{2}^{2}\rangle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}[e]=H^{*}(B\!\SO(2)).

For degree reasons, the
\beta_{i}
go to
0
.
It will suffice to determine the image of
c_{1}=c_{12}^{3}
and
c_{2}=c_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{3^{\prime}}
, from which we can read off the value of
\gamma_{1}=(\tfrac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{2})^{2}
and
\gamma_{2}=(\tfrac{c_{1}-c_{2}}{2})^{2}
.
These classes are defined by pulling back, along the restriction to either
M
or
M^{-}
, a chosen (integral) generator of
H^{2}
of the space

B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}(\partial M)}^{+}(M)\simeq B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}(\amalg_{3}S^{2})}^{+}(S^{3}\setminus\amalg_{3}\smash{\mathring{D^{3}}})\simeq\Conf_{3}(S^{3})\sslash\SO(4).

Consider the four maps

\displaystyle f_{i}^{\rm L}

\displaystyle\colon B\!\SO(2)\xrightarrow{B\xi_{i}}B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(N_{i},\Sigma_{i})\xrightarrow{\text{left}}B\!\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(\partial M)}(M)

\displaystyle f_{i}^{\rm R}

\displaystyle\colon B\!\SO(2)\xrightarrow{B\xi_{i}}B\!\Diff_{\pi_{0}(2\Sigma)}^{+}(N_{i},\Sigma_{i})\xrightarrow{\text{right}}B\!\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(\partial M^{-})}(M^{-})\simeq B\!\Diff^{+}_{\pi_{0}(\partial M)}(M)

where the second map restricts to the left or right factor.
(In the case of
f_{i}^{\rm R}
the last equivalence uses the reflection
r\colon M\cong M^{-}
.)
Each of these composites is a homotopy equivalence by
6.6
, and hence they pull back
c_{1,2}^{3}
(or
c_{1^{\prime},2^{\prime}}^{3^{\prime}}
) to
\pm e
.
We will need to work out how these signs differ.
The maps
f_{1}^{\rm L}
and
f_{2}^{\rm L}
are identical, because we identified the left copy of
M\sqcup M^{-}=(N_{i}\!\mid\!\Sigma_{i})
in the same way,
but the maps
f_{1}^{\rm R}
and
f_{2}^{\rm R}
differ by a the involution on
\Conf_{3}(S^{3})\sslash\SO(4)
that swaps two of the points.
(This introduces a sign as
c_{2^{\prime}1^{\prime}}^{3^{\prime}}=-c_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{3^{\prime}}
.)
Moreover, the maps
f_{1}^{\rm L}
and
f_{1}^{\rm R}
differ the reflection
r
, which reverses the
\SO(2)
-action and hence acts by
-1
on
H^{2}
.
In summary, we have

\displaystyle(f_{2}^{\rm L})^{*}c_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{3^{\prime}}=(f_{1}^{\rm L})^{*}c_{12}^{3}

\displaystyle=-(f_{1}^{\rm R})^{*}c_{12}^{3}=(f_{2}^{\rm R})^{*}c_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{3^{\prime}}

and all of these classes are
\pm e
.
It follows that

\displaystyle B\xi_{1}^{*}(\gamma_{2})

\displaystyle=B\xi_{1}^{*}(\tfrac{c_{1}-c_{2}}{2})^{2}=(\pm e)^{2}=e^{2}

\displaystyle B\xi_{2}^{*}(\gamma_{1})

\displaystyle=B\xi_{2}^{*}(\tfrac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{2})^{2}=(\pm e)^{2}=e^{2}

and
B\xi_{1}^{*}(\gamma_{1})=0=B\xi_{2}^{*}(\gamma_{2})
.
Finally,
B\xi_{i}^{*}(\varepsilon)
must be
0
as its square is
0
.
∎
